Rather than report nine failures on a machine built in a non-elevated shell, the fixture detects `REGDB_E_IIDNOTREG` and stands down with that instruction: a runtime check and not `[Explicit]`, because an `[Explicit]` fixture stays skipped on the machines where it WOULD pass, and a security feature whose tests never run anywhere is indistinguishable from one that does not work. |
| ✅ | Per-account 2FA | **Schema 6017.** A TOTP second factor on a mailbox account, and the end of the chain app passwords were built for. TOTP has been in this product for years and protected exactly one thing: the admin tool's own logon, checked in C# by the Control Panel *after* the server had already accepted the password. The server had no idea the feature existed - which is why no mailbox account could have one. The obstacle was never the algorithm. IMAP, POP3 and SMTP have nowhere to type a code, so an account required to present one could not be opened by any client at all. The rule that makes it work is therefore blunt: **once a secret is enrolled the account password stops being a mailbox credential**, and an app password becomes the only one. Enforced in `PasswordValidator::ValidatePassword`, which every protocol goes through, with a `secondFactorSatisfied` flag that defaults to false - so a new call site is safe by omission rather than dangerous by it. `Application.AuthenticateWithCode` is the one path that can satisfy it, because it is the one place a code can be presented; `Authenticate` refuses an enrolled account, as does every mail protocol. Both factors are genuinely required: a valid code with the wrong password is refused, and so is the right password with a wrong code. The implementation is RFC 6238 with the parameters every authenticator app actually implements (HMAC-SHA1, 30-second step, 6 digits, one step of tolerance either side - RFC 6238 permits SHA-256, and using it would produce a QR code that scans and then never matches). **The RFC's published vectors are pinned in the server's self-tests**, including the 20000000000 row, which also proves the counter stays 64-bit past 2038 - an implementation that agrees only with itself will agree with itself about the wrong answer, and the symptom looks like a clock problem for a long time. The regression fixture then computes codes with .NET's own HMACSHA1 rather than anything the server owns, which is what "works with a real authenticator app" actually means. The secret leaves the server exactly once, inside the `otpauth://` URI that becomes the QR code; nothing reads it back, so possessing the account does not yield the factor. Removing the second factor restores the account password and **deliberately leaves app passwords alone** - they were separately revocable credentials before it was enrolled and remain so after, and deleting them would break every client the account holder had set up as a side effect of an unrelated action. Six tests, plus a Control Panel tab placed immediately after App passwords, because that order is the advice. |
| ⬜ | bcrypt / scrypt | Not implemented; the strong-KDF menu is PBKDF2 and Argon2id only. |
| ✅ | Client-side OAuth2 for outbound relay | Shipped 16 August 2026; this row simply had not been updated - the dated forcing-function row was, and carries the full description. When a route's destination host is on `OutboundOAuth2Hosts` (default `smtp.office365.com`) the AUTH step presents a bearer token in Microsoft's exact XOAUTH2 shape, one round trip, from `OutboundOAuth2TokenClient`: a client_credentials POST to `OutboundOAuth2TokenUrl` built like the MTA-STS fetcher (certificate verification, bounded timeouts), cached to 80% of the token's lifetime and invalidated the moment a relay refuses one. `OutboundOAuth2FixedToken` passes a token obtained outside this server verbatim. Host-gated deliberately, with a negative-control test: a client that presented bearers to every relay would leak tokens to all of them. There is no silent downgrade to a password - a provider configured for OAuth has Basic auth off, so a token fetch failure is reported (HM5904) and the refusal that follows is visible rather than a stall. [See the dated item](#dated-items--the-forcing-functions). |
| ✅ | Client-side OAuth2 for the external account fetcher | Shipped 16 August 2026 alongside the outbound half, and likewise left un-ticked here. An external account whose server is on `FetchOAuth2Hosts` (default `outlook.office365.com`) authenticates with `AUTH XOAUTH2` carrying a bearer from the same token client, so Microsoft 365 mailboxes can be collected again - collecting from them with `USER`/`PASS` has been impossible since 2022. Asserted on the bytes the POP3 server actually receives, with the unlisted-host control that `USER`/`PASS` is untouched everywhere else, and on the messages arriving afterwards, since a login that succeeds and fetches nothing is not a login worth having (`FetchXOAuth2.cs`). |
| ⬜ | IMAP fetching of external accounts | Only POP3 fetching exists, so a mailbox that must stay intact on the far side cannot be collected: POP3 either leaves every message to be re-fetched or deletes it. This is the reason the OAuth2 rows above stop at POP3 - there is no IMAP fetcher to give a bearer token to. A real one needs a client-side IMAP session, per-folder UID state to know what has already been collected, and a decision about whether it mirrors folders or flattens them into the inbox. |
| ⬜ | CRAM-MD5 (RFC 2195) | Not implemented anywhere — not advertised, not accepted. Would in any case be impossible against the PBKDF2/Argon2id stores since it needs a reversible or MD5-equivalent secret. |
| ⬜ | DIGEST-MD5 (RFC 2831) | Not implemented. Obsoleted by RFC 6331; SCRAM-SHA-256 is the replacement that is shipped. |
| ✅ | ES256 tokens | Implemented 19 August 2026. The obstacle was a format mismatch, not a missing algorithm: JWS carries an ECDSA signature as the raw fixed-width **R||S** pair (RFC 7515 section 3.4) and OpenSSL verifies **X9.62 DER**, so ES256 could only ever have reported "signature verification failed" - which describes nothing and sends whoever hits it looking at their key, their clock and their provider. It was therefore refused by name rather than left to fail, and the transcode is what has now been written. The DER rules are where this goes wrong quietly: an INTEGER is signed and minimally encoded, so leading zeros are dropped and a leading octet with the high bit set needs a `0x00` in front. Getting either wrong still produces a structurally valid signature that OpenSSL rejects for roughly **half** of all real inputs, which presents as an intermittent fault rather than a broken encoder. So the transcode is pinned in the server's self-tests **against OpenSSL's own `i2d_ECDSA_SIG`**, case by case - high bit set, high bit clear, leading zeros to strip, and the boundary - because a transcode checked by round-tripping through its own decoder proves only that it agrees with itself. Three regression tests then take the other half: a token minted the way a real provider mints one (.NET's `ECDsa.SignData` emits exactly the raw R||S form, asserted at 64 octets) travelling the real SASL path. Two things came with it. The public key is now checked to actually **be** the kind the algorithm names - an EC key configured against RS256, or an RSA key against ES256, is refused on the key type rather than surfacing as a signature failure, which is the difference between reporting an algorithm-confusion attempt and reporting its symptom. And implementing an algorithm deliberately does **not** enable it: a valid ES256 token is still refused while the allow-list omits it, which has its own test. The Control Panel's readiness check, which used to report ES256 as unusable, was updated with it. |
| ⬜ | EXTERNAL (client-certificate SASL) | Not implemented as a SASL mechanism — but no longer blocked, and the reason this row used to give is out of date: per-port inbound client certificates shipped, so the contexts do request one and the verified subject is already held on the connection. What is missing is the mechanism itself: advertising `AUTH=EXTERNAL` on a port whose policy requests or requires a certificate, and mapping that verified subject to an account. |
| ⬜ | GSSAPI / Kerberos | Not implemented as a SASL mechanism. Active Directory accounts authenticate by replaying the plaintext password to `LogonUser`, which is password-based, not Kerberos SSO. |
| ⬜ | JWKS fetch with key rotation | Not implemented. The validator never contacts the identity provider; a static PEM or shared secret is the only key source, so provider key rotation requires manual re-configuration. |
| ✅ | LDAP / directory account backend | **Corrected 14 August 2026: the "no bind-based authentication against a directory" in the original wording below is simply false, and had been for some time.** `Server/Common/LDAP/` holds `LdapClient`, `LdapSettings` and `LdapDirectoryAuthenticator`; `PasswordValidator` calls it for any AD-linked account when `[LDAP] Enabled` is set, and there is a Control Panel page for the whole section and a regression fixture that stands up an in-process LDAP server to test it. It is also carefully built rather than merely present: a directory *rejection* deliberately does not fall through to `LogonUser`, because doing so would double every failed attempt against the directory and trip a lockout policy on a single wrong password. What remains genuinely missing is the other two thirds — **an account source and directory sync** — which is what row *LDAP / Active Directory as a directory backend* states correctly. **Scoped on 14 August 2026 against the live lab DC, which is now fully usable** (the credential rejection recorded on 13 August is gone: a Negotiate bind succeeds and a subtree search returns the five test users in `OU=hMailServer Test`, and `_ldap._tcp`/`_kerberos._tcp`/`_gc._tcp` all resolve from the host). Three findings shape the work and are recorded so it does not have to be rediscovered. **`LdapClient` cannot enumerate.** `FindUserDn` is built for authenticating one user — it asks the directory for *two* entries specifically so it can detect ambiguity, and treats more than one match as a configuration fault that must authenticate nobody. Enumeration wants the opposite, so it needs its own method rather than a relaxed `FindUserDn`. **That method must page.** Active Directory caps a search at `MaxPageSize`, 1000 by default, and answers a larger directory by returning the first thousand entries *with a success status*; an account source that silently sees part of a directory is worse than one that fails, because the users it cannot see look **deleted** rather than unread — and a lab of eight users would never reveal it. **The service credential binds with `BindSimple` only.** That needs a protected transport, so on a DC with no working LDAPS — exactly this lab, and exactly the unjoined-DMZ deployment the feature exists for — the search bind cannot be made safely, even though `BindMethod=Negotiate` already exists for the *user* bind and would work. Sync needs the service bind to honour that setting too. Nothing is implemented yet: an enumeration was written and compiled, then reverted rather than left in the tree unverified. **The original wording follows, because the argument in it is still the right one and only its first sentence was wrong:** **The measurement recorded against the SSPI row above is the argument for this item, and it is stronger than "a directory backend would be nice".** The existing AD support goes through `LogonUser`, which requires the mail server itself to be domain-joined: on a workgroup host it cannot succeed and cannot even report why. An LDAP simple bind over LDAPS has no such requirement - it is a network call to a directory, so it works from a DMZ host that is deliberately not joined, which is exactly where a mail server usually sits, and it works against directories that are not Windows at all. Verified against a real Windows Server 2025 controller on 13 August 2026: anonymous RootDSE reads answer, the SASL mechanisms offered are GSSAPI, GSS-SPNEGO, EXTERNAL and DIGEST-MD5, and simple bind is accepted on 389 and 3268 - so the protocol side of this is a straight implementation, not a research problem. |
| ⬜ | NTLM | Not implemented on any protocol. |
| ✅ | Password complexity policy | The server had opinions about passwords and enforced none of them: `Utilities.IsStrongPassword` exists, carries a hard-coded seven-entry deny-list and a longer-than-four-characters rule, is reachable over COM - and **nothing in the server has ever called it**, so every mailbox could be `test`. Five settings now do (`PasswordPolicyMinimumLength`, `RequireMixedCase`, `RequireDigit`, `RequireNonAlphanumeric`, `RejectCommon`), plus a rule that is always on once any of them is: a password may not contain the account name, which no length requirement catches and which is the most guessable choice an account holder can make. **Enforced where a password is CHOSEN and nowhere else** - `InterfaceAccount::put_Password`, which the Control Panel, DBSetup, scripts and the API all go through - and deliberately not on any path that verifies, re-hashes or reads back an existing one. A policy applied to verification would lock people out of mailboxes they can open today in the name of making them safer, which is why the fixture's central test tightens the policy far past a stored password and then asserts that POP3 and IMAP still admit it. Off by default in every part, so an upgrade never starts refusing passwords on its own. The evaluator is pure - configuration in, verdict out, no ini access - in the shape `OAuth2TokenValidator::ValidateWithConfig` already uses, and the refusal names the requirement that was not met, because an administrator told only "not acceptable" guesses again at random. App passwords are held to the same policy on top of their own twelve-character floor: a credential that opens the same mailbox must not be the documented way round the rule. Seven tests. |
| ✅ | Password expiry and history | **Schema 6019.** Shipped as a pair, because expiry without history teaches people to alternate between two passwords - which is worse than not expiring at all, since it turns one secret into two known ones. **History** (`PasswordPolicyHistoryCount`) refuses a new password that matches the last N *or the current one* - the repeat somebody is most likely to try. Compared with the verifier rather than by comparing hashes, because every scheme here is salted and a string comparison would report "not a repeat" every time. The old hash is recorded at the moment the new one replaces it, and the table is trimmed there too: it only grows when a password changes, so that is the cheapest place to bound it. History is deleted with its account, because account ids are reissued and a new account inheriting a stranger's history would be refused a password for a reason nobody could explain. **Expiry** (`PasswordPolicyMaximumAgeDays`) is the half with a caveat, and it is stated in the setting's own description rather than a release note: **this server has no self-service password change** - no mechanism in IMAP, POP3 or SMTP, and no web surface - so an expired password can only be reset by an administrator. Three deliberate escape hatches keep that from being an outage: existing **app passwords keep working**, so an affected person can still collect mail; **Active Directory accounts are exempt**, because their password lives in the directory and expires by its policy rather than this one; and an **unreadable or unstamped date is never treated as expired**, which is the failure that would otherwise lock out every account on the server that upgraded. The 6019 step stamps every existing account with the upgrade time, so switching expiry on starts everybody's clock then rather than expiring the entire server at once. The check runs **after** the password has been found correct, so a wrong guess against an expired account is indistinguishable from a wrong guess against any other, and the client is told nothing a mail client could act on anyway. The distinction is made in the **log**, where an administrator can act on it. Fourteen tests across the two policy fixtures. |
| ✅ | Per-account lockout | Shipped 17 August 2026, and hardened the same day by TWO adversarial review passes - five independent lenses over the first cut, then four more over the fixes, every finding put to skeptics before being accepted. The first pass found six real defects; the second found that one of the FIXES had quietly reintroduced the worst of them by another route. Both are described below, because a security control's worth is in the attacks it actually stops. `AccountLockout` counts failed authentications by the NAME being guessed at - the one thing a distributed attack cannot vary - and locks it for `AccountLockoutMinutes` once `AccountLockoutThreshold` failures land inside `AccountLockoutWindowMinutes` (ini `[Settings]`; threshold 0 = off, the shipped default). The name rather than the account, deliberately: counting only real accounts would make the lockout an existence oracle. But the CANONICAL name - aliases resolved and the default domain applied, as the authentication paths do before they match an account - because keying on the string as it arrived gave one mailbox a separate bucket per spelling and an attacker a fresh threshold for each, plus a trivial evasion by alternating them. **Enforced on every path a password can be guessed through**, which took two mechanisms, not one: `AccountLogon` covers POP3 `PASS`, IMAP `LOGIN`, SMTP `AUTH PLAIN`/`LOGIN` and ManageSieve, and each protocol's SCRAM account lookup covers SCRAM-SHA-256(-PLUS), where a locked name is handed the forced-failure exchange that already exists for accounts SCRAM cannot serve - so the lock is enforced with no second refusal shape to distinguish. Without that half an attacker simply chose SCRAM, guessed at full rate, and their attempts still locked the victim out of every password client. Every mechanism that verifies credentials itself also clears the counters on success, or a user who mistyped twice and then authenticated by SCRAM kept those failures for ever. **Deliberately excluded, each for a stated reason**: OAuth2 bearer failures (a token is not guessable, and a client looping on an expired one would lock its own user out of password auth), the REST API's administrator credential (not a mailbox name, so a per-name lock could only lock a string nobody uses), the COM administration path (guessing at an administrator's mailbox from the internet must not cost them the tool they need to respond), and SCRAM forced-failure exchanges against accounts SCRAM can never serve - an Argon2id account would otherwise be locked out of its working mechanisms by a client insisting on the one that cannot work, with no success able to clear it. A locked refusal is byte-identical to an ordinary wrong-password refusal, and the fixture asserts that by comparison with a real unlocked refusal rather than by a substring every refusal contains. What the reply hides, the clock still shows: skipping the check skips a PBKDF2/Argon2id verification, so lock state is measurable by timing. That is accepted and documented rather than papered over - paying the KDF to hide it would hand an attacker a memory-hard amplifier on demand, and sleeping to equalise would park a connection thread, which is why login tarpitting is deferred. The account-existence oracle that predates this feature is neither widened nor narrowed. A locked refusal is also kept out of the **per-IP** auto-ban, which was the review's most serious find: the person most likely to keep trying a locked name is its owner, with the correct password, and charging those attempts to their address got an innocent office NAT - or the loopback address of a co-hosted webmail front end - banned from every protocol for everyone behind it. An attack on one mailbox must not become an outage for unrelated users. The second pass earned its keep here: expressing "locked" as "cannot serve SCRAM" had routed the locked refusal into the SCRAM failure helper, which still charged the address - so the fix held on the password path and leaked straight back in on the SCRAM one. Each SCRAM refusal helper now consults the lock too, and the exemption is deliberately NOT extended to the other forced-failure causes sharing the same empty account handle (an unknown name, an Argon2id or AD account, a hash policy excluding PBKDF2), since exempting those would let an attacker spray user names over SCRAM without accumulating one per-IP failure. A fixture drives twelve correct-password retries down each route with auto-ban armed and asserts no ban range appears - the assertion the first pass correctly noted did not exist. A lock cannot be maintained indefinitely either: attempts made while locked are not counted, and the window restarts when the lock expires, so a re-lock costs a fresh threshold rather than the single guess it used to. Nothing runs under the lock that should not - the log line and the table-full report are emitted after releasing it, since one takes the process-wide logger mutex and the other fires the `OnError` script event - and every entry point carries an exception barrier that fails open, because failing to lock one name is smaller than dropping a session, writing a minidump and skipping the per-IP accounting that follows. The table is capped, its retirement sweep is rate-limited so a full table cannot turn each further failure into a walk of it with all authentication queued behind, and a full table reports HM6190 rather than silently ceasing to protect. Expiry arithmetic (window ageing, lock duration, clock steps both ways, case folding, no-count-while-locked, expiry-clears-window) is pinned by injected-clock self-tests; seven fixtures cover the wiring, the SCRAM enforcement and clearing, the alias-spelling evasion, the byte-identical refusal, the untouched bystander account and the inert default. |
| ⬜ | Login tarpitting | Not implemented, and the blocker is now written down: a progressive delay slept on a connection worker thread is a self-DoS an attacker triggers at will with cheap failed logons. A real tarpit needs the async-timer plumbing (`TCPConnection::timer_` exists) threaded through each protocol's refusal path so a delayed reply parks no thread - a focused change, not a one-liner. Per-account lockout (above) now covers the throttling half of what tarpitting is usually deployed for. |
| ⬜ | SCRAM-SHA-1 / SCRAM-SHA-512 | Neither variant exists; SHA-256 is the only SCRAM family member. Some older clients that only speak SCRAM-SHA-1 will therefore fall back to PLAIN/LOGIN. |
| ⬜ | Second factor on the administrator credential | Half of this is now enforced and the more important half is not. A **mailbox** account's second factor is enforced server-side — an enrolled account is refused on every mail protocol, and `Application.AuthenticateWithCode` is the one path that can satisfy it. The **administrator's** TOTP is still checked by the Control Panel client *after* the server has accepted the password, and the REST API's HTTP Basic check knows nothing about it, so a stolen administrator password remains a complete credential on both surfaces. That is the wrong way round: the administrator credential is the one worth stealing. |
| ⬜ | Token introspection (RFC 7662) | Not implemented — no revocation awareness at all; a stolen token is valid until `exp`. |

### Anti-spam, anti-virus and content control

60 shipped · 0 underway · 6 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Action on detection and notifications | Two actions only - delete the message, or strip all attachments and continue delivery (VIRUS_ATTACHMENT_REMOVED server message); optional notification to sender and/or to every recipient. No quarantine-and-release option |
| ✅ | Anti-spam scope: inbound, unauthenticated only | All spam protection is skipped for authenticated sessions, for IP ranges with spam protection disabled, and for white-listed senders - so there is no outbound/authenticated-submission spam filtering at all |
| ✅ | Attachment blocking by file-name wildcard | Independent of virus scanning and of the scan flag: every delivered message is checked when enabled, matching attachment file names against wildcard patterns… |
| ✅ | Attachment blocking by name/extension | A configurable blocked-attachment list is applied after scanning; matching parts are stripped from the message rather than the message rejected. Independent of the virus scanner and available with scanning off. |
| ✅ | Blocked-attachment list management | DB-backed list of wildcard + description rows (hm_blocked_attachments), editable through the COM API and the Control Panel "Blocked attachments" collection page. Matching is file-name only - no MIME type… |
| ✅ | Bounded SpamAssassin session | Idle timeout scaled between SAMinTimeout (30s) and SAMaxTimeout (90s) plus an absolute session ceiling of SAMaxTimeout+30s, so a trickling spamd cannot pin the thread that acknowledges the message (discussion #18) |
| ✅ | Built-in scanner test harness (EICAR + negative control) | TestClamAVConnect / TestCustomVirusScanner / TestClamWinVirusScanner each scan a plain file first (must not alarm) then an EICAR file (must alarm); exposed on the COM AntiVirus interface and wired to the Control Panel buttons |
| ✅ | Built-in weighted test pipeline | Nine tests, each independently enabled and independently scored: DNSBL, HELO-host match, PTR, sender MX records, SPF, SURBL, DKIM, DMARC and SpamAssassin… |
| ✅ | ClamAV connection timeout, load-scaled | Timeout computed by TimeoutCalculator between ClamMinTimeout (15s) and ClamMaxTimeout (90s), both settable in hMailServer.ini and surfaced on the Control Panel ClamAV tab |
| ✅ | ClamAV integration (clamd TCP + clamscan) | Native clamd client (host/port, INI ClamMinTimeout/ClamMaxTimeout bounds) plus a clamscan/ClamWin command-line path; the whole spool file is scanned first, then every MIME attachment is written to a temp file and scanned individually… |
| ✅ | ClamAV via clamd INSTREAM | TCP host+port only. Sends "nINSTREAM\n", then 4-byte big-endian length-prefixed 4096-byte chunks, then a zero-length terminator, and matches the reply against ^stream.*: (.*) FOUND$ |
| ✅ | ClamWin (local clamscan.exe) scanner | Launches clamscan with --database and --tempdir; exit code 1 means infected, and the virus name is reported as "Unknown" because the CLI output is not parsed. Executable path is force-quoted against unquoted-path hijack |
| ✅ | Concurrency cap of 10 parallel scans | Interlocked counter caps simultaneous scanners at MaxRunningScanners=10. After waiting 60s (or 10 failed CAS attempts) a scan proceeds without reserving a slot, deliberately, because mail should still flow past a wedged scanner. The slot is now an RAII guard that records whether it actually got one, so the decrement cannot run unless the increment did — previously every give-up drove the counter one lower until it went negative, at which point the cap stopped limiting anything at all. [See defects](#defects-found-by-the-audit) |
| ✅ | Custom command-line virus scanner | Any external scanner can be wired in by executable path plus a 'virus found' return code, launched through ProcessLauncher and bounded by [Settings] ExternalProcessTimeout. |
| ✅ | Custom external command-line scanner | Runs any executable; %FILE% is substituted anywhere in the command line, otherwise the quoted file path is appended. A configurable exit code means "infected"; virus name is again "Unknown" |
| ✅ | Custom-scanner presets in the Control Panel | One-click command lines and exit codes for Microsoft Defender (MpCmdRun), Sophos savscan, ESET ecls, Bitdefender bdscan and Kaspersky avp.com, plus Test buttons for all three scanner types and ClamWin auto-detect |
| ✅ | DKIM and DMARC as scored tests | DKIM permfail and DMARC reject/quarantine each contribute their configured failure score; a DMARC p=quarantine verdict is scored exactly like p=reject — the quarantine store exists now, but nothing routes a DMARC verdict into it, so the two policies remain indistinguishable in effect and wiring them apart is a real item rather than a wording one — and p=none is logged only |
| ✅ | Custom DNS server for all lookups | `[Settings] DNSServer` sends every DNSBL, SURBL, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, MX, PTR and host lookup to one nominated IPv4 resolver instead of the system's, which is what makes a DMZ mail server usable when the host's own resolver cannot see the internal zones. Bounded since 6.2.17 by `DNSQueryTimeout` (default 10s), so an unresponsive resolver can no longer hold a delivery thread. **Documented here for the first time on 13 August 2026, after it spent a morning broken by a one-line "improvement" of mine and being fixed again.** 6.2.17 moved from the classic `DnsQuery` - which took a bare `PIP4_ARRAY` of addresses with no port field - to `DnsQueryEx`, whose `DNS_ADDR_ARRAY` carries a full `SOCKADDR` per server. A `SOCKADDR` with a zero port reads like an oversight, so it was "corrected" to 53; the DNS client supplies the port itself and **rejects** an entry that specifies one, returning `ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER` for every query before a packet leaves the machine. Every lookup then fails, and because DNSBL, SURBL, SPF and DMARC all fail open and silently, the only visible symptom is SpamAssassin - one of the few callers that reports a failed lookup - while the spam filtering quietly stops. Measured both ways against a real DNS server before and after. The port is now zero with a comment at the line saying not to change it, and `Infrastructure/CustomDnsServer.cs` covers it, marked explicit because the setting only takes effect on a service restart and that invalidates the COM object the fixture base caches. |
| ✅ | DNS results are traceable | A `DNS - Result.` debug line for every query records the name, record type, the raw status, how many records came back, whether the nominated server was used, and how the status was classified. Added because the absence of it is what hid the defect above through three wrong theories: a status the resolver treats as benign - "no such name", "no records" - returned success with an empty list and logged nothing at all, so a lookup that quietly found nothing was indistinguishable from one that was never made. Debug level, so it costs nothing until somebody is diagnosing resolution, which is a recurring support question. |
| ✅ | DNSBL / RBL checking | Multiple configurable lists, each with its own DNS host, expected-result expression, score and rejection text. Expected results support pipe-separated alternatives, last-octet ranges (127.0.0.1-5) and wildcards… |
| ✅ | DNSBL check timing | [Settings] DNSBLChecksAfterMailFrom (default 1) moves the pre-transmission checks from connect time to after MAIL FROM; hosts listed as incoming relays are switched to post-transmission scoring instead of pre-transmission rejection. |
| ✅ | DNSBL check timing is configurable | DNSBLChecksAfterMailFrom (default 1) moves blacklist lookups from connect time to after MAIL FROM, so a blacklisted client is not paying DNS cost before it has identified itself |
| ✅ | DNSBL lists with per-list score and reject text | Each configured blacklist has host, expected result, score and reject message, and is checked against the originating IP; list managed via COM/Control Panel collection editor |
| ✅ | External scanner processes are bounded and killed | Scanner launches set a 20s "slow" warning and are then hard-bounded by ExternalProcessTimeout (default 300s), after which the child is TerminateProcess'd with a 5s grace so the delivery thread is never lost… |
| ✅ | Fail-open on scanner failure | A scanner error (cannot connect, cannot launch, unparseable reply) is reported as HM5406 and then treated as NoVirusFound, so the message is delivered unscanned… |
| ✅ | Fixed HTTP endpoint surface (no user-defined routes) | The built-in web-services listener serves only a fixed set of paths — ACME challenges, /.well-known/mta-sts.txt, /.well-known/security.txt, Thunderbird autoconfig, Outlook autodiscover and the caldav/carddav discovery redirects; there is no way to register a script- or plugin-backed route |
| ✅ | Greylisting | Classic sender/recipient/IP triplet store with configurable initial delay, initial-delete and final-delete windows, per-domain opt-out, IP whitelist, bypass on SPF pass… |
| ✅ | Greylisting with bypasses and per-domain opt-out | Triplet-based greylisting with initial delay and two expiry windows, a dedicated IP white list, bypass on SPF pass, bypass when the connecting IP is the sender domain's A or MX record, per-recipient-domain enable flag… |
| ✅ | HELO host check | Resolves the HELO name and requires the connecting IP to appear among its A/AAAA records; accepts a bracketed literal [addr] or [IPv6:addr] that matches the peer; skips loopback; treats DNS failure as not-spam. Scored… |
| ✅ | Mark / delete thresholds and message tagging | Score >= delete threshold rejects with 550/554 and the failing test's message; score >= mark threshold sets the spam flag and adds X-hMailServer-Spam, X-hMailServer-Reason-N per failed test, X-hMailServer-Reason-Score… |
| ✅ | Maximum message size to scan | Messages larger than AntiVirus.MaximumMessageSize (KB) are skipped entirely and delivered unscanned; 0 means unlimited |
| ✅ | Multiple engines chained per scan | ClamWin, then custom scanner, then ClamAV are each run if enabled, in that fixed order; the first VirusFound short-circuits the rest. Order is not configurable |
| ✅ | Outbound webhooks | No configurable webhook feature; webhook delivery exists only as an admin-written event-script handler using MSXML2.ServerXMLHTTP, shipped as a Control Panel template. Synchronous by default… |
| ✅ | Per-destination outbound rate shaping | [Settings] MaxOutboundPerDestinationPerMinute caps messages sent to one recipient domain per minute; over the cap the delivery is deferred non-fatally and retried. 0 = unlimited (default). |
| ✅ | Per-fetch-account scanning of externally downloaded mail | The POP3 external fetcher sets the scan flag from the fetch account's UseAntiVirus property, so downloaded mail can be scanned independently of any IP range |
| ✅ | Per-IP submission rate limiting | [Settings] MaxSubmissionsPerIPPerMinute caps transactions started per source IP per minute, answering 421 when exceeded. 0 = unlimited (default). |
| ✅ | Per-test timing instrumentation | Each spam test is timed and logged by name with its score contribution; anything taking 10s or more is escalated from debug to the application log, so a sick DNS resolver or stalled spamd is identified by name (discussion #18) |
| ✅ | Pre- vs post-transmission test split | DNSBL, HELO, PTR, MX and SPF run before the body is transferred (rejectable at RCPT/MAIL with 550); SURBL, DKIM, DMARC and SpamAssassin run after the body arrives and reject with 554 |
| ✅ | PTR / reverse-DNS check | Requires a PTR record for the peer whose forward A/AAAA resolution includes the peer IP (full FCrDNS round trip); DNS failure is treated as not-spam. Separately… |
| ✅ | Return-Path injected for SA then removed | A Return-Path built from the envelope sender is written as the topmost header before handing the file to spamd (so SA's SPF and stock rules work) and deleted afterwards; the rewrite is skipped if the message cannot be reloaded… |
| ✅ | Safe degradation when spamd misbehaves | Truncated, short-read, EOF-early or zero-length responses abort the rewrite and keep the original message; the test-incomplete case is reported as HM5508 ("message was accepted without a SpamAssassin verdict"), i.e. fail-open by design |
| ✅ | Scan applies to both directions, gated per IP range | Virus scanning is not inbound-only: the per-message scan flag is set when the connecting IP range has Virus protection enabled, and the scan itself runs at delivery time… |
| ✅ | Scan concurrency cap and size ceiling | Messages over VirusScanMaxSize KB are skipped entirely, and a global running-scanner counter makes new scans wait (10 retries) before giving up, so a slow scanner cannot fan out across the thread pool. |
| ✅ | Scan scope: whole message plus each attachment | The complete spool file is scanned first, then every MIME attachment is extracted to a GUID-named temp file and scanned individually; first detection wins and temp files are deleted either way… |
| ✅ | Score merging or fixed score | If X-Spam-Status starts with YES the message scores either SpamAssassin's own score merged into the hMailServer total, or a flat configured score. Caveat: the merge parser takes only the integer part before the first '.' of score= |
| ✅ | Scored test pipeline with early exit and per-test timing | Nine tests run in a fixed order (DNSBL, HELO, PTR, MX, SPF, SURBL, DKIM, DMARC, SpamAssassin), split pre/post transmission, aborting as soon as the mark/delete threshold is reached… |
| ✅ | Sender-domain MX check | Scores mail whose envelope-from domain publishes no MX records; a failed DNS query is treated as having records. |
| ✅ | Sender/IP white list with wildcard matching | Cached list of (IP range, sender-address wildcard) entries; a match bypasses all spam protection including greylisting. Cache is refreshed lazily under a shared mutex |
| ✅ | Size ceiling on post-transmission scanning | Post-transmission tests are skipped above AntiSpam.MaximumMessageSize, hard-capped at the MIME parser's 80MB limit because scanning above it could not load the message and the post-scan rewrite would destroy it |
| ✅ | SpamAssassin integration | Native spamd client speaking PROCESS SPAMC/1.2, with configurable host/port, score, merge-score mode and its own min/max timeouts. Runs as the last (post-transmission) test. |
| ✅ | spamd integration over SPAMC/1.2 PROCESS | Connects to the configured host/port and sends "PROCESS SPAMC/1.2" with a Content-length header, streams the whole message in 20KB chunks, then replaces the spool file with spamd's rewritten message (move or copy, per SAMoveVsCopy) |
| ✅ | SURBL (URI blacklists) | Extracts URLs from plain and HTML bodies with a regex, strips soft-line-break artefacts, de-duplicates, trims to the registrable domain via the TLD table, skips known boilerplate hosts (w3.org, schemas.microsoft.com… |
| ✅ | SURBL URL-reputation checks | Extracts URLs from the plain and HTML bodies by regex, caps at 15 URLs per message, skips well-known boilerplate hosts (w3.org, schemas.microsoft.com, fonts.googleapis/gstatic)… |
| ✅ | Version-tolerant SPAMD response parsing | Accepts any SPAMD/<version> banner as long as the status is EX_OK (upstream required 1.1 exactly), then requires a well-formed non-negative Content-length; malformed… |
| ✅ | Admin-reviewable quarantine (hold and release) | **Schema 6018.** Messages the server would have refused are held instead, in a store an administrator can list, read, release or delete. The thing that makes this a different decision rather than a tidier one is the SMTP reply. Refusing happens DURING the conversation with a 550: the message is never accepted, so there is nothing to review and nothing to release. Quarantining answers **250**, and the sender believes it was delivered - which is exactly what a false positive needs (no bounce to a return path that is probably forged) and exactly what a true positive does not deserve. That trade is the feature, and `TheSenderIsToldItWasAccepted` is the test that pins it. **Post-transmission verdicts only**, and that is arithmetic rather than a limitation to be fixed later: a pre-transmission verdict - a blacklisted connecting IP, a bad HELO - is reached before DATA, when there is no message to hold, and refusing there is also strictly cheaper since the body never crosses the wire. A quarantine that fails to store falls through to refusing. Accepting mail that was not actually stored would turn a spam refusal into silent deletion, which is the one outcome nobody wants and the one that leaves no trace. Releasing re-enters at the **delivery queue**, not at SMTP: rules, forwarding and quota still apply, and the spam filtering does not - because it cannot, that verdict is reached during the SMTP conversation a released message never has. Which is fortunate, since re-running it would simply quarantine the message again and the administrator could never get it out. That has its own test. Deleting removes the row and the file together, and there is no other copy. Files live under `Data\Quarantine\YYYY-MM-DD\`, so the retention sweep can drop whole days; retention is 30 days by default and 0 means never, which is a real answer for an administrator keeping evidence. Over COM as `Settings.AntiSpam.Quarantine` - `ReleaseMessage` rather than `Release`, because every COM interface already inherits `IUnknown::Release` and the collision is a compile error - and in the Control Panel as a **review queue** beside the anti-spam settings, listing sender, recipients, subject, score and reason, with Release, Delete and a manual retention sweep. Delete asks first, because that message is the only copy. The two settings sit directly under the delete threshold rather than in a section of their own, since what they change is what that threshold DOES. Six tests, and the Control Panel's own "the overview links to every spam page" test caught the new page the moment it was added. **Off by default**: a server that has been refusing spam for years must not start accepting and storing it because of an upgrade. |
| ⬜ | Bayesian / statistical / ML classifier | No built-in learning filter, no per-user training, no autolearn feedback path; statistical filtering is only available by delegating to SpamAssassin |
| ⬜ | Binary plugin / module loading API | No plugin loader, no DLL extension point, no registered filter chain. The only extension surfaces are the COM API and the Active Scripting event file |
| ✅ | Configurable fail-closed / defer on scanner outage | Shipped 17 August 2026 as `[Settings] AVFailAction`. 0 is the shipped default and is exactly what this server has always done - deliver the message, having reported the scanner error - so no installation changes behaviour on upgrade. 1 inverts it: the message is held, re-attempted every `AVFailRetryMinutes` (default 15) at most `AVFailMaxHolds` times (default 16 - about four hours; 0 means never queue unscanned mail at all, tell the sender immediately), then returned to the sender with a stated reason. **The bound took three attempts, and all three rejected versions were real defects caught before shipping - which is why the row says this much.** Borrowing the SMTP delivery retry budget looks obviously correct until you check what `smtpnooftries` ships as - it is **0**, so that version bounced on the very first scanner blip and never held anything on a stock install; the fixture that 'proved' the hold only passed because it set the retry count itself. Bounding by the message's AGE was the second attempt, and an adversarial review of the delivery core caught it before it shipped: age is anchored to ARRIVAL, so any message already older than the window had its entire budget spent before the scanner ever failed. A backlog released after an outage, an ETRN batch, or POP3-fetched mail carrying an upstream `Received` date would be bounced with no hold at all - restarting a server whose scanner comes up a minute later than its mail queue would have mass-bounced the whole backlog, and a future-dated arrival time made the hold unbounded in the other direction. The third version counted the message's own try counter, and reading the code the review pointed at showed why that was worse than either: `PreprocessMessage_` DKIM-signs and mirrors only while `GetNoOfRetries() == 0`, so a hold that incremented it would have meant the pass that finally scanned the message skipped signing - every held message delivered UNSIGNED, failing DMARC at every receiver that checks. A security feature quietly disabling another security feature is worse than the gap it closes. The bound is therefore a count of holds this policy keeps itself, in memory, leaving the delivery machinery's own counters exactly as the rest of the core expects to find them. **Two limits of that choice are recorded rather than glossed, both found by a third review pass and both in the fail-safe direction** (mail is preserved and delivered on the first pass a scanner answers; nothing is ever delivered unscanned): a restart resets the counts while everything else about the message persists, so a hold window configured LONGER than the interval between restarts - 180 minutes x 16 holds on a host that reboots nightly, say - never reaches its budget and the sender is never told, though every hold is logged with the message id and hold number and the shipped four-hour default cannot reach this; and the hold table tracks 100,000 messages, beyond which a message is held without its holds being counted, so it too is never returned to its sender while the overflow lasts. That second one replaced something worse: the first cut cleared the WHOLE table when full, which reset every tracked message's count, so above the cap the bound stopped bounding for every message rather than for the overflow - and the warning latched once per process, so an operator was told once and never again. HM6195 now repeats hourly while the condition lasts. Persisting the count needs a schema change that one integer does not yet justify. It never delivers unscanned mail once set, and it never holds a message silently for ever - a message nobody is told about is the failure shape this project keeps finding in its own history. An unreadable creation time - the one input that would make the hold unbounded - bounces and reports HM6194 rather than holding a message nobody will ever hear about. A message with no reportable sender - one arriving with `MAIL FROM:<>`, from a mailer-daemon address, or carrying `Auto-Submitted` - cannot be told about, so it is discarded at the end of the window rather than delivered: falling back to delivery whenever a report cannot be raised would make an empty envelope sender a one-line bypass of the whole policy. That is the one outcome where mail is destroyed with nobody notified, so it reports HM6194 naming the message and its recipients rather than leaving an operator to find a log line. Deliberately not offered: an immediate 5xx reject, because a scanner outage is transient and bouncing legitimate mail because clamd was restarting is worse than deferring it. The lever is a distinction that did not previously exist in the delivery core: `PreprocessMessage_` returning false has always DELETED the message, so 'do not deliver this now, but keep it' needed its own signal (`deferDelivery`) rather than reusing the abort path - getting that backwards would lose mail, which is why the parameter is documented at the declaration. `VirusScanner::Scan` now reports whether any enabled scanner actually completed, which is the difference between 'this message is clean' and 'nobody looked'; both writes on the hold path are checked and report (HM6192/HM6193) rather than being assumed. Kept in the ini rather than the settings table because a new settings row reports HM5015 on every read until every database in the field has been upgraded, and this is one integer - the Control Panel field is the follow-up. |
| ⬜ | Milter protocol support | Not implemented, and now less likely rather than more: the alternative it was ruled out in favour of exists as of 20 August 2026. Milter is a binary protocol with a session state machine; the HTTP hook reaches the same engines over a transport this server already speaks in both directions, and can be debugged with curl. This row stays open only because "we chose not to" is worth recording next to "we have not got to it". |
| ✅ | Native HTTP filter hook (rspamd / MTA-hook style) | Shipped 20 August 2026 — see **HTTP filter hook** under *Planned work* for the design and its limits. |
| ⬜ | Other clamd transports and commands | No Unix/named-socket support, no TLS to clamd, and no use of PING, VERSION, STATS, MULTISCAN or zINSTREAM - INSTREAM is the only command the codebase ever sends |
| ✅ | Per-account spam settings — shipped 21 Aug 2026 on schema 6025 | One user drowning in spam wants aggressive filtering; the sales address must never lose a mail; both used to get the global thresholds. Accounts now carry a mark-threshold override, a delete-threshold override, and a filtering opt-out — deliberately **not** a per-account copy of every spam setting. **The design followed the evidence, not the brief**: my brief assumed the spam score was available at delivery, and the agent proved it is destroyed at accept time — only the spam flag survives into recipient copies, and the score exists solely in the optional `X-hMailServer-Reason-Score` header. So the delete override fires **only when the score is provable** — the recorded header when present, else the global mark threshold as a lower bound — and never deletes on a guess; the removed copy goes to the quarantine store when enabled, and a quarantine failure delivers marked rather than losing mail (HM6350). The opt-out's honest scope is stated in the helpstrings: delivery-only — a message the global settings refuse, quarantine or greylist is stopped for every recipient before any per-account setting can run, because a shared SMTP conversation cannot be refused selectively. An unmarked copy is genuinely unmarked: flag, headers and subject tag are rewritten out of that account's copy alone, and Sieve `spamtest`, rule conditions and the auto-replies all see the account's own view. |
| ⬜ | Per-user spamd preferences / other spamc features | No User: header is sent, so all mail is scanned under spamd's global preferences; no SYMBOLS/REPORT/CHECK commands, no TELL learning, no Unix-socket or TLS transport, no per-recipient SA profile |
| ✅ | Sender/domain blacklist — shipped 21 Aug 2026 on schema 6024 | There was a white list business object and no deny-list counterpart, so blocking a sender meant an IP-range deny, a rule, or nothing. `BlockedSenders` runs **through the existing spam-test pipeline** rather than a parallel refusal path, so thresholds, the quarantine fallback, the headers and the logging all apply unchanged; an entry carries its own score and the default of 100 crosses the shipped delete threshold. Matching is deliberately **narrower than the white list's**: `@` means an exact address, no `@` means a domain covering subdomains **anchored at a label boundary** (`example.com` matches `mail.example.com`, never `notexample.com` or `example.com.attacker.net`), and there are **no wildcards** — a deny list must be predictable, and a near-miss that matches is a delivery failure nobody asked for. The null sender is never blocked, so bounces stay deliverable. **What it does not do is stated in the helpstrings**: it matches the envelope sender, which the sender controls, so it stops a correspondent who reuses one address and nothing that rotates — it is not an anti-spoofing tool. Building it found a bug in the code it was modelled on: **`WhiteListCache` never cached** — `Refresh()` never cleared its dirty flag, so every message re-read `hm_whitelist`, and that accidental reload was masking a second bug where deleting an entry never invalidated the cache. Shipping needed one elevated `build/register-com.ps1` (an out-of-process COM server marshals by IID under HKLM, on both sides), after which its seven tests went green — including a raw-socket 550 whose assertion had to learn that RFC 2034 enhanced codes are an ESMTP capability the server rightly withholds from a HELO session. Full gate 1806/1806. |
| ⬜ | Tarpitting | Not implemented. The COM properties TarpitDelay and TarpitCount survive only as stubs marked "OBSOLETE: To be removed in v6" that return 0 and ignore writes; there is no delay logic anywhere in the SMTP or Common code. |
| ✅ | Virus-scanner failure policy is fail-open and undocumented | Resolved 17 August 2026. The posture is now named, configurable and tested rather than implicit: fail-open remains the default, `AVFailAction=1` makes it fail-closed (see the row above), and the code says which of the two it is doing and why at every branch. Three fixtures pin it, with the failure injected the way it actually happens in the field - the custom scanner pointed at an executable that does not exist, producing the same 'unable to launch' error a wrong path or a missing engine gives. The one that matters most is not that a withheld message is withheld: it is that the SAME message is delivered afterwards once the scanner works, because a fail-closed policy that lost the mail it declined to deliver would be far worse than the fail-open behaviour it replaces. The third asserts the sender gets a non-delivery report naming the reason when the retries are spent. Two adjacent cases are deliberately left fail-open and say so at the code: a message that could not be parsed for the per-attachment pass (HM6001) and an attachment that could not be written out for scanning (HM6002). The whole message file was still scanned in both, so treating a transient file lock as a scanner outage would hold ordinary mail for no security gain. |

### Storage, accounts and data model

90 shipped · 0 underway · 6 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Account address validation is filesystem-constrained | Beyond normal address validation the local part additionally forbids \ / ? * \| and spaces/quotes, and the whole address is capped at 254 characters, because the address becomes a directory name in the message store… |
| ✅ | Account and domain signatures with four combination modes | Plain-text and HTML signatures on both account and domain; the domain method is Set-if-not-specified / Overwrite-account / Append-to-account / none… |
| ✅ | Account delete cascade | Deletes all folders and their messages, force-deletes the Inbox, deletes rules, fetch accounts, group memberships and owned ACL grants, deletes the hm_accounts row… |
| ✅ | Account forwarding | Per-account forward address with keep-original and abort-if-spam-flagged flags, integrated with SRS rewriting and the rule loop counter. |
| ✅ | Account rename rewrites dependent data | **Two corrections, 13 August 2026 — this row was wrong in both halves.** The domain half now works as described: `PersistentDomain::SaveObject` follows `NameChanger::RenameDomain` with a sweep of the three columns that can name an address in another domain — `hm_aliases.aliasvalue`, `hm_distributionlistsrecipients.distributionlistrecipientaddress` and `hm_accounts.accountforwardaddress` — because RenameDomain iterates only `pDomain->GetAccounts()`, `GetAliases()` and `GetDistributionLists()`, so a second local domain holding a reference into the renamed one kept the old name. That was not untidiness: the old name stops being hosted, `RecipientParser` finds no account for it and falls to its external branch, so mail that had been delivered locally was relayed to whatever MX now answers for the old name — and on the alias path `bTreatSecurityAsLocal` is already set, so the relay restriction did not stop it. Matching is on the exact domain name, case-insensitively, so an external target, a third local domain and a subdomain of the renamed one are all left alone. Covered by `Domains/DomainRenameCascade.cs`, six tests, two of which assert the message arrives in the mailbox rather than that a field changed. **The account half is still not true and is now declared: `NameChanger::RenameAccount` renames the on-disk mailbox directory and nothing else.** Renaming one account leaves every alias targeting it, every list membership naming it and every other account forwarding to it pointing at an address that no longer exists. The exposure is lower than the domain case — the stale address is still at a local domain, so mail bounces at 550 or hits the catch-all rather than leaving the server — but it is a gap, not a feature. |
| ✅ | Account size tracked by an in-memory delta cache | AccountSizeCache seeds from SUM(messagesize) on first read then applies +/- deltas on every save and delete; it is per-process and is reset on account save/delete… |
| ✅ | Aliases, catch-all and plus-addressing | Per-domain aliases and domain aliases, a domain catch-all address, and per-domain plus-addressing with a configurable separator character. None of the address-resolution surface appears in the inventory. |
| ✅ | Automatic reconnect and statement retry | Only *connection* problems retry. `DALConnection::Execute` loops up to six times, reconnecting on attempts 2 and 4 and after any DALConnectionProblem with a 1-second pause - but it breaks out of the loop on any other result, so a statement rejected for a SQL reason is attempted exactly once. `DALRecordset::Open` hardcodes six tries and ignores `SetTryCount`. **Corrected 13 August 2026:** the reconnect branch never fired on PostgreSQL for the case it exists for. When the server closes the connection mid-statement libpq does not return a null result - it builds a PGRES_FATAL_ERROR result and marks the connection bad - so `PGConnection::CheckError`, classifying on result status alone, returned DALErrorInSQL and the statement was reported failed and dropped; only the *next* statement, which libpq refuses outright, triggered a reconnect. One statement lost per connection drop, silently. CheckError now consults `PQstatus`: a statement the server rejected leaves the connection CONNECTION_OK, one that got no answer does not. MySQL already recognised the same event through error codes 2006/2013. |
| ✅ | Background indexer thread with quick/full modes | Dedicated worker wakes every minute; a "quick" pass indexes only the newest IndexerQuickLimit (default 1000) messages, a "full" pass runs every IndexerFullMinutes (default 720) up to IndexerFullLimit (default 25000) rows. |
| ✅ | Backup and restore | BackupManager/BackupExecuter with a scheduled BackupTask, selectable components (settings, domains, messages), 7za compression, an optional messages-database-only mode (BackupMessagesDBOnly), and COM/Control Panel surfaces. A restore validates the archive and stages the message store before its first deletion (BackupRestorer::Prepare), so an archive that cannot satisfy the selected options is refused with the server untouched rather than deleting everything and finding it has nothing to put back. **Audited for honesty on 13 August 2026 - not "does it work" but "can it tell you when it did not" - because this is now documented as the database-migration mechanism and an operator's whole safety net is that a backup which reports success contains what they think it does.** It could not. `Configuration::XMLStore` called nine child stores for their side effects, discarded every answer and returned true, and `BackupExecuter` discarded that true as well: the settings half of a backup was structurally incapable of failing. `XMLLoad` beside it checks at every single step, which is what gives it away - the restore was hardened at some point and the backup was not. Both are checked now, and a settings section that cannot be written fails the backup with no archive left behind. Three more of the same shape: `SMTPConfiguration::XMLLoad` restored routes and incoming relays unchecked under a function that returned true regardless - the two collections `Configuration::XMLLoad`'s otherwise complete guard did not reach, and the two that decide where mail may go and who may relay through this server; `IMAPConfiguration` was unchecked in both directions for public folders and groups; and the public-folder `DeleteAll` in the restore path was unchecked one line below a domain `DeleteAll` that is checked, where the consequence is worse than the domains case because `XMLLoad` then puts the backup's folders in on top of whatever survived. Underneath all of them, `Collection<T,P>::XMLLoad` answered **true** when its opening `DeleteAll` failed and then loaded nothing, so a collection the restore could not clear was reported as restored - `BackupExecuter` guards the domains case from outside for exactly this reason, and the guard now lives where it covers every collection instead of the one that was noticed. **A gap found on 14 August 2026 while costing the INI-settings migration, and it is a real one:** `Configuration::XMLStore` writes its `<Properties>` node by iterating `PropertySet`, which is the `hm_settings` table — so the **126 `[Settings]` values in hMailServer.INI are in no backup archive at all**. MTA-STS, DANE, ACME, TLS-RPT, greylisting intervals, thread counts, rate limits: every setting this fork has added. Back up, rebuild a machine, restore, and you get the domains, the accounts, the mail and the *old* database-held settings, while everything else silently falls back to its default. The same-machine backend migration in `docs\MigratingDatabaseBackend.md` is unaffected because the INI never moves there, but a machine rebuild is not, and "the backup contains the settings" is not true as stated. Closed by the INI-to-database migration below rather than by teaching the backup to read a file, because settings that live in the database are backed up, restorable and remotely administrable for the same single reason. |
| ✅ | Backup/restore of settings, domains and messages | Backup writes an XML document of business objects plus (optionally) the whole data directory, 7z-compressed; BackupMessagesDBOnly skips the files and keeps only the rows. Requires all message files to be inside the data folder. A restore refuses any category the archive does not contain — Collection::XMLLoad cannot tell "empty at backup time" from "absent from this archive", so restoring domains or settings from an archive without them used to empty them and report success — and refuses an archive written by a later hMailServer than the one running, which is what the index's Version attribute has been for since 2010. |
| ✅ | Backup of a live message store | The data-directory copy tolerates what a running server does underneath it: files deleted between enumeration and copy are counted and reported in the backup log, a destination file left by an earlier run is overwritten, and a file another process holds is retried within a bounded budget and then fails the backup by name. It does not use FileUtilities::CopyDirectory, whose throwing copy_file escaped the work-queue thread and terminated the service — which, with CompressDestinationFiles off, happened on the second backup an installation took. A backup is still not a snapshot: rows are read before files, so a message deleted in between is listed in the archive without its file, and the backup log says how many. |
| ✅ | Bound parameters on MSSQL/SQL CE only | ADO and SQL CE report GetSupportsCommandParameters()==true; MySQL and PostgreSQL report false, so their statements are rebuilt by literal interpolation with SQLStatement::Escape rather than server-side binding. |
| ✅ | Bulk delete by account bypasses path reconstruction | DeleteByAccountID selects messagefilename and passes it straight to DeleteFile — which works only for rows still holding a full path, so partial-filename rows leave orphan files behind (logged as error 5024). |
| ✅ | Catch-all is the domain postmaster field | There is one catch-all per domain (hm_domains.domainpostmaster). If no account, alias, list or route matches, delivery is redirected to it; if it is blank the recipient is rejected with "Unknown user". No server-wide catch-all exists. |
| ✅ | Continuous upgrade chain, 0 to the required version | Every step from hMailServer 1.0 onward is registered; from step 5001 onward every step ships in four dialects (MSSQL, MSSQLCE, MySQL, PGSQL). Run by DBUpdater.exe, not by the service. Checked mechanically by `build\check-schema-versions.ps1` rather than by reading: no duplicate or self-referencing step, none unreachable from 0, and the walk from 0 reaches `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION` in exactly as many steps as are registered. The script reads both numbers out of the tree rather than holding either, which is why this row was the only place they could go stale — and they had, twice: by sixteen versions before one audit, and by three before the next. So the numbers are gone from the row. `build\check-schema-versions.ps1` prints them and proves the walk from 0 reaches `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION` in exactly as many steps as are registered, which is the fact worth having; a transcription of it is not. |
| ✅ | Data Directory Synchronizer (two-way reconcile tool) | Import mode adds .eml/.hma files found on disk into the database via Utilities.ImportMessageFromFile; Delete mode removes files that have no matching row. Domain-scoped or whole-directory. |
| ✅ | Database-outage behaviour | A recipient lookup that fails because the database did not answer returns 451 rather than 550, so a database locked by a backup defers mail instead of bouncing it… |
| ✅ | DBSetup wizard | Wizard flow (Welcome → select database type → connection info → action → service dependency → perform task → completed) supporting Microsoft SQL Server, MySQL/MariaDB and PostgreSQL… |
| ✅ | DBSetupQuick — unattended installer path | Headless entry point used by the installer: creates the database if absent, otherwise shells DBUpdater.exe /SilentIfOk (forwarding /silent) and propagates the child exit code… |
| ✅ | DBUpdater — chained schema upgrades | Registers a chain of upgrade steps from schema 0 through 6006 and applies them in sequence inside one transaction. Scripts exist for four backends (MSSQL, MySQL, PGSQL and MSSQLCE from 5001 onward); the older pre-5001 steps ship MSSQL and MySQL only. **Hardened 13 August 2026,** and what it was hiding is the worst defect this area has. Three of the four dialects mark their `ALTER TABLE` with the DAL's `[IGNORE-ERRORS]` marker so that re-running a step is harmless — but `MySQLConnection::TryExecute` (`bool bIgnoreErrors = SQL.Find(_T("[IGNORE-ERRORS]")) >= 0`), `ADOConnection::TryExecute` and `SQLCEConnection::Execute` all match that marker *anywhere in the statement* and then discard **every** error, not only "already exists", while the `update hm_dbversion set value = N` in the same script carries no marker and therefore always runs. So an `ALTER` that failed for a real reason — insufficient rights, a row-size limit, a syntax form the backend rejects — stamped the new version onto a database that did not have the column; `DoUpgrade` set `UpgradeSucceeded` because `CommitTransaction()` had not thrown; the installer got exit code 0; the service then started, because the version it required was the version it found; and `IMAPFolders::LoadFolders`, whose `select` names `folderspecialuse` explicitly, failed for **every account on the system** with nothing anywhere saying why. `SQLScriptRunner::ExecuteScript` is otherwise fail-fast, so the marker was the only swallow — and it is on the newest step, 6005→6006. Now: `SchemaVerification` proves each marked step **before** the commit with a statement that cannot fail for any other reason (`update <table> set <column> = <column> where 1 = 0`, run on the transaction's own connection so a missing object is still rollback-able on the two backends with transactional DDL), and the committed `hm_dbversion` is read back and compared with `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION` instead of assumed. `UpgradeSucceeded` — which `Program.Main` turns into the installer's exit code — is set only after both, and a post-commit mismatch deliberately leaves the ERROR log in place rather than deleting the server's own complaint about the version. `build\check-schema-versions.ps1` fails the release if a marked script has no probe, which is what stops the next added column from re-opening this; it reports 6 failures against the tree as it stood before this change. Still true, and not fixed by any of the above: MySQL and SQL CE commit DDL implicitly, so a step that completes followed by one that fails leaves a genuinely half-upgraded database — the tool now says so instead of reporting success, and making the scripts idempotent is the remaining work. |
| ✅ | Dead legacy tables left behind on upgraded databases | hm_serverlog, hm_filters, hm_deliverylog and hm_deliverylog_recipients are created by the 1.2→1.4 and 3.301→3.4 steps and never dropped on MySQL/PGSQL; no current code references them, and fresh installs never get them. |
| ✅ | Delivery-queue poll query has no supporting index | The queue scan filters messagetype/messagelocked/messagenexttrytime and orders by messagesize, messagecurnooftries, messageid; the only relevant index is idx_hm_messages_type on messagetype alone… |
| ✅ | Distribution lists | List modes (public/membership/announcement), require-SMTP-authentication, a require-from address restriction, and AD bulk-import of members from the Control Panel. No moderation, no self-subscribe… |
| ✅ | Domain aliases | hm_domain_aliases rewrites the domain part of both sender and recipient addresses before any account/alias/list lookup, and is applied again when comparing list owners and members. |
| ✅ | Domain delete cascade | Deletes all accounts, aliases, distribution lists and domain aliases, then the hm_domains row, then the {DataDir}\{domain} directory tree. **Extended 13 August 2026 to deal with what other domains still point at, which was a mail-confidentiality defect and not an orphan-row one:** an address at a deleted domain is no longer local, so an alias target, a list membership or an account forward naming it silently became a relay to whatever MX now answers for that name. Each is now handled in the way that fails closed and stays visible — an alias is switched off (delivery is refused with "Alias is not active.") with its target left in the row so the administrator can see what it was, forwarding is switched off with the address likewise kept, and a list membership, which has no active flag to switch, is removed and logged with the address and the list so it can be put back. Every name the domain answered to is covered, including its domain aliases, collected before those rows are deleted. The sweep runs only after `delete from hm_domains` succeeds, so a failed delete cannot disable references into a domain that still exists, and it never fails the delete — a row it could not update is reported (5980/5981) and the rest continue. Still not transactional, and the row below explains why that is not a small fix. Covered by `Domains/DomainDeleteCascade.cs`, six tests, one of which asserts the message is REFUSED rather than accepted for outbound relay. |
| ✅ | Domain limits enforced only at object save | Max accounts / aliases / distribution lists, max per-account size and total domain size are all checked in PreSaveLimitationsCheck when the object is created or edited — never during mail flow… **Correction, 13 August 2026: "individually toggleable" was true of two of the three.** Each limit is a number plus a bit in `domainlimitationsenabled`, and the Control Panel draws it as a checkbox beside a number (`DomainDialog.cs`) which leaves the number alone when the box is cleared. The account and alias checks test the flag; the distribution-list check tested the NUMBER, so clearing that one checkbox did nothing at all — the stale number went on being enforced — while ticking it with the number at 0 enforced nothing. Both columns arrived in the same schema step (Upgrade4301to4400), so no upgraded database can hold a number without its flag: the only way to reach the broken state was for an administrator to switch the limit off. Now reads the flag, like the other two. Covered by `Domains/DomainLimitEnableFlags.cs`. |
| ✅ | Embedded SQL CE as the zero-config default | The installer's default choice is "Use the built-in database engine", which registers SQL Server Compact 4.0; the connection string caps the file at 4000 MB, and the create script used is CreateTablesMSSQL.sql. |
| ✅ | Envelope recipient de-duplication | After alias, list and catch-all expansion, AddRecipient_ drops any address already present (case-insensitive), so overlapping lists do not produce duplicate copies. |
| ✅ | Fixed-size connection pool with bounded acquisition | Pool size from [Database] NumberOfConnections; waiters block on a condition variable with a DBConnectionAcquireTimeout deadline, and a timeout marks the database unavailable so callers return 451 instead of 550. |
| ✅ | Folder depth cap | Hard limit of 25 nesting levels enforced when validating folder paths. |
| ✅ | Four SQL backends behind one abstraction, with a continuous upgrade chain | MySQL/MariaDB (vendored MariaDB Connector/C, caching_sha2/ed25519 capable), MS SQL via ADO, PostgreSQL via libpq, and embedded SQL CE for zero-configuration installs, all behind DALConnection/DALRecordset with parameterised queries… |
| ✅ | Four SQL backends behind one DAL | MS SQL Server (ADO/OLE DB), MySQL/MariaDB (vendored MariaDB Connector/C as libmysql.dll), PostgreSQL (libpq) and SQL Server Compact Edition, selected by an enum and built by a factory; every persistence class is backend-agnostic. |
| ✅ | Groups as ACL principals | hm_groups / hm_group_members give named account groups usable as an ACL principal type, with their own cache TTL setting. **Fixed 13 August 2026: deleting a group never deleted its membership rows.** `PersistentGroup::DeleteObject` removed the hm_groups row and the group's ACL grants, but there was no `DeleteByGroup` anywhere — only `DeleteByAccount` — and with no foreign key in the schema the rows survived for the life of the database. Dead weight while the id stayed unused, and worse if it were ever handed out again, because a group is an ACL principal. The same change stopped the cleanup running unconditionally: a failed group-row delete used to strip the permissions off a group that still existed, and now touches nothing. Declared gap: this one has no regression test and cannot have one through the public surface — orphan membership rows are invisible to COM, which has no SELECT-returning API, and forcing identity reuse to expose them is backend-specific enough to prove nothing. |
| ✅ | GUID-named .eml files, hashed into 256 sub-folders | Filename is `{GUID}.eml`; account mail lives at {DataDir}\{domain}\{localpart}\{first 2 GUID chars}\{filename}, public-folder mail at {DataDir}\{#Public}\{first 2 GUID chars}\{filename}, and queue files sit flat in {DataDir}. The 2-character level bounds directory width per account. The reverse mapping, `PersistentMessage::GetPartialFilename`, reduces either layout back to `{guid}.eml`; its public-folder branch compared the wrong substring and so could never match, which meant nothing under #Public was recognised as part of the store — fixed, with a regression test. |
| ✅ | hm_imapfolders with per-folder UID and MODSEQ counters | Unique index on (accountid, parentid, name); foldercurrentuid and foldercurrentmodseq are monotonic counters bumped with UPDATE ... = x + 1 then re-read, mirrored into the in-memory folder container. |
| ✅ | hm_message_metadata header index | Indexes exactly five fields per delivered message — date (UTC), from, subject, to, cc — keyed by account+folder+message with a unique index. Values are truncated to 100 characters on write even though the columns are varchar(255). |
| ✅ | IMAP QUOTA extension | QUOTA is advertised and GETQUOTA/GETQUOTAROOT report a single unnamed root with STORAGE used/limit in KB; when the account has no quota the resource list is returned empty. Gated by the enableimapquota setting. |
| ✅ | Index is consumed by IMAP SORT only | IMAPSort loads the metadata map for From/Subject/To/CC/Date; IMAP SEARCH does not touch it at all and calls PersistentMessage::LoadHeader per message, so header search is unindexed even when indexing is on. |
| ✅ | Indexing is off by default | The MessageIndexing setting ships as 0; with it off nothing populates hm_message_metadata and IMAP SORT falls back to reading each message header from disk. |
| ✅ | Max message size, global and per-domain | The effective limit is min(global maxmessagesize, domain maxmessagesize where non-zero) in KB. Enforced three times: against the MAIL FROM SIZE= estimate (552), against the actual buffer during DATA, and at IMAP APPEND… |
| ✅ | Message archiving | [Settings] ArchiveDir writes a raw copy of every message into {ArchiveDir}\{senderDomain}\{senderUser}\ trees, with an optional hardlink mode. No retention, no per-domain scope, no index, no search, no immutability or legal hold. |
| ✅ | Message files are never replaced by data that failed to load or failed to write | `MessageData::Write` refuses to serialise a MimeBody whose `LoadFromMessage` gave up — a message over the 80 MB parser cap, or one that threw while parsing — because the load leaves the body EMPTY and writing it replaced the message with a stub; it reports error 6011 when the write itself fails instead of stamping the row with the size of a file it did not write. The check lives in Write rather than in its callers because rules, attachment stripping, forwarding and the bounce generator all ignore the return value. **Corrected 13 August 2026 — "one that threw while parsing" was not true as written.** A throw from `MimeBody::Load` never reached `MessageData`: `MimeBody::LoadFromFile` wrapped the parse in its own `catch (...)`, reported 4228, copied the file into "Problematic messages" — and then returned **true**. Only a throw from `ReadTextFile`, which sits outside that try, reached the caller. So the guard covered a read that failed and not a parse that failed, which is the more likely of the two. `LoadFromFile` now returns `MimeLoadResult` — `Loaded` / `OpenFailed` / `ParseFailed` — and `LoadFromMessage` fails on `ParseFailed`, so `load_failed_` is set and the row is true for both. |
| ✅ | The MIME parser is bounded against the message it is parsing | Added 13 August 2026, the first audit this subsystem has had. `MimeBody::Load` recursed once per level of multipart nesting with nothing bounding it — each level costs a `Load` frame plus `MimeHeader::Load` and `MimeField::Load` beneath it, and a level is spelled in about forty bytes, so roughly 250 KB of nested boundaries exhausts a 1 MB thread stack. That is `EXCEPTION_STACK_OVERFLOW` in the delivery thread, a mail outage rather than a rejected message, and not something the surrounding `catch (...)` can be relied on to survive because the handler runs on the stack that just ran out. Parsing now stops splitting below `MimeBody::MaxNestingDepth` (20) and keeps the remainder as opaque content, so **nothing is discarded** — the sub-tree survives in `text_` and both serialisation paths round-trip it byte for byte; what is lost is only the split, so BODYSTRUCTURE shows fewer levels and an attachment buried below 20 is not enumerated. Also closed in the same pass: `MimeCodeBase64::Decode` stopped at the first byte outside the base64 alphabet, so one space anywhere in a base64 part truncated it silently — RFC 2045 6.8 requires such bytes to be ignored, and the consequence was that `VirusScanner` wrote a truncated attachment to its temp file and the scanner reported it clean; and `LoadEncapsulatedMessage` copied a `message/rfc822` part with `strncpy_s` into an uninitialised buffer before parsing the full length, so a NUL byte in the part left the parser reading heap, and what it found there became the attachment filename shown to COM clients, matched against the blocked-attachment list and used to name the scanner's temp file. |
