| ✅ | RFC 2231 parameter continuations and charset-tagged parameter values | `MimeField::GetParameter` concatenates every segment whose base name matches, so `filename*0="long"; filename*1="name.txt"` reassembles; a `*` immediately before the `=` marks the value as charset-tagged and `MimeParameterRFC2184Decoder` rewrites `UTF-8''%E9%2Epng` into an encoded word for the normal decoder. `MimeField::RemoveParameter` removes all continuations of a name together, which is what makes `SetFileName` able to replace a split filename rather than leave half of it behind. Not implemented: the language tag is parsed and discarded, and continuation segments are joined in the order they appear rather than by their index. |
| ✅ | Message delete order of operations | DELETE the hm_messages row → write a QRESYNC tombstone (if it was in a folder) → delete hm_messagerecipients rows (queue messages only) → adjust the account size cache → delete hm_message_metadata → zero the in-memory id → delete the file. The file path is rebuilt from the cached account, so when the account cannot be loaded the path degrades to a public-folder path and the delete removes nothing while answering success; that case is now written to the application log rather than leaking the file in silence. |
| ✅ | Message flags are a fixed 8-bit bitmask | messageflags is tinyint unsigned holding Seen/Deleted/Flagged/Answered/Draft/Recent/VirusScan/Spam. SELECT advertises those five system flags and no \* in PERMANENTFLAGS, so client-defined IMAP keywords cannot be stored. |
| ✅ | Message metadata indexer | A background worker populates hm_message_metadata (date, from, subject, to, cc), which is what makes IMAP header SEARCH and SORT indexed rather than linear. Tunable via IndexerFullMinutes/IndexerFullLimit/IndexerQuickLimit… |
| ✅ | Message rows carry DSN NOTIFY per recipient | hm_messagerecipients.recipientdsnnotify (added at DB version 6005) records the RFC 3461 NOTIFY setting, and LocalDelivery suppresses the failure DSN when the sender opted out. |
| ✅ | Message-store consistency scan | Hourly task walks every hm_messages row, resolves the on-disk path and reports rows whose file is missing — as a Prometheus gauge and as a tab-separated recovery report in the log directory… |
| ✅ | Message-store integrity: fsync and consistency check | MessageStoreFsync=1 forces each received message to physical storage (_commit/FlushFileBuffers) before it is acknowledged; MessageStoreConsistencyCheck=1 runs a read-only periodic cross-check of message rows against files on disk… |
| ✅ | Missing-file placeholder generation | If a file referenced by a row is gone at retrieval time, the server synthesises a MESSAGE_FILE_MISSING notice in its place, resizes the row *and* the cached account size to match the placeholder, and logs error 5026, so the client gets a readable message instead of an error. If the placeholder itself cannot be written, the row is left describing the original message and error 6012 is logged — it used to store a size of 0, which AddObject then refused, so the failure was invisible twice over; and the cached account size was never adjusted, so the account stayed charged for a message that no longer existed. |
| ✅ | MSSQL failover partner and provider override | [Database] Provider and DatabaseServerFailoverPartner are honoured; with a failover partner set the provider defaults to SQLNCLI, otherwise MSOLEDBSQL, and FailoverPartner= is appended to the connection string. |
| ✅ | Native vacation / auto-reply | Per-account vacation subject, body, on/off and an expiry date, with a spam-flag guard and per-sender dedupe. Cited in the roadmap as the irony behind the missing Sieve vacation extension… |
| ✅ | Object caches with per-type TTLs | Domain, account, alias, distribution-list and group caches each have their own TTL setting (default 60) plus a master usecache flag; there is also an inbox-id cache and a short-lived message cache used to hand a just-accepted message t… |
| ✅ | OpenTelemetry spans for database queries | Each statement emits a `db.query` client span with `db.system` (mysql/mssql/postgresql/sqlce) and a redacted `db.statement`, parented to the active protocol-command span; no-op unless OtelEndpoint is set. |
| ✅ | Optional fsync before acknowledgement | MessageStoreFsync=1 flushes the message file to disk before the transmission buffer completes, trading throughput for durability across a power loss. Off by default. |
| ✅ | Orphaned-metadata cleanup | DeleteOrphanedItems runs once, when the indexer thread starts; there is no scheduled sweep, so metadata for rows deleted while indexing was disabled lingers until the next service restart. |
| ✅ | Partial filenames in the database | hm_messages.messagefilename normally stores only `{guid}.eml` and the path is reconstructed from account address + location; full absolute paths are still tolerated for pre-5.4 rows. The reduction back to a partial name is case-insensitive about both the data directory and the fan-out folder, because the paths it is handed come from Windows callers, and it now works for public-folder paths as well as account-folder ones. |
| ✅ | Password hash upgrade on successful login | `accountpwencryption` records the algorithm (0 none, 1 Blowfish, 2 MD5, 3 SHA256, 4 PBKDF2, 5 Argon2id, 6 DPAPI). After a password verifies, `PasswordValidator` re-hashes to `PreferredHashAlgorithm` and persists it, upgrading only upward and only to a strong KDF. Plaintext (`0`) accounts are upgraded too — the comparison branch falls through to the upgrade, re-hashing the stored password — with `PersistentAccount::ReadObject`'s in-memory re-hash integrated via a pending-upgrade flag. Blowfish rows are deliberately left alone: their comparison is case-insensitive live behaviour, and hashing them would make it case-sensitive. The minimum-hash policy check runs after the upgrade attempt, so a policy-enabled install heals rather than locks out. |
| ✅ | Per-account forwarding | hm_accounts carries forwardenabled / forwardaddress / forwardkeeporiginal / forwardabortspamflagged; optional envelope-from rewriting on forward is controlled by RewriteEnvelopeFromWhenForwarding. |
| ✅ | Per-account mailbox quota | accountmaxsize in MB; Account::SpaceAvailable compares AccountSizeCache + incoming size against it. Zero means unlimited. |
| ✅ | Per-backend connection + recordset pair | ADOConnection/ADORecordset, MySQLConnection/MySQLRecordset, PGConnection/PGRecordset, SQLCEConnection/SQLCERecordset all implement DALConnection/DALRecordset; MySQL client is loaded dynamically through MySQLInterface. |
| ✅ | Per-backend DDL macro expanders | Upgrade scripts embed `@@@macro@@@` directives (drop column, rename column, add index...) that each backend expands into its own dialect, so one logical upgrade step is written four ways only where it must be. |
| ✅ | Per-domain aliases | hm_aliases maps a full address to any target address (local or external) with an active flag; resolution loops with a 25-level recursion cap and an inactive alias produces "Alias is not active." |
| ✅ | Plus addressing (subaddressing) | Enabled per domain with a configurable separator character (validated as non-empty when enabled); everything from the separator to @ is stripped before account lookup. The tag is not preserved anywhere in the data model. |
| ✅ | Pre-upgrade data prerequisites | A prerequisite registry runs data fixes before the step that needs them — currently PreReqNoDuplicateFolders, which renames duplicate IMAP folders before the 5200 unique index is applied. Only one prerequisite is registered. |
| ✅ | Public folders are exempt from quota | APPEND checks account quota only when the destination is not a public folder, and public-folder messages carry accountid 0 so they never contribute to any account's size — public folder growth is entirely unbounded. |
| ✅ | Public-folder message path | Public folder mail lives at {DataDir}\{PublicFolderDiskName}\{2 chars}\{filename}; the public folder disk name comes from the imappublicfoldername setting (default `#Public`). |
| ✅ | QRESYNC expunge tombstones (hm_imapexpunged) | Every expunge writes account+folder+UID+modseq so VANISHED (EARLIER) can be replayed. Added at DB version 6003. |
| ✅ | Queue files are flat in the data-directory root | Messages in the Delivering state get no fan-out folder at all — the file sits directly in {DataDir}. A large backed-up queue therefore produces one very wide directory. |
| ✅ | Quota is enforced at delivery, not at RCPT TO | CheckAccountQuotas_ runs inside LocalDelivery, after the message has been accepted, so an over-quota recipient produces a bounce/DSN ("Inbox is full") rather than an SMTP-time 4xx/5xx — i.e. backscatter to a possibly forged sender… |
| ✅ | Quota model: per-account and per-domain size limits only | Account and domain maximum sizes are enforced, but only during local delivery, so an over-quota recipient generates a DSN (backscatter) rather than a 5xx at RCPT TO. No warning threshold… |
| ✅ | RFC 4314 ACLs with inheritance | hm_acl stores per-folder rights (l r s w i p k t e x a as a bitmask) for a user, a group, or "anyone"; ACLManager walks up to the nearest ancestor that has an ACL, and the folder owner always gets full rights. |
| ✅ | Server configuration lives in hm_settings as a name/value property set | Single table of settingname / settingstring (varchar 4000) / settinginteger, loaded into a PropertySet; secret-valued properties are stored through Crypt::ProtectSecret. Everything not in hm_settings is in hMailServer.INI (paths… |
| ✅ | Signatures only append to existing body parts | The signature is appended to the text/plain part and/or the text/html part if they already exist; no part is created, so a message with neither (or an unusual MIME structure) silently gets no signature… |
| ✅ | Single #Public shared namespace | A folder is a public folder purely by having accountid 0; the namespace name is configurable via imappublicfoldername (default `#Public`) and is reported as the one shared namespace. |
| ✅ | Single-recipient file reuse | When a queue message has exactly one local recipient the file is moved into the account folder and the same hm_messages row is repurposed (accountid/folderid set, state → Delivered) rather than copied. |
| ✅ | Slow-query log with SQL literal redaction | Queries over SlowQueryLogMilliseconds are logged with every single-quoted literal collapsed to '?' (handles '' and backslash escapes) so credentials never reach the log; also feeds ServerStatus query counters. |
| ✅ | [IGNORE-ERRORS] marker semantics | The DAL's marker lets a re-run of an upgrade step whose column already exists pass, on the three backends whose DDL has no IF NOT EXISTS form. It was found with a plain substring search over the whole statement, which meant a *value* carrying that text switched error handling off for the statement it appeared in - reachable from outside, because hm_message_metadata writes four attacker-supplied header fields per delivered message and the SQL log device writes logged protocol lines; the prize was one silently discarded write. **Narrowed 13 August 2026:** `DALConnection::HasIgnoreErrorsMarker` scans the statement, skips single-quoted literals (handling `''` and the MySQL backslash escape), and honours the marker only inside a `--` or `/* */` comment, which is where all 17 real uses in the tree write it. Separately, no backend may now apply the marker to a `DALConnectionProblem`: "this object already exists" and "the statement never reached the server" are not the same thing, and it was the second masquerading as the first that stamped schema 6006 onto databases that had not got the column. Still true: what the marker discards is "every error that is not a connection problem" rather than a known set of already-exists codes, because getting one of those codes wrong per backend would turn a harmless re-run into a hard installer failure. Covered by `Infrastructure/SqlLayerErrorHandling.cs`, with a negative control asserting the marker still works where it is genuinely written. |
| ✅ | Statement timeouts on all four backends — shipped 21 Aug 2026 | `DALConnection::SetTimeout` was an empty `{}` on MySQL and PostgreSQL, so on those two a statement blocked on a lock held its pooled connection until the server or the network gave up. Now: PostgreSQL sets the `statement_timeout` GUC at connect (and again after a reconnect, because a GUC dies with its session); MySQL and MariaDB are told apart by `SELECT VERSION()` **before** any variable is set, because the two forks share a variable name and disagree on the unit — MariaDB's `max_statement_time` is seconds, MySQL's `max_execution_time` is milliseconds, and probing by name alone would set a 30-millisecond limit and abort essentially every statement on one of them. The client-library version call cannot be used for the split: MariaDB announces itself in the handshake as `5.5.5-<version>` and is reported as MySQL. One limit for the whole server, `DatabaseStatementTimeout` (default 30, the value ADO and SQL CE already used; 0 = none), and `SQLScriptRunner` now restores *that* after its long-window script runs instead of a hard-coded 30. Honest limit, stated in the header: MySQL's mechanism covers read-only SELECTs, so a blocked UPDATE there still waits — MariaDB and PostgreSQL cover statements generally. **Untested against live MySQL/MariaDB/PostgreSQL on this bench** (the suite is SQL CE), which is written here because it is the row's biggest caveat. |
| ✅ | SRS and BATV reverse resolution in the recipient parser | An SRS0 address at a local domain is HMAC-verified and rewritten to the original sender; a prvs= address is verified and stripped to the original local recipient. Both run before plus-addressing… |
| ✅ | Transactions | BeginTransaction/Commit/Rollback exist on the connection manager and are reachable only from the COM API; nothing inside the server uses them, so the message-save path is not transactional. **Characterised per backend, 13 August 2026, and written into the code above `DatabaseConnectionManager::BeginTransaction`.** Scope first: the connection is checked out of the pool and only statements run on *that object* are inside the transaction. `Execute()` and `OpenRecordset()` take no connection and borrow a different pooled one per statement, so the only caller that gets this right is `InterfaceDatabase`, which keeps the connection in `conn_` and routes ExecuteSQL / ExecuteSQLScript / EnsurePrerequisites onto it. Then per backend: **MSSQL** real, DDL included, but `Connect()` puts the session at READ UNCOMMITTED so it buys atomicity of its own writes and no read isolation at all. **PostgreSQL** real and the strongest of the four - DDL is transactional and the session is at the server default. **MySQL** conditional: BEGIN is issued only if every table reported InnoDB at connect time (`LoadSupportsTransactions_`), otherwise BeginTransaction returns true having done nothing and it is Rollback that finally says so with error 5104; DDL commits implicitly regardless. **SQL CE** DML only - schema changes commit as executed. So: worth having on MSSQL and PostgreSQL, worth having for DML on SQL CE, worth having on MySQL only for an all-InnoDB database, worth nothing for DDL on two of the four - and file deletions cannot be rolled back on any of them. |
| ✅ | UID assignment serialised in-process only | FolderManipulationLock is a static std::set guarded by a process-wide mutex, so strictly-ascending UID assignment holds for one server instance but is not enforced by the database — a second node against the same schema could interleav… |
| ✅ | Version pin enforced at startup | The `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION` in `Constants.h` is compiled in (read it there rather than from this row, which has been wrong three times); `Application::OnDatabaseConnected` refuses the connection and returns false in both directions, logging error 5011 if hm_dbversion is lower ("run DBUpdater") or higher ("upgrade hMailServer"). The regression pre-flight now names this fault directly (`build\preflight-tests.ps1` check 4): a bench database left behind by a version bump previously surfaced only as "service could not start" plus a stale ERROR log, and neither of those says the word "database". |
| ✅ | Zero-byte and folderless messages are refused at save | AddObject aborts if the message size is 0, and reports error 5213 if a Delivered message has no folder id — the two ways a row could otherwise point at nothing. |
| ⬜ | Send-As for a shared mailbox | The other-users namespace itself shipped 21 Aug 2026 — see the completed row above: `#Users` resolves, per-folder ACLs are evaluated through `ACLManager`, and the grant surface is open. What is still absent is **Send-As**: a delegate can read and file mail in a shared mailbox but cannot send *as* it, because submission authorises the authenticated account and nothing consults the ACL when choosing an envelope sender. (This row read "the other-users namespace is hard-coded to NIL" until 21 Aug 2026, hours after it stopped being.) |
| ⬜ | Deduplication / hardlinking of delivered mail | A message to N local recipients is written N times: CopyFromQueueToInbox does a full FileUtilities::Copy per recipient. Hardlinking exists only in the archiver, never in the live store, and there is no content-hash dedup. |
| ✅ | Disk-full behaviour — shipped 21 Aug 2026, and it found mail being lost | Nothing owned it, and the audit of what actually happened on a full disk found silent loss: when local delivery's per-recipient file copy failed, the failure was logged at Medium and *nothing else happened* — the recipient row was deleted, the queue row was deleted, the metrics counted a delivery, and the sender kept their 250. That is fixed first and matters most: the sender now gets a DSN. Around it, the precondition and the alarm: `MinimumFreeDiskSpaceMB` (default 100, 0 = off) refuses MAIL FROM with **452 4.3.1** — temporary, because a permanent answer would destroy mail a five-minute cleanup would have delivered — IMAP APPEND with `NO [UNAVAILABLE]`, and — added later the same day — **pauses external account fetching entirely**, which is the one refusal that is free: the mail stays on the remote server, and the whole sweep is skipped rather than each account so nothing's next-try time is advanced and every pending account fetches the moment space returns; the reading is cached for five seconds so the check is not a syscall per message; and an hourly task warns in the application log at `DiskSpaceWarningThresholdMB` (default 1 GB), once per band transition rather than once per hour. The free-space call asks for bytes available *to this user*, which under a quota on the service account is the number that matters. **Upgrade note: an install already below 100 MB free starts refusing mail (temporarily) on upgrade** — deliberate, because a server that close to the wall is about to fail much worse, but it belongs in the release notes rather than in a surprise. |
| ⬜ | Attachment text extraction for search | The posting-list index itself shipped 21 Aug 2026 on schema 6023 (see the completed row above): `hm_messageindexterms` lets `SEARCH BODY` prove which messages *cannot* match before any file is read. What remains is **attachment text**: the index tokenises the message body and HTML body only, so a phrase that exists solely inside a PDF or an Office document is still invisible to search — and, because the index can only ever exclude, invisible in a way that is correct rather than wrong. (This row read "no posting-list table" until 21 Aug 2026.) |
| ⬜ | Message retention / auto-expiry policy | There is no "delete mail older than N days" for delivered mailbox mail — the age-based sweeps this server does have, for the archive (`ArchiveRetentionDays`) and the quarantine (`QuarantineRetentionDays`), deliberately touch neither the message store nor a mailbox. The scheduled-task list is greylisting cleanup, expired IP ranges, TLS-RPT, log retention, message-store consistency… |
| ✅ | Quota warning thresholds and per-account send quotas | Both halves shipped. `QuotaWarningPercent` (default **90**) warns a user as their mailbox fills, and warns on **crossing** the threshold rather than on being above it, so the notice arrives once instead of on every message for the rest of the mailbox's life — which is why it needs no stored state. Per-account outbound caps are `[SendingLimits]` messages and recipients per period, enforced by `RateLimiter` with per-account overrides; both default to 0, meaning unlimited, so the mechanism is inert until an administrator configures it. Per-IP and per-destination-domain rate limiting still exists alongside them. |
| ⬜ | Referential integrity in the schema | No FOREIGN KEY or ON DELETE CASCADE anywhere in any CreateTables script; all cascades are application-level loops in the Persistent* classes, so a crash mid-delete can leave orphans. **Measured 13 August 2026, and the finding was worse than "un-transactional": two of those loops did not exist.** Deleting a group left every one of its hm_group_members rows behind, and deleting or renaming a domain left every reference another domain held into it - both now fixed, see the rows above. **The stated blockers were re-measured on 13 August 2026 and one of them was wrong.** Blocker 1 stands and is the real one: `DatabaseConnectionManager::Execute` takes no connection parameter and calls `GetConnection_()`/`ReleaseConnection_()` around each statement, so a transaction opened by `BeginTransaction` runs on a different pooled connection from the statements it is meant to cover. Blocker 2 as previously written - "`SQLCEConnection::BeginTransaction` returns true without doing anything" - was only half right: it *did* issue `BeginTrans`, what it did not do was look at the outcome (now fixed - all three SQL CE transaction functions report failure instead of returning true unconditionally, which matters because DBUpdater turns a successful commit into the installer's exit code). SQL CE transactions do cover DML; what they cannot roll back is DDL, which commits as executed. A transaction around the domain-delete cascade would therefore **not** be inert on the default backend - that cascade is DML. What blocks it is blocker 1 alone, plus the message files already unlinked from disk, which no backend can roll back. |
| ⬜ | TLS to the database server | **Re-measured 13 August 2026; the facts were right and the conclusion was wrong for one of the three backends.** The PostgreSQL conninfo is still built from host/port/user/password/dbname only, with no sslmode - but libpq's default sslmode is `prefer`, so a PostgreSQL connection to a TLS-capable server is *already encrypted today*, and an administrator can already force `verify-full` by setting PGSSLMODE and PGSSLROOTCERT in the service process's environment. What is missing there is the ability to require and verify it from hMailServer's own configuration, not encryption itself. MySQL is the genuine gap: `MySQLConnection::Connect` sets only the plugin directory, never `mysql_options` TLS parameters, and never `MYSQL_READ_DEFAULT_GROUP`, so there is no config-file route either - those connections are plaintext. MSSQL can get encryption through the provider's own connection-string keywords. |
| ✅ | Tombstone table bounded — shipped 21 Aug 2026, fallback first | `hm_imapexpunged` grew for the life of the installation, but pruning it as the code stood would have been worse than the growth: the QRESYNC readers had no notion of "older than what I remember", so a pruned table would have handed a resynchronising client a **subset** and deleted mail would have sat in its cache forever. So RFC 7162 3.2.6 came first: a client whose mod-sequence predates the oldest surviving record is now given every UID in its requested range that the mailbox does not hold — a superset, which is what makes it safe, computed as the gaps between present UIDs so a sparse mailbox with a huge UIDNEXT costs nothing. Only then the bound: a per-mailbox record cap (`IMAPExpungeRetentionRecords`, default 5000, 0 = keep all), which is the unit RFC 7162 5.3 itself recommends — a resynchronising client asks in mod-sequences, not in days, and the table has no timestamp column to prune by anyway. Deletes are batched by mod-sequence range so the new statement timeout cannot abort one big DELETE forever, orphaned rows for deleted folders are swept at every start, and the newest record is never removed, because it is what keeps "nothing was ever expunged here" distinguishable from "I have forgotten". Still true: no primary key. |
| ✅ | Upgrade/migration documentation | Done: the docs index at [`hmailserver/docs/README.md`](hmailserver/docs/README.md), the unattended-install contract in the README, and [`hmailserver/docs/Upgrading.md`](hmailserver/docs/Upgrading.md) covering the 59-step schema chain from version 0 to 6008, what to back up and why, and the one genuine sharp edge — there is no downgrade path, the server refuses to start in *either* direction on a version mismatch, and rollback is therefore only as good as the snapshot taken first. |

### Routing, queue and delivery

24 shipped · 0 underway · 0 not started · 1 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | AWStats journal as the only delivery event stream | A tab-separated file journal writing time, sender, recipient, sender IP, recipient IP, SMTP code and byte count, called from the SMTP rejection paths, LocalDelivery, ExternalDelivery and SMTPDeliverer… |
| ✅ | Bounce / NDR generation | Generates a mailer-daemon message from the SEND_FAILED_NOTIFICATION template with %MACRO_SENT%/%MACRO_SUBJECT%/%MACRO_RECIPIENTS%/%MACRO_ORIGINAL_HEADER% substitution, marks it auto-generated, counts it in ServerStatus… |
| ✅ | Bounce-loop and backscatter suppression | Never bounces a mailer-daemon sender or a null sender, honours the Auto-Submitted header and a rule-loop counter, and increments the loop count on the generated NDR. |
| ✅ | Delivery worker pool and outbound source IP | Configurable delivery thread count, and SMTPDeliveryBindToIP pins the outbound socket to a chosen local address (needed for PTR/SPF-correct multi-IP hosts). Recipients per outbound transaction are capped by MaxSMTPRecipientsInBatch. |
| ✅ | End-of-data finalization deadline | If the accept/save/queue work after the final dot (or last BDAT chunk) exceeds [Settings] FinalizationTimeout, the message is discarded and a temporary 451 is returned rather than letting the relaying MTA time out and duplicate - the f… |
| ✅ | External POP3 account fetcher | Scheduled download from remote POP3 accounts on behalf of local accounts, with per-account UID tracking, a delete-after-days policy, MaxNumberOfExternalFetchThreads, and an OnExternalAccountDownload script hook. **Hardened against the remote server on 13 August 2026, because this is the one subsystem in which hMailServer is the client of a machine the administrator does not run — and it trusted that machine.** Six defects, five of them losing or duplicating mail. A `-ERR` to `RETR` abandoned the entire session: `uidlresponse_` is a map keyed by message number, so the mailbox is walked in ascending order, and one message the server listed and then would not hand over — deleted by another client between UIDL and RETR, or simply unreadable — stopped every message behind it from being collected, on that check and on every check afterwards, with nothing written to any log. A UIDL line with no space in it was not rejected but **guessed at**: `Find` returned -1, `Mid(0, -1)` range-checked itself to an empty message number, `_ttoi` made that 0, and the junk line became the unique-id of "message 0" — the lowest key in the map, hence the first thing requested, so one malformed line plus the previous defect meant nothing was ever collected from that account again. A unique-id longer than the 255-character `uidvalue` column could not be recorded at all, so the message was re-downloaded and re-delivered **on every single check, for ever**; ids that do not fit are now folded to a shorter form that still depends on every character of the original. A download cut off mid-message left its partly written spool file in the data directory permanently, with no database row referring to it, one per interrupted fetch. `MessageCleanup_` dereferenced the result of `GetUID` without checking it — an access violation, so the whole service — reachable from a server that gives one unique-id to two message numbers once the retention window has passed. And the UIDL listing was accumulated in memory with no ceiling whatsoever, so a server that never sent the terminating dot could exhaust the process; it is now bounded at 8 MB, about a hundred thousand entries at the RFC 1939 maximum id length, and the fetch is abandoned with a log line that names the ceiling rather than failing silently. **The duplicate-delivery fix is the one with teeth.** The local record of a downloaded message was deliberately *not* written when the account was set to delete immediately — why write a row that is about to be removed? — and it was thrown away the instant `DELE` was handed to the socket, with the response never examined at all. So anything interrupting the session between the delivery and the confirmed deletion (the server dropping the connection during cleanup, a service restart, a `DELE` the server refused) left the message on the server with no local trace that it had already been delivered, and the next check delivered it a second time. The row is now written for every downloaded message and removed only on a `+OK` `DELE`, with `DeleteUIDsNoLongerOnServer_` pruning strays, so the table still does not grow. One consequence had to be handled rather than discovered later: with rows now written in that configuration, a server that derives unique-ids from message content would have had the second of two identical messages silently dropped, so an id repeated within the *same* listing is treated as a second message — but only when messages are being deleted, because where they are left on the server the listing repeats every session and doing it there would deliver an extra copy on every check. Seven fixtures in `ExternalAccounts/HostileRemoteServer.cs` drive a purpose-built misbehaving POP3 server, and each one fails against the previous binary. Still open, and the reason this row is not a fresh claim of completeness: the fetcher applies **no size limit** to a downloaded message, so a remote server can still write an unbounded file into the data directory. `SetMaxSizeKB` deliberately was not wired in — `SaveToFile_` stops writing past the limit without setting `write_failed_`, so using it would deliver a truncated message as complete, which is the failure shape the ignored-return-values row calls the most serious class there is. **And one more, found on 13 August 2026 from the TCP side and deliberately not patched in a hurry:** `ExternalFetch::Start` runs on the ExternalFetchManager work queue and ends with an UNBOUNDED `disconnectEvent->Wait()`, set only by `~TCPConnection`. `POP3ClientConnection` sets an idle timeout and no absolute ceiling, so a remote server that sends one byte before each expiry - broken, hostile, or merely very slow - holds that fetch thread for as long as it likes; enough accounts in that state and external fetching stops for every account. This project already knows that an idle timeout is not a ceiling because it re-arms on every byte. The reason it is recorded rather than fixed is that the obvious fix is wrong: `Start` drops its reference to the connection before waiting, so simply timing out would return while the session is still running and let the next cycle fetch the same account concurrently - which is precisely the duplicate-delivery shape the rest of this row is about. It needs the timeout to disconnect the connection it is abandoning, which means keeping a weak reference to it, and a test that a fetch which overruns cannot be started twice. `Event::WaitFor` now returns whether it was signalled or timed out, so the ceiling is at least expressible. |
| ✅ | Four list modes | Public (anyone may post), Membership (members only), Announcement (one nominated owner address only) and DomainMembers (any sender in the list's own domain). |
| ✅ | Global SMTP relayer (smart host) | Fallback relayer with port, optional AUTH credentials and its own connection-security setting; deliveries to identical target/port/credentials are merged into a single connection. |
| ✅ | Incoming relays (trusted forwarders) | Configured relay IPs get spam scoring deferred to post-transmission rather than pre-transmission rejection, so a trusted forwarder's connection is not judged on its own IP. |
| ✅ | Message-trace table (`hm_messagetrace`) | Shipped on schema 6020, and named `hm_messagetrace` rather than the `hm_message_events` this row was designed under. Append-only, keyed on the queue id rather than the RFC Message-ID, and **off by default** — a durable record of who corresponds with whom is a privacy decision before it is a diagnostic one. See [Queryable message trace](#observability-and-diagnostics) for what that key does and does not buy. |
| ✅ | MX resolution and host-attempt limits | Manual MX resolution with preference ordering; MaxNumberOfMXHosts truncates the candidate list, and MXTriesFactor limits hosts attempted per retry to (retries+1)*factor. A fixed relay host may carry several pipe-separated hostnames. |
| ✅ | Per-domain outbound relay | Shipped 20 August 2026 on schema 6021, answering the oldest open feature request on the tracker: a domain can name its own smart host, with port, credentials and connection security, and everything that domain sends goes through it. Six properties on the domain object, the password stored DPAPI-protected like every other credential this server has to present rather than verify. It sits between the route and the global relayer in the resolution order — see the row on that order for why that is the only place it can go.  |
| ✅ | Per-route address allow-list | A route either applies to all addresses in the domain (routealladdresses) or only to the explicit addresses in hm_routeaddresses; a non-listed address at a routed external domain is rejected with "Recipient not in route list." |
| ✅ | Recursive member permission and expansion checks | Before accepting, the server recursively checks the sender may reach every member (lists inside lists included); expansion and permission recursion are both capped at 25 levels. |
| ✅ | Relay controls by IP range | Per-range option bits for allow-SMTP, the four relay permissions (local/remote to local/remote), the four require-SMTP-AUTH permissions, spam-protection and virus-protection opt-out, require-TLS-for-AUTH… |
| ✅ | Retry schedule | Fixed retry count and fixed minutes-between-tries (global, or per-route override taking the maximum across matching routes), plus optional QuickRetries/QuickRetriesMinutes for the first N attempts (greylisting recovery) and QueueRandom… |
| ✅ | Route and fetch-account passwords stored DPAPI-protected | routeauthenticationpassword and fapassword are written through Crypt::ProtectSecret, producing a machine-bound `DPAPI:` envelope by default with a Blowfish fallback; legacy Blowfish values are still read transparently… |
| ✅ | Route target selection order | A rule-forced route id wins outright; otherwise recipients are grouped per domain and each domain is resolved to a route, then to the **sending** domain's own relay, then to the global SMTP relayer, then to MX — route first because it is about where the mail is going, the sending domain's relay next because it is about where the mail is from, the global relayer last because it is the fallback for mail nothing else has an opinion about… |
| ✅ | Routes (per-domain smart host) | Wildcard-matched per-domain route with target host, port, credentials, connection security, retry count, retry interval, to-all-addresses or an explicit address list, and treat-sender/recipient-as-local flags… |
| ✅ | Routes with wildcard domain matching | hm_routes stores domain pattern, target host/port, retry count and interval, connection security, optional relay authentication, and two "treat as local" flags (recipient and sender)… |
| ✅ | Sender restrictions enforced at RCPT TO | UserCanSendToList_ applies the require-SMTP-authentication flag first, then the mode check, with distinct rejection texts ("SMTP authentication required.", "Not authorized owner.", "Not authorized domain.", "Not authorized sender.")… |
| ✅ | Distribution-list moderation and per-list bounce handling — shipped 21 Aug 2026 on schema 6025 (self-subscribe deliberately not included: it needs a confirmation round-trip and a token store, and is its own item) | A list can now have a **moderator**: a sender the list's mode refuses is forwarded there — labelled `X-hMailServer-Moderation`, with no `List-*` headers — instead of being bounced, and the moderator approves by **resending the message to the list from an authenticated session**. No token store and no magic approval address: the existing mode machinery was 90% of the mechanism, and the bypass demands authentication unconditionally, whatever the list's own RequireAuth says. A forged approval from an unauthenticated session does not just fail — it lands, labelled, in front of the real moderator, which is the test that matters most. The honest limitation is stated rather than hidden: this fork does not bind MAIL FROM to the authenticated account anywhere, so any valid credential holder could forge an approval — the same trust model the existing require-auth machinery has always had. **Per-list bounce handling** fixes a real operational bug: distributed copies carried the original poster's envelope sender (verified in the expansion path — which lives in `RecipientParser`, not the deliverer), so a dead subscriber's bounces hammered whoever posted last. A list's bounce address now becomes the envelope sender of every copy it sends, which also makes receivers evaluate SPF against the list's domain instead of failing on the author's. Guards refuse a moderator or bounce address equal to the list itself (HM6362/6363), because that is a mail loop with a config page. |
| ✅ | RFC 2369 / RFC 2919 List-* headers | Done — `DistributionListSender::AddListHeaders`, called from the SMTP accept path for postings to a local distribution list: List-Id (RFC 2919, derived from the list address), List-Post, List-Help, List-Owner, and a List-Unsubscribe whose mailto points at the list's owner address with the list named in the subject — an address a person actually reads, because there is no automated unsubscribe workflow to point at (see the moderation row). Headers a message already carries are never duplicated; a posting arriving with its own List-Id is left untouched per RFC 2919's one-List-Id rule. |
| ⏸️ | RFC 8058 one-click unsubscribe (List-Unsubscribe-Post) | Deferred with a measured reason, 2026-08-15: one-click requires the HTTPS URI in List-Unsubscribe to identify the individual member (the mail client POSTs to it blindly, carrying no identity), which means a per-member token in the header — and this server stores ONE message file shared by every recipient (single-instance storage), so per-member headers are impossible without per-member message copies. Revisit together with the list moderation/self-subscribe row, which needs per-member processing anyway. The infrastructure that is NOT the blocker already exists: WebServicesServer routes HTTPS requests and could serve the POST endpoint tomorrow. |
| ✅ | RFC 3464 machine-readable DSN format — shipped 21 Aug 2026 | Every bounce this server had ever sent was a paragraph of English: no list manager could tell a dead address from a full mailbox, and monitoring could not classify a failure at all. The hard part was not the MIME — it was that the failure had been flattened into prose by the time the bounce was assembled, so the error vector now carries a `DeliveryFailure` (recipient, RFC 3463 enhanced status, prose) whose constructor demands all three and has no default — a call site that forgets is a compile error, not a bounce claiming 5.0.0, and the first thing that trap caught was a call site written the same day. The report is `multipart/report; report-type=delivery-status`: the same human-readable text first and unchanged, so a client that has never heard of RFC 3464 shows what it always showed; then per-recipient `message/delivery-status`; then the original headers. The distinctions carry the value: over-quota is **5.2.2** (the address is good — a list must not strike it), a local write failure is **class 4** (the recipient's mailbox was never the problem), a Sieve reject is **5.7.1** per RFC 5429, and a remote refusal uses the remote's own enhanced code when it sent one — taken from the last line of a multi-line reply, and only when its class agrees with the reply code, because "550 5 users were unknown" is prose that begins with a digit. `Diagnostic-Code:` and `Remote-MTA:` are filled only from what a remote server actually said and who said it; an omitted field is correct where an invented one is not. No off switch, deliberately: the old form was the compatibility problem. |

### Administration, API and Control Panel

65 shipped · 0 underway · 4 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Every setting an administrator needs is reachable from the Control Panel | A program-wide audit against the GUI rule found roughly eighteen capabilities with no GUI at all. The worst were not obscure: **directory authentication (LDAP)** was a README headline capability configurable only by hand-editing an INI section documented nowhere else, with no COM either; **scheduled backups** were invisible, so an administrator who set a destination and used the manual button reasonably believed backups were running while no scheduled task was ever constructed; and **group membership** could not be edited at all, so a group could be created and granted rights on a public folder while applying to nobody. Also closed: the SSL certificate passphrase (encrypted keys were advertised in README with no editor), metrics credentials and TLS (the GUI could move the bind off loopback, which makes `/metrics` answer 503, but not set the credential that fixes it), per-account sending limits, the IMAP search ceilings the README tells owners of large mailboxes to raise, the remaining timeout and queue bounds, and the CalDAV/CardDAV discovery redirects - implemented and served, and documented nowhere. **The tail, closed 14 August 2026.** The **Windows service account**, which is the largest single reduction available in what a compromise of this server is worth and which had no GUI - and is not a setting the running service reads at all, since `ServiceManager::RegisterService` consumes it once at registration, so the card reads the Service Control Manager, says which account the service is *actually* running as, and prints the exact elevated command that would apply the one being asked for. The **external-account connection security**, which was a checkbox over a four-valued setting: `get_UseSSL` is `ConnectionSecurity == CSSSL` and `put_UseSSL` writes `CSSSL` or `CSNone`, so an account set to STARTTLS read back as unticked and saving rewrote it to implicit TLS - which on port 110 does not connect, making STARTTLS unreachable from the GUI for the providers that require it. The three **`[Database]` tuning values**, which needed a section-aware INI row first, because writing a `[Database]` key through the existing one would have put it in `[Settings]` where the server never looks: saved, correct on reload, and inert. And three **actions that existed over COM and nowhere else** - run the message indexer, discard and rebuild the index, and recalculate the IMAP folder UID counters, which is the repair for a folder that hands the same UID out twice and makes a client show one message in place of another. |
| ✅ | The GUI says when a setting is doing nothing | Eleven configurations look enabled and are inert, and the guards now compute and show each one. The worst is OAuth2 enabled with no usable key material: the mechanisms are still advertised to clients, every token fails, the failure is not logged server-side on SMTP, and failed bearer authentications feed auto-ban - so a correctly-configured client gets its IP banned by a half-configured server. Also `SRSEnabled` with an empty secret, which does nothing *and* suppresses the envelope-rewrite fallback, making it strictly worse than off; MTA-STS hosting and autoconfiguration enabled while both web ports are zero, which is the shipped state; and ARC sealing with no domain DKIM key. The cache-size editors were the same defect inside this GUI - runtime-only values with no storage behind them, presented as ordinary settings - and now say so, following the treatment `WorkerThreadPriority` already had. **A twelfth, found while exposing the service account and worth naming because the wording was ours:** the "Stored secret protection" card claimed that "sensitive values in hMailServer.INI (database password, OAuth/SRS/BATV secrets, password pepper) are encrypted with Windows DPAPI on the next service start". That was wrong three times over. Nothing rewrites hMailServer.INI at start-up; `Crypt::ProtectSecret` is called only from `Property::Save`, `PersistentRoute`, `PersistentFetchAccount` and `PersistentSSLCertificate`, all of which write to the **database**; and `OAuth2HmacSecret`, `SRSSecret`, `BATVSecret` and `PasswordPepper` are each read with a plain `ReadIniSettingString_`, so the switch has never touched one of them. The card now names the five things it does cover, says they are re-enveloped when next saved rather than at start-up, and names the secrets that are stored exactly as typed - which is also the honest argument for the password-less virtual account. **A thirteenth, of a different shape, found on 15 August 2026 while scoping the directory work: a setting whose effect is invisible rather than absent.** An account's Directory tab was a checkbox reading "This account is linked to Active Directory" and two unexplained boxes. An **empty** domain there is not "not configured" - `SSPIValidation` treats empty, `.` and the computer's own name alike as "validate a LOCAL Windows account", which is a legitimate and useful way to run the server for anyone who has no Active Directory at all, and was completely undiscoverable from an interface that says Active Directory in every label. The tab now says which of the two things the values actually do, as a sentence that updates as the domain is typed, and the checkbox says "Active Directory, or a local Windows account". It also states the rule that decides whether the other case can work at all: a real domain needs the SERVER's own computer to be domain-joined, because `LogonUser` does, and from an unjoined host every attempt fails as though the password were wrong - including a misspelt domain name - so such a mailbox can never log in and never says why. Where that bites, the tab points at LDAP directory authentication, which binds over the network and needs no domain join. The server's join state is deliberately NOT asserted from the Control Panel: it is the server's host that must be joined, and the Control Panel may be running somewhere else - the same distinction the LDAP test card is careful to disclose about itself. |
| ✅ | Certificate state is visible | The certificates page showed file paths and nothing else. It now shows expiry and days remaining (already exported as a metric and displayed nowhere), whether the certificate and private key actually match, and whether a selected key file is encrypted while no passphrase is set - which would stop the listener using it from starting at all. On a remote Control Panel session, where the files cannot be read from this machine, it says so rather than showing a false green. |
| ✅ | Guided DKIM key rotation in the Control Panel | Rotation was COM-only and is the operation most easily got wrong, because promoting before the new selector has propagated signs mail with a key the world cannot look up. The domain dialog now walks it: stage a new selector, generate the key, copy the exact DNS host and TXT value separately, check whether the record is actually published, and promote - with Promote disabled until that check passes in this session, and a prior session's pass deliberately not counting. The check distinguishes three outcomes that need three different responses: not published yet (propagation takes minutes to hours), published but not matching (waiting will not fix it), and the lookup itself failing (which says nothing about the record). It bypasses the resolver cache, or an administrator who has just published would be told it is missing for as long as the negative cache lasts. |
| ✅ | "What still needs doing outside hMailServer" | A checklist of every prerequisite the server cannot satisfy for itself - DNS records, CA bundles, trusted-sealer lists, key passphrases, reachability - each with a live status where one can be determined rather than a description. Eleven items, several found only while verifying the others: ARC sealing silently produces nothing with no domain DKIM key, OAuth2 with RS256 allowed and no public key file fails every token, and client autoconfiguration ships enabled with both web ports at zero. Statuses are Done / Not needed / Action needed / Cannot tell, and "cannot tell" is used honestly rather than guessed - a page whose purpose is to say what is really configured must not overclaim. |
| ✅ | Per-port client certificates and ARC filtering in the Control Panel | Both shipped COM-only and were unreachable from the admin UI. Inbound mutual TLS is now a port setting with the three refused combinations shown as inline validation before Save rather than as a COM error after it - including that Require on a STARTTLS-optional port is a lock on an open door. ARC inbound filtering needed COM accessors first (`AntiSpam.ArcFilteringEnabled` / `ArcTrustedSealers`), because an editor that can write but not read back would misreport its own state on load, which is the exact defect this fork keeps finding. |
| ✅ | **The 126 `[Settings]` INI values move into the database** | Shipped 14 August 2026 — with one deliberate departure from the design below, which is worth reading first because it is the whole difference between this working and this being the next silent-ignore defect. **The plan said the database overlay wins and a stale INI key is reported by name. Building it made clear that is the wrong way round.** Twelve Control Panel write sites, `hmconfig.ps1`, `WebServicesServer`'s two DAV redirects, `UseLanguage` and an administrator with a text editor all write or read hMailServer.INI *directly*; under "the database wins", every one of those writes would appear to succeed, read back correctly, and be discarded on the next restart. Reporting it by name makes the defect legible, not absent. So `IniSettingStore` is a **three-way merge in which the file wins**, and each row remembers what the file said when the two last agreed (`inisettingfilevalue`): file changed → the file wins and the row follows; row changed alone → the row wins and is **written back into the file**, so everything that reads the file directly keeps working and `/Register` — which runs with no database open at all — still sees it; both changed → the file wins and the key is named in an error rather than resolved in silence. `filevalue` advances only when the file write actually succeeded, so a service account without write access to the ini retries on the next start instead of recording a lie. **The rest of the design held.** New table `hm_inisettings` at schema 6011 across all four dialects, for the `nvarchar(30)`/unique-index reason given below; `LoadSettings()` stays INI-only for the bootstrap and is called a second time immediately after the schema gate, so a pre-6011 database gives "run DBUpdater" rather than a SQL error; the overlay applies to `[Settings]` only, so the keys needed to reach the database can never be overridden from it. `Configuration::XMLStore`/`XMLLoad` carry the table, which closes the backup gap named in the backup row above — the name goes in an *attribute*, because XMLite writes element names raw and these names come from a file an administrator edits. **One arm of the merge was wrong on the first attempt and the suite proved it in the loudest way available:** a row whose key had gone from the file was resurrected unconditionally, on the reasoning that the row was the last copy. A test that enabled ACME, restarted, then cleaned up by *deleting* its keys had them written straight back — so `AcmeEnabled=1` survived into every following test and **394 of 1445 failed**. Removing a key is a normal way to return a setting to its default, and `filevalue` is exactly what distinguishes that from a database-side addition. Covered by `Infrastructure/IniSettingsMirror.cs`, one test per arm, each asserting both what the file now says and which arm the server logged taking; the removal test fails against the resurrecting build. **Phase 2 shipped the same day.** `Settings.GetIniSetting`, `SetIniSetting`, `DeleteIniSetting` and `IniSettingNames` put the `[Settings]` section on the COM API, so a Control Panel connected to another machine can administer it — the half that makes these settings *administrable* rather than merely backed up — and `IniSettingNames` closes the configuration-as-code gap, since it is what lets anything enumerate them. Server-administrator only, inherited from `InterfaceSettings` as a whole: the section holds `OAuth2HmacSecret`, `SRSSecret`, `BATVSecret` and `PasswordPepper`, which is the same treatment `Account.Password`, `FetchAccount.Password` and `SSLCertificate.PrivateKeyPassword` already get. Four decisions are worth recording, because each is a way this could have been quietly wrong. **The file is written first and the row recorded as agreed only if that write succeeded** — the reverse order, on a service account that cannot write the ini, produces a stored value every direct reader disagrees with and no way to tell which copy is right. **A name or value that could not survive a round trip is refused rather than written**: `=`, `[`, `]`, a line break, surrounding whitespace, over 100 characters, or a value over 4000 — each of which comes back as a *different* key, or as two settings. **A write does not take effect until a restart**, and says so: `LoadSettings()` latches these into ~150 typed members at start-up, and re-running that underneath live sessions to make one value live would be a poor trade — it is also the contract editing the file by hand has always had. And **`[Database]` and `[Directories]` are deliberately not reachable this way**, because they are the bootstrap that says where the database *is*, and a server whose database location can be changed through the database it is currently using can be pointed elsewhere by anyone who reaches the one it is on. On the client side `IniFeatureStore` prefers the file when it is on the same machine — that is the copy the server actually runs on — and falls back to COM otherwise; the COM route is *injected* rather than called directly, because that file compiles into ControlPanel.Tests and reaching `ServerSession` from it would drag COM and `System.ServiceProcess` into the test assembly behind it. Covered by `Infrastructure/IniSettingsOverCom.cs`, which pins the four things that make it safe to hand an administrator rather than merely "a value round-trips": that a write reaches the **file** and not only the mirror, that deleting is not the same as writing empty, that unstorable names are refused, and that the enumeration is the file's own contents. **The costing this was built from, unchanged, because the parts of it that held are the reasons the table looks the way it does:** Of 149 INI reads, **126 are in `[Settings]` and should move**; 12 `[Database]`, 6 `[Directories]` and `AdministratorPassword` **cannot** — you need them to reach the database, `LogFolder` is where the "the database is down" message goes, and an administrator password held in the database locks you out of the tool you would use to diagnose a database outage. The two fault-injection switches stay INI-only deliberately. **The reason is not tidiness.** An INI setting is invisible to remote administration: `IniFeatureStore` reads a file that only exists on the server, which is why the Control Panel's own log-folder card has to say "not readable from this machine", and none of the 126 appear in `hmconfig.ps1`'s config-as-code. They are also **absent from every backup** (see the backup row above). One change closes all three. **Design.** `LoadSettings()` stays INI-only so the bootstrap still works, and is called a second time after `OpenDatabase`; `ReadIniSettingString_/Integer_` prefer a database overlay **only for the `[Settings]` section**, so the keys needed to reach the database can never be overridden from it — which is the security answer as well as the ordering one. No per-setting registry is needed because the existing loader is simply re-run. **A new table rather than widening `hm_settings`:** `settingname` is `nvarchar(30)`, five current names are 31–40 characters (`GreylistingEnabledDuringRecordExpiration` is 40), and the column is uniquely indexed on all four backends — so widening means dropping and recreating a unique constraint on **SQL CE, which commits DDL as it executes and cannot roll back**. `CREATE TABLE` is safe everywhere and needs no index surgery; the cost is that the backup wiring becomes ~20 explicit lines instead of free, which is the better trade. **Upgrade path**, which is the part that matters for existing installations: on first start, any `[Settings]` key present in the INI with no row in the database is copied in — idempotent, driven by "is there a row" rather than a version flag, so it is safe to interrupt and safe to re-run. Nothing is deleted from the INI, because deleting an operator's configuration during an upgrade is how people stop trusting upgrades. But a stale INI key that differs from the stored value is **reported by name on every start** — without that, somebody edits the INI, nothing happens, and there is no way to find out why, which is precisely the silent-ignore failure the August sweeps spent three passes removing. |
| ✅ | ACME http-01 challenge serving (/.well-known/acme-challenge/) | Serves key authorizations from the in-process AcmeChallengeStore; rejects tokens containing a slash or longer than 256 characters. Always enabled on the web-services listener (not gated behind a setting). |
| ✅ | Admin helper services | Active Directory account picker, DKIM RSA key-pair generator producing PEM + the DNS TXT p= value, password generator and strength meter, protected-secret storage, message-store consistency report parser. |
| ✅ | Advertised authentication method | Autoconfig hard-codes <authentication>password-cleartext</authentication> for every server block; there is no OAuth2 authentication element even though the server has an OAuth2 token validator… |
| ✅ | Authentication: administrator password or a scoped API key | HTTP Basic with username "administrator" against the hMailServer.ini hash, or `Authorization: Bearer hmapi_<64 hex>` against `hMailServerApiKeys.ini`. A key has a label, an expiry, an optional source restriction, a scope (`readonly`\|`full`, readonly by default) and an optional domain list; the store is read on every request, so creating or revoking one takes effect immediately with no restart. The administrator password is still replayed on every request that uses it — a key exists so that scripts do not have to. |
| ✅ | Browser admin SPA served from the REST listener | Single self-contained HTML/CSS/JS page served unauthenticated at / and /index.html (it is only the login shell); every data call underneath uses the authenticated /api/v1 routes. Installed to {app}\WebAdmin by the installer. |
| ✅ | COM / IDispatch administration API | 95 Interface* implementation files covering every configuration object (accounts, domains, aliases, lists, rules, routes, IP ranges, ports, certificates, backup, status, utilities). It is the seam every tool and third-party script uses. Audited mechanically in August 2026 rather than by eye: every `propget`/`propput`/method the IDL declares across all 92 `IInterface*` interfaces has an implementation, so there is no declared-but-missing member. What the audit did find was four getters that returned S_OK without writing their out parameter, and two setters that stored a value they had never checked - [see the defects list](#defects-found-by-the-audit) |
| ✅ | COM automation surface size | 92 dual/IDispatch interfaces with 92 matching coclasses, and 86 forward declarations at the head of the file — three different numbers, and treating any one as the others is how this row went stale twice. Entry point is IInterfaceApplication with 20 members including Start/Stop, Settings, Domains, Database, Utilities, Status… |
| ✅ | Control Deck views | Four views only: Dashboard (animated KPI tiles + live session counts, 3 s poll), Domains (with account drill-down, create and delete), Delivery queue (retry / delete per message), DANE/TLSA (copy-all record block). |
| ✅ | Control Panel (WPF, .NET 8) | The sole GUI since the classic Administrator was removed in 6.2.10: ~30 views covering domains, accounts, routes, rules, IP ranges, ports, SSL certificates, server and feature settings, queue, logs, status, backup… |
| ✅ | Ctrl+K command palette over pages and individual settings | Fuzzy palette searches both navigation leaves and individual settings; picking a setting opens the page hosting it. The settings index is generated by `build/generate-settings-index.ps1`, and CI now regenerates it and fails if the working tree moves, so it can no longer drift from the settings it indexes. **But see [findability](#control-panel-findability-and-the-ctrlk-problem): the palette exists because the navigation does not make things findable, and that is the actual defect.** |
| ✅ | Data-directory diagnostic | A built-in diagnostic verifies the data directory is reachable and writable, alongside the backup-directory test, in the Control Panel "Run diagnostics" set. |
| ✅ | DataDirectorySynchronizer | Wizard (Welcome → synchronisation mode → select domain → progress) that reconciles the on-disk data directory with the database, per-domain or wholesale. |
| ✅ | DELETE /api/v1/accounts/<address> | Deletes an account by address; 404 when absent. No bulk delete, no soft delete. |
| ✅ | DELETE /api/v1/queue/&lt;id&gt; | Removes a message from the delivery queue, and answers 404 for an id that does not exist rather than reporting success. The same change closed a mail-destruction path: the old handler could match and delete a *mailbox* message rather than a queued one. [See defects](#defects-found-by-the-audit). |
| ✅ | Delivery queue management | Grid of id/created/from/recipients/next try/tries/file with view-raw-message, retry-now (ResetDeliveryTime + StartDelivery, 64-bit ids) and remove. |
| ✅ | Endpoint selection from real configuration | Both autoconfig and Autodiscover pick the best advertised port per protocol from the configured TCPIPPorts by ranking implicit TLS above STARTTLS-required (with 587 preferred) above STARTTLS-optional above plain — so clients are told w… |
| ✅ | Failed-auth accounting and a per-credential request budget | A rejected credential is logged and fed to `AccountLogon::RegisterFailedLogin`, the same accounting the mail ports use. Because security ranges are only consulted through `TCPConnection`, the ban that creates would be inert on this listener, so the address is also recorded in an in-process, bounded, self-expiring refused set that Run_ checks before the request is read, before the TLS handshake and before anything touches the database — which is also what stops a credential flood growing `hm_securityranges` at a third of the request rate. Loopback is excluded on purpose: a ban covering 127.0.0.1 would deny the server's own local clients. Successful requests are bounded separately, per credential (not per source address), at 200 in a fixed 10-second window, answered 429 with `Retry-After`. |
| ✅ | Folder UID recalculation maintenance operation | A single maintenance operation, RecalculateFolderUID, resets each folder's foldercurrentuid to MAX(messageuid) where it has fallen behind — the repair for the "message has no UID" condition the reader reports as error 5025. |
| ✅ | GET /api/v1/domains | Array of {name, active} only — no id, quota, alias or account counts. |
| ✅ | GET /api/v1/domains/<name>/accounts | Array of {address, active}. No paging, no quota/size, no forwarding data. |
| ✅ | GET /api/v1/queue | Delivery-queue listing {id, created, from, recipients, next_try, locked, tries} reusing the same query behind COM Status.UndeliveredMessages. No filtering or paging; whole queue on every call. |
| ✅ | GET /api/v1/status | JSON: version, numeric server state, processedMessages, spamMessages, virusesRemoved, and SMTP/IMAP/POP3 session counts. No uptime, no queue depth. |
| ✅ | GET /api/v1/tlsa | Emits publish-ready DANE TLSA 3 1 1 (DANE-EE / SPKI / SHA-256) records for every configured SSL certificate, falling back to the ACME fullchain.pem. Hard-codes the _25._tcp prefix, so submission/IMAPS records are not generated. |
| ✅ | Graceful fallback when the page is not installed | If WebAdmin\index.html is missing the listener serves a small built-in HTML notice pointing at /api/v1/ rather than 404ing. |
| ✅ | HTTPS REST listener | Self-contained OpenSSL listener; refuses to start unless the administrator password is set, and refuses plaintext unless bound to 127.0.0.1; TLS 1.2 floor; falls back to the ACME cert when none configured… |
| ✅ | IInterfaceCache — cache observability | Per-collection (domain / account / alias / distribution list) hit rate, TTL, max size and current size in KB, plus Clear(). This is the only per-subsystem statistics surface in COM. |
| ✅ | IInterfaceDiagnostics / DiagnosticResults / DiagnosticResult | PerformTests() plus LocalDomainName/TestDomainName inputs; results expose Name, Description, ExecutionDetails and a boolean Result. Every accessor is gated on GetIsServerAdmin(). |
| ✅ | IInterfaceLogging | 23 members: per-category toggles (SMTP/IMAP/POP3/TCPIP/application/debug), live-log enable + LiveLog read, log directory, resolved paths for the current event/error/awstats/default logs, AWStats toggle, KeepFilesOpen… |
| ✅ | IInterfaceMessageIndexing | TotalMessageCount, TotalIndexedCount, Enabled, Clear() and Index() — lets an operator drive and observe the message index from script. |
| ✅ | ImportTool — mbox and text import | Chooser offering two importers: an mbox importer with a streaming parser that handles classic and Thunderbird "From - <date>" envelopes, LF and CRLF, mboxrd >From unquoting and CRLF normalisation without dot-stuffing… |
| ✅ | INI-backed feature settings pages | Seven FeatureSettingsView sections (Security, Automation/ACME, Integration/API & monitoring, Hardening/Advanced INI, Authentication, DNS, Web services) edit hMailServer.ini keys directly through IniFeatureStore, including RestApi*… |
| ✅ | IStatus exposes seven members | UndeliveredMessages (tab-separated blob), StartTime, ProcessedMessages, RemovedViruses, RemovedSpamMessages, SessionCount(eSessionType) and ThreadID. The delivered/deferred/bounced, auth-success/failure, TLS-handshake… |
| ✅ | Live dashboard with charts | Throughput (msgs/min, derived from the processed counter delta) and per-protocol session lines, plus KPI tiles for processed, queue length, spam, viruses, uptime. History is 90 samples at a 2 s poll — about three minutes, in RAM… |
| ✅ | Live log viewer | Tails the current log file from disk on a 750 ms timer with per-category colouring (SMTP/IMAP/POP3/error/application), a 2000-line ring buffer and a pause control. Reads files directly rather than using the COM live-log stream. |
| ✅ | MTA-STS policy hosting (/.well-known/mta-sts.txt) | Serves STSv1 policies only from a mta-sts.<domain> Host for domains hosted and active here. mx: lines come from an explicit MtaStsPolicyMx override or from the domain's live MX records (1 h cache, 512-entry cap)… |
| ✅ | Navigation tree / feature areas | Welcome, Dashboard; Status group (Server status, Delivery queue, Live logs); Domains; Rules; Settings group with Protocols, Delivery, Routes, Public folders, Anti-spam (settings + SURBL + DNSBL + white list + greylist white list)… |
| ✅ | Outlook Autodiscover — POX only | /autodiscover/autodiscover.xml answered for POST and GET, parsing <EMailAddress> out of the POX request and returning the outlook/responseschema/2006a Account block with IMAP/POP3/SMTP Protocol entries (SSL on/off, Encryption TLS/SSL… |
| ✅ | POST /api/v1/domains/<name>/accounts | Creates an account from {address, password}; validates the address belongs to the domain, 409 on duplicate, hashes with PreferredHashAlgorithm, returns 201. No way to set quota, display name or active flag. |
| ✅ | POST /api/v1/queue/<id>/retry | Resets the delivery time and kicks the delivery thread; ids parsed strictly numeric, max 18 digits. |
| ✅ | Public web-services listener | Separate HTTP and HTTPS listeners (own threads) hosting ACME challenges, MTA-STS policy and autoconfiguration. Falls back to the ACME fullchain.pem/privkey.pem when no certificate is configured… |
| ✅ | Remote (DCOM) administration | The Control Panel activates hMailServer.Application by ProgID against a named host, so a remote server can be administered over DCOM; authentication is Application.Authenticate(user, password). |
| ✅ | Request handling limits | Single accept thread handling clients serially; HTTP/1.0 with Connection: close; 64 KB max request; 10 s per-socket and 30 s total read deadline to stop a slow-dribble client pinning the one worker. |
| ✅ | REST admin API covers domains, accounts, the queue and its own keys | GET /api/v1/domains, GET\|POST /api/v1/domains/<name>/accounts, DELETE /api/v1/accounts/<address>, GET /api/v1/queue, POST /api/v1/queue/<id>/retry, DELETE /api/v1/queue/<id>, GET\|POST /api/v1/apikeys, DELETE /api/v1/apikeys/<id>, plus /status and /tlsa. No aliases, distribution lists… |
| ✅ | REST administration API | Raw-socket HTTP(S) listener serving /api/v1/status, /domains, /domains/<name>/accounts, /accounts/<address>, /queue (+retry/delete), /apikeys (+revoke) and /tlsa. One authorisation choke point: the method and path are turned into a route, the route is authorised against the caller's authority, and only then is a handler reached. Headers and body together are capped at 64 KB (413 beyond it, rather than the truncate-then-process it used to do), with a total receive deadline… |
| ✅ | Self-healing COM session | Probes ServerState (not Version) to decide whether the link is alive, checks the Windows service state for local hosts, reconnects with bounded retries and toasts "Reconnected …" then re-enters the current page. |
| ✅ | Session handling and theming | Basic credential kept in sessionStorage, 401 forces re-login, dark/light toggle persisted in localStorage. Server-supplied domain and account names are interpolated into innerHTML and inline onclick handlers… |
| ✅ | Setup and migration tooling | DBSetup (interactive DB creation), DBSetupQuick (headless), DBUpdater (schema migration), DataDirectorySynchronizer (reconcile files against the database) and ImportTool (accounts from text files, messages from mbox)… |
| ✅ | Thunderbird autoconfig (Mozilla clientConfig 1.1) | Served at both /mail/config-v1.1.xml and /.well-known/autoconfig/mail/config-v1.1.xml; derives the mail domain from an autoconfig.<domain> Host header, then the emailaddress query parameter (handles %40)… |
| ✅ | TOTP two-factor on the admin logon | RFC 6238 HMAC-SHA1, 30 s, 6 digits, with QR enrolment; secret stored DPAPI machine-scope in HKLM\SOFTWARE\hMailServer\AdminTotpSecret so it is shared with the classic Administrator. Three attempts then the session is dropped… |
| ✅ | Unattended launch and window/theme persistence | hMailCP.exe /connect <host> <user> <password> auto-connects; theme follows the OS until an explicit toggle, and window bounds/maximised state are persisted under HKCU\Software\hMailServer\ControlPanel with an off-screen guard. |
| ✅ | Unit tests for Control Panel services | xUnit project covering the consistency-report parser (including mid-rewrite and comment-line cases), numeric field validation, password generation and strength. |
| ✅ | WPF .NET 8 Control Panel (hMailCP) | 41 registered pages behind a cached page factory, WPF-UI/Fluent chrome, LiveCharts + SkiaSharp charting, QRCoder for TOTP enrolment. Replaces the classic Administrator (no MFC/Delphi admin remains in the tree). |
| ✅ | /.well-known/caldav and /.well-known/carddav redirects | This row said "Not served" and had said so since it was written; it was wrong, and the correction is worth more than the row. `WebServicesServer::ProcessRequest_` answers both well-known paths from `CalDavRedirectUrl` and `CardDavRedirectUrl`, deliberately not restricted to GET (RFC 6764 section 6 has clients issuing PROPFIND straight at the well-known URI, and a client that gets 404 for its PROPFIND stops looking) and deliberately not gated on `AutoconfigEnabled`, since the target is a different server entirely. Both values are cached for sixty seconds because the paths are public and unauthenticated, and a value that is not a usable absolute URL is reported once per start rather than once per request. Neither setting had an editor, a `README` mention or an `IniFileSettings` accessor, so a capability that was implemented, served and hardened was reachable only by hand-editing a key whose name appeared nowhere. Both are now on the Web services page, under a blurb that leads with the thing an administrator would otherwise assume wrongly: **hMailServer does not implement CalDAV or CardDAV** - these only point a client that knows the mail address at the server that does. |
| ✅ | The Control Panel shows live state, not only configuration | Five pages now answer a question the configuration cannot. **TCP/IP ports** gained a Listening column read from the operating system's own open-port table, so a port that failed to bind - `TCPServer::InitAcceptor` reports error 4316 and the server carries on running every other listener - is visible instead of looking identical to a working one; it distinguishes "the protocol is switched off" from "it should be up and is not", and says outright that the check cannot tell hMailServer's listener from another program's, because a port held by a different mail server is the usual cause. **ACME** shows the expiry of the certificate actually issued, which is the only thing that distinguishes working automation from automation that stopped: an expired automatic certificate changes nothing on the page and its first symptom is clients refusing to connect. **ManageSieve** says whether STARTTLS will be offered - it has no certificate setting of its own and borrows one from a TLS-capable IMAP, POP3 or SMTP port, so an administrator could otherwise configure the listener, restart, connect, and send the mailbox password in the clear with nothing having suggested it. **OAuth2** says whether the settings form a configuration that can accept any token at all - and, separately, how much a valid token is allowed to prove. And the **cache** page shows the hit rate the server has counted all along and exposed over COM, without which four TTLs and four size caps could only be tuned by guessing. **Every one of these readouts was then adversarially refuted against the server source, and the OAuth2 one was wrong in the dangerous direction** - it treated a blank issuer or audience as a fatal gap and said "no client can log on", while `OAuth2TokenValidator` applies each of those checks only when the value is set, and blank is the shipped default. The configuration it called a lockout is in fact the permissive one: any correctly-signed unexpired token is accepted, including one the identity provider minted for a different application. It now says that, and separately names the two things that genuinely stop every token - no key material for the algorithms allowed, and an allow-list naming an algorithm the server does not implement, which is anything but RS256 and HS256. The same pass corrected the ManageSieve readout (the server also requires the borrowed certificate to resolve and to name both files, and a dangling id is the ordinary result of deleting a certificate that was in use), the listener diagnosis (it blamed a port conflict for the commonest case, which is a port added since the service last started - the state this very page creates every time it adds one), and four smaller claims. |
| ✅ | REST API keys are managed from the Control Panel | The keys were reachable only over the API they authenticate: minting one meant a hand-written POST carrying the administrator password against a listener that is off by default. In practice that means automation is given the administrator password, which is exactly what scoped keys exist to prevent. A page now lists, creates and revokes them - file operations on the server's own machine, and the server re-reads the store on every request, so a key is live or dead immediately and the page works while the REST listener is switched off, which is the state every installation is in when it first arrives. Each rule shared with `RestApiServer` is mirrored deliberately and pinned by tests, including the digest against an independently computed vector: the `hmapi_` prefix and 32-byte secret, lower-hex SHA-256, `Hash` written last so an interrupted write leaves an unusable record rather than an unrestricted key, read-only as the default scope, the 90-day default lifetime, and the label rule that refuses control characters - which is an INI-injection guard, not a tidiness rule. A disagreement between the two sides would not fail a build; it would write a key that lists on the page and authenticates nothing. The token is shown once and the page says so before showing it, because the store holds a digest and no page could honestly offer to show it later. |
| ⬜ | Admin UI accessibility | Nobody owns it. The dashboard charts render unlabelled series distinguishable only by colour (no legend; Success/Warning separate by ΔE 5.1 under protanopia against a target of 8)… |
| ⬜ | API coverage beyond status/domains/accounts/queue/TLSA | ProcessRequest_ routes exactly nine API operations. No endpoints for settings, aliases, distribution lists, rules, certificates, DKIM, logs, IP ranges or backup — everything else is COM-only. |
| ✅ | Apple .mobileconfig configuration profile — shipped 12 Aug 2026 | `GET /email.mobileconfig` (and `/mail/config.mobileconfig`) returns an Apple configuration profile built from the same live TCP/IP port table the other autoconfig endpoints use, so an iOS or macOS device configures itself from one tap. **It refuses to serve a half-configured profile**: a profile needs both an incoming (IMAP, or POP3 only when no IMAP port exists) and an SMTP port, and without both it 404s rather than installing an account that accepts mail and silently cannot send it. Apple's payload has one encryption key per direction and no separate STARTTLS flag — Mail infers the mode from the port — so both encrypted socket types map to true and only a genuinely plaintext port maps to false.
