UUIDs are derived from the domain and therefore stable, so reinstalling a profile replaces the account instead of adding a second one. The address is pre-filled only when the request actually supplied a plausible one; otherwise the keys are omitted and the device prompts, which is right for a profile fetched with no hint of who is installing it. (This row read "no profile generator anywhere in the tree" until 21 Aug 2026, nine days after it shipped.) |
| ✅ | IPv6 for the management/observability listeners — shipped 21 Aug 2026 | All three hand-rolled listeners (REST, metrics, web services) built `AF_INET` sockets and parsed the bind address with `inet_pton(AF_INET)`, so none could be reached over IPv6 while the Boost.Asio mail listeners always could. All three now bind either family, and `::` clears `IPV6_V6ONLY` so it serves both — deliberate, because unlike the mail ports (one row per address) these have exactly one bind setting each, so `::` is the only spelling that can serve everyone, and a v6-only `::` would strand every IPv4 client with nothing in any log. **The security half is the part that mattered**: several of these refuse to run without TLS unless bound to loopback, and the metrics endpoint withholds data from non-loopback callers, so `::1` had to start counting as loopback or the checks would have silently weakened. It does — and `::` deliberately does not, which is pinned by its own test. A dual-stack IPv4 peer arrives v4-mapped (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) and is unmapped before any address comparison, because otherwise the auto-ban loopback exclusion would stop matching and the server would ban its own loopback. |
| ⬜ | OpenAPI / Swagger description | No machine-readable API description anywhere in the repo; the endpoint list exists only as prose in the README's Configuration reference. |
| ⬜ | Settings, logs, certificates and rules in the browser | The Control Deck cannot edit any server setting, view logs, manage certificates or edit rules — those exist only in the desktop Control Panel and COM. |
| ✅ | SRV client-discovery records — shipped 21 Aug 2026 | `GET /api/v1/srv` emits the `_imap`/`_imaps`/`_pop3`/`_pop3s`/`_submission`/`_submissions`/`_autodiscover` records an administrator should publish, built from the TCP/IP ports that are actually configured and enabled — modelled on the DANE TLSA endpoint, which had been the only place this server told anyone what to put in DNS. A service that is not listening gets no record, because a record advertising a dead port is worse than none; port 25 is never advertised, because SRV discovery is for clients and no client should submit there. It advertises the *configuration*, so a port added since the last restart appears before its listener does — the same semantics autoconfig already has. |

### Observability and diagnostics

36 shipped · 0 underway · 2 not started · 1 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Archiving runs synchronously inside message acceptance | The archive copies happen between the anti-spam/modification stages and PersistentMessage::SaveObject, i.e. before the 250 is sent, so archive I/O latency is added to every accepted message and a slow archive volume slows acceptance. |
| ✅ | AWStats delivery journal | Per-recipient tab-separated stream (time, sender, recipient, sender IP, recipient IP, SMTP, ?, code, bytes) written to hmailserver_awstats.log on delivery success and failure; toggled by Logging.AWStatsEnabled. Serialised inside the process by the logger's own mutex, so concurrent deliveries cannot interleave a record. **Every field is sanitised as of 13 August 2026, not only the two addresses.** The column that most needed it was the one nobody had touched: `%host_r` is filled from the MX target hostname on the outbound path, which is a value taken from a DNS answer served by whoever runs the recipient's domain, and a CR or an LF in it forges a complete, plausible delivery record in a stream that an AWStats installation parses by field position. Control characters, tabs, spaces and angle brackets are now stripped from all four text fields, and a null message pointer is refused rather than dereferenced, so the journal writer cannot be the thing that faults the server. The one remaining hole was in the logger rather than here, and is **closed as of 13 August 2026**: `Logger::WriteData_` applied MaxLogLineLen truncation to AWStats records, and because the journal is positional and machine-read, cutting bytes out of the middle did not produce a short record - it produced one whose every remaining column had moved, parsing without complaint into the wrong fields. A long recipient list was enough. AWStats is now exempt from truncation entirely, and the head/tail arithmetic is guarded as well: it subtracted 30 and 25 from lengths it never checked, so a MaxLogLineLen below those - an administrator's to set - produced negative Mid() counts and, through CStdString's clamping, a "truncated" line longer than the original. Reachable only with LogLevel <= 2 and debug logging off, which is precisely why it would go unnoticed on the installations where it did fire. |
| ✅ | Backup and restore over COM | BackupManager.StartBackup() and LoadBackup(xmlFile) → Backup.StartRestore(); BackupSettings selects destination, whether to include settings/domains/messages and whether to compress. LoadBackup fails (DISP_E_BADINDEX, with the reason in hmailserver_backup.log) for an archive that cannot be read, is not an hMailServer backup, or was written by a newer version; the Contains* properties report what the archive actually holds and a restore will not exceed them. |
| ✅ | Backup log | Backup progress is written to hmailserver_backup.log (UTF-16 with BOM) and the path is exposed through BackupSettings.LogFile. |
| ✅ | Built-in diagnostic test suite (9 tests) | TestInformationGatherer, TestIPv6, TestOutboundPort (needs a test domain), TestBackupDirectory, TestMXRecords, TestConnectToSelf (both need a local domain), TestDataDirectory, TestIPRanges, TestErrorLogs — each returning name… |
| ✅ | Crash reporting: minidump plus an error-log record | An out-of-process writer (`hMailServer.Minidump.exe`, `MiniDumpNormal` - stacks and thread contexts, no heap, so no message bodies or TLS keys) driven through shared memory, bounded at ten dumps and 100 MB with nothing deleted until it is four hours old, so a fault storm can neither fill the disk nor erase the first and most useful dump. **Four defects fixed on 13 August 2026, all in the part that runs when things are already going wrong.** The report did not say which fault it was: `exception_code` was accepted as a parameter of `ExceptionLogger::Log` and then never referenced anywhere in the file, so an access violation, a stack overflow and a divide by zero were all announced as the identical sentence with a path in it - and an unreferenced parameter is C4100, a /W4 warning, which is why /W3 /WX never caught it. It now carries the code, its name, the faulting address, the thread, and for an access violation which operation was attempted against which address. Worse, a crash that could not be dumped was not reported **at all**: the refusal went to `LOG_DEBUG`, which is off by default, and the function returned before the error-log entry, so the eleventh fault in a four-hour window and every one after it was invisible - and the eleventh is often the one that finally makes somebody look. It is now HM6015. The reporter could also kill the service while describing a crash, because `ExceptionHandler::Run` calls it from inside the `__except` **filter expression** and three calls in it can throw - including `windows_shared_memory(create_only, …)`, whose segment name is the fixed, unqualified "hMailServerMiniDumpMemory", so a second hMailServer process beside the service was enough. And the wait on the dump writer was `INFINITE` on a pool thread holding the reporter's mutex, so one wedged dump writer took every later crash report with it. Covered by `Infrastructure/ExceptionHandlerDiagnosticsTests.cs`, whose three tests fail against the previous build on the missing exception code, the entirely absent report for the eleventh crash, and the fifth large dump written past the byte ceiling. |
| ✅ | Delivery-queue depth caching | hmailserver_delivery_queue_messages is backed by a COUNT(*) against hm_messages, cached for 10 s so a fast scrape interval cannot hammer the database; safe without locking because clients are handled serially. |
| ✅ | Filesystem archive on receipt | With ArchiveDir set, every accepted message is copied to {ArchiveDir}\{senderDomain}\{senderUser}\Sent-<file> for local senders, to {ArchiveDir}\Inbound\ for external senders… |
| ✅ | Instrumented span points | Three: one server-kind span per client protocol command line (named after the verb, attributed with hmailserver.session.id, one trace per session), one client-kind db.query span at the database chokepoint… |
| ✅ | JSON-lines structured logging | JsonLogging=1 switches every category to one JSON object per line with ts, category, thread, optional session, optional remoteip and message, with correct escaping including \u00XX for control characters. Off by default… |
| ✅ | Kubernetes-style probes | Three probes on the metrics listener, always served and never authenticated because a load balancer health check has nowhere to keep a credential. /livez always 200 if the thread answers (deliberately no dependency checks, so a database outage does not get a healthy process killed). /readyz 200 only when the state is StateRunning AND the database has answered a real round trip within the last 20 s — not merely when the connection pool holds objects, which is what it used to check and which stayed true throughout a total outage. /healthz returns JSON status/state/database/uptime; the session counts were removed from it because /metrics answers 503 to withhold exactly those from an unauthenticated caller on a non-loopback bind, and this endpoint stays open in that configuration. No probe issues a database query of its own — all three read what a background refresh thread published, so a stalled database cannot stall a probe. |
| ✅ | Live log streaming over COM | Logging.EnableLiveLogging + Logging.LiveLog drain an in-memory buffer; the buffer self-disables past LiveLogMaxSize so a listener that walks away cannot grow it without bound. |
| ✅ | Log categories and files | Nine destinations: dated hmailserver_<date>.log, dated ERROR_hmailserver_<date>.log, hmailserver_awstats.log, hmailserver_backup.log, hmailserver_events.log… **Two defects closed on 13 August 2026, in the first audit this file has had** - it was scoped out of the diagnostics pass, which is why the AWStats hole above sat documented and unfixed. `CleanLogMessage_` flattened the CRLF pair and nothing else, so a lone LF or CR went into the file untouched and split one record into two; every record is tab-separated with quoted fields, so the halves read as two ordinary log lines with the second one's contents chosen by whoever supplied the message. The messages are not ours - protocol conversations are logged verbatim, along with header values, peer banners, resolver text and remote host names - and this server accepts bare LFs on the wire deliberately, so "the protocol strips it first" does not hold. ErrorManager was given the same treatment days earlier for the error log and the OnError hook; this was the same defect in the logger every other line goes through. The JSON format was never affected: `EscapeJson_` handles quotes, backslash, the five named escapes and every control character below 0x20. Separately, `GetCurrentLogFile_` refreshed `SepSvcLogs` *after* building the file name that setting decides, so on the one call where it changed, the name was chosen with the old value and the File object with the new one - one line into the wrong file, correcting itself only on the next. |
| ✅ | Log retention | LogDeleteDays prunes by last-write time, run once shortly after start-up and then every six hours. Deliberately narrow: only files named hmailserver_*.log or error_hmailserver_*.log are ever deleted. Disabled by default (0). |
| ✅ | Log rotation | Daily only, and implicit — rotation happens because the filename embeds the date and the writer reopens when the name changes. No size-based rollover, no numbered generations, no compression… |
| ✅ | Log volume controls | LogLevel (>=3 full detail, <=2 quieter) plus MaxLogLineLen truncation that keeps the head and last 25 characters with " ... " between; debug logging overrides truncation. |
| ✅ | Message-store consistency check | Background task cross-checks hm_messages against files on disk at start-up and hourly, publishes hmailserver_messagestore_missing_files, and rewrites hMailServer_messagestore_consistency.report in the log folder… |
| ✅ | Metric catalogue (30 families) | Counters: hmailserver_processed_messages_total, _spam_messages_total, _viruses_removed_total, _tls_handshakes_total, _tls_handshake_failures_total, _auth_success_total, _auth_failures_total, _messages_delivered_total… **Two added 13 August 2026, and the count corrected from 20 to 28 by measuring rather than remembering** (`grep -o '# TYPE hmailserver_[a-z_0-9]*' MetricsServer.cpp | sort -u | wc -l` - it had drifted by six before today, which is what a hand-maintained count in a document does). The new pair is `hmailserver_workqueue_depth` and `hmailserver_workqueue_blocking_tasks_waiting`, both gauges labelled by queue. They exist because `WorkQueue::GetQueueDepth` and `GetWaitingBlockingTaskCount` were correct and had no caller anywhere in the tree, so the only way queue pressure reached an operator was the stall report - which fires once a queue is already wedged, and a queue filling up is the part worth seeing coming. Both are cheap enough for the request thread (an atomic load, and a short lock around a size()) and the label is bounded: this server creates five queues, all named at startup. `GetRunningTasks` is deliberately not exposed - it is per-task detail with a name and a thread id, which would mint a series per task, and it already has the right home in the stall report. |
| ✅ | Metric labels | Only two label dimensions exist: hmailserver_sessions{protocol="smtp"\|"imap"\|"pop3"} and hmailserver_db_connections{state="busy"\|"available"}. Every other series is a single unlabelled global value — no per-domain… |
| ✅ | OpenTelemetry trace export (OTLP/HTTP JSON) | Dependency-free exporter: batches completed spans onto a background thread and POSTs OTLP JSON to <OtelEndpoint>/v1/traces (default port 4318), tagged with a service.name resource attribute from OtelServiceName… |
| ✅ | OpenTelemetry trace export and JSON logging | OtelTracer exports spans in batches to an OTLP/HTTP collector (OtelEndpoint/OtelServiceName; a cheap no-op when unset) with message-to-session correlation IDs; JsonLogging=1 switches the logger to JSON lines… |
| ✅ | Operator runbooks | Two operator-facing documents ship in-tree: DiagnosingStalledMail.md and HighAvailabilityRunbook.md. |
| ✅ | Optional hardlinking of per-recipient archive copies | ArchiveHardLinks=1 uses Win32 CreateHardLink for the per-recipient copy and falls back to a full copy (logging "HardLink failed.. Falling back to Copy.") when the link cannot be made — e.g. across volumes or on non-NTFS. |
| ✅ | Prometheus /metrics endpoint | Text exposition format version 0.0.4, single accept thread, plain HTTP/1.0 or HTTPS when MetricsServerCertificateFile and MetricsServerPrivateKeyFile are set (context from SslContextInitializer, TLS 1.2 floor). Off by default (MetricsServerPort=0), default bind 127.0.0.1, SOMAXCONN backlog. Bounded in every direction: 8 KiB request header block, 5 s per-socket and 5 s absolute read deadlines, a 15 s absolute write deadline over a looping send, and a 128-series ceiling on the one family whose size is driven by data (certificate expiry). No database or certificate-file I/O happens on the request thread at all. |
| ✅ | Prometheus metrics and Kubernetes-style health probes | /metrics plus /livez (liveness), /readyz (200 only when StateRunning and the database has answered a round trip within the last 20 s; 503 while draining, and 503 during an outage — it used to answer 200 there, because DatabaseConnectionManager::GetIsConnected() counts connection objects rather than reachability) and /healthz (JSON status/state/database/uptime), covering processed/spam/virus counts, TLS handshakes, auth outcomes, delivery outcomes, session and pool gauges, delivery-queue depth and age, certificate expiry, the database readiness probe's timestamp and age, and two latency histograms… |
| ✅ | Slow-query log | SlowQueryLogMilliseconds logs database statements above the threshold and feeds the hmailserver_db_slow_queries_total counter; 0 disables (the default). |
| ✅ | Work-queue stall reporting | A once-a-minute scheduled task names the tasks holding the message-acknowledgement threads when they are all busy with work queued behind them, running on a different queue than the one it measures… |
| ⏸️ | OTLP over TLS / gRPC transport | Endpoint must be an http:// URL; https:// is rejected at startup with "OtelEndpoint must be an http:// URL; tracing disabled." Consciously postponed in a source comment: "Only plain HTTP is supported … TLS export is future work." |
| ⬜ | Archive index, search, immutability, per-domain scope | The archive is a raw directory tree with no database record, no WORM/legal hold and no way to scope it to particular domains. Retention left this row on 20 August 2026: `ArchiveRetentionDays` (default 0 = keep for ever) drives a sweep that prunes by age and removes the emptied per-user folders behind it, refusing symlinks and depth-capped so it cannot be steered into a walk of the disk. |
| ✅ | Authentication on /metrics | MetricsServerAuthToken (Authorization: Bearer) and/or MetricsServerAuthUsername + MetricsServerAuthPassword (HTTP Basic); either satisfies the check when both are configured, compared in constant time through the shared SecureCompare.h over the whole "username:password" so the user name is not separably timeable. Optional on a loopback bind, so every existing local scrape keeps working across an upgrade; REQUIRED beyond loopback, where /metrics answers 503 naming the settings that would open it rather than 401 challenging for a credential that does not exist. The health probes are never authenticated in any configuration. Refusals are counted in hmailserver_metrics_unauthorized_requests_total rather than logged per request. |
| ✅ | TLS on /metrics | Opt-in and never inferred: HTTPS when both MetricsServerCertificateFile and MetricsServerPrivateKeyFile are set, otherwise plain HTTP. The context comes from `SslContextInitializer::InitServer` — the same call SMTP, POP3 and IMAP make, so the cipher list, version toggles and the post-quantum key-exchange groups in `TlsKeyExchangeGroups` cannot diverge here — with a TLS 1.2 floor re-applied afterwards so it can only tighten. `SSL_CTX_new` is deliberately absent from the file. The whole port is HTTPS when enabled, probes included; first-byte sniffing to serve both on one port was rejected because it makes encryption the client's choice. TLS is not forced on a non-loopback bind (that would break the plain-HTTP probe the HA runbook documents), but a credential without TLS there logs that it will cross the network in clear text. A TLS configuration that cannot be prepared serves the probes over plain HTTP and answers 503 on /metrics rather than silently downgrading. |
| ✅ | Latency percentiles | hmailserver_command_processing_seconds and hmailserver_db_query_seconds are Prometheus histograms with compiled-in bucket layouts (twelve bounds each, scaled differently because a protocol command line is tens of microseconds and a database statement is milliseconds), so histogram_quantile() gives p95/p99. The bucket is chosen on the observation path in ServerStatus, so the declared type is "histogram" from the first scrape on an idle server and never changes at runtime — a family whose type flips is worse than no histogram, because Prometheus keeps the first type it saw. _sum and _count keep their names and meaning, so anything already deriving a mean keeps working, and _count is emitted from the le="+Inf" bucket so the two can never disagree. |
| ⬜ | Metric history / persistence | /metrics is a stateless scrape and nothing stores samples server-side; the only history anywhere is the Control Panel's three-minute in-RAM buffer. No metrics-sample table, no retention setting, so no 24 h / 7 d / 30 d views. |
| ✅ | OTLP metrics and logs signals — shipped 21 Aug 2026, both off by default | The traces signal had been alone; `/v1/metrics` and `/v1/logs` now sit beside it, sharing one transport so three exporters cannot drift apart. **The metrics are the same counters `/metrics` already serves, under the same Prometheus names** — deliberately, because two independent tallies of one event drift, and then two dashboards disagree with no way to tell which is lying. What is **not** exported is the more interesting half and is stated rather than quietly omitted: queue depth, database probes and certificate expiry are computed by the metrics *listener* from database and file reads, and a push exporter has no business owning those. Logs come from the single `Logger` chokepoint — same categories, same mask as the files — carrying `traceId`/`spanId` whenever a span was active on the logging thread. The exporter's own worker thread is excluded from its own feed, because a dead collector would otherwise export its own failure lines and feed itself. The Control Panel claim for `OtelEndpoint` had read "there is no metrics or logs exporter"; it was true when written, and updating it was part of shipping this. |
| ✅ | Queryable message trace | **Schema 6020.** "What happened to the message Jane sent at 14:20" is now a search rather than a grep across several log files, one of which has probably rotated. **The events were never missing.** `AWStats.cpp` has been called from every interesting site for years - accepted, refused, delivered, failed - and wrote them to a text file shaped for a web-statistics tool with no way to ask it anything. So this is not an instrumentation pass: it is the same events, given a correlation key and somewhere to be questioned. Both entry points now record, which covers all sixteen call sites at once. **The correlation key is the queue id**, and what that does and does not buy is stated rather than implied. Every event sharing a queue id is one message instance, which answers the question above. It does **not** follow a message across a fork - a forward, a distribution list or a rule that re-sends creates a new queue entry, and the trace shows that as a separate instance. Following a fork needs the RFC `Message-ID`, which lives in the message file rather than in the objects these call sites hold, and reading it would put a file parse on every delivery. An event recorded before a queue entry existed - a refusal at RCPT - carries queue id **0**, which a test asserts so that a reader knows 0 means "there was never a queue entry" rather than "the id was lost". Recorded **before** the AWStats guard, because the two are separate features that happen to observe the same moments: an administrator who wants a queryable trace should not have to switch on a web-statistics journal to get one. The recorder swallows its own exceptions without even reporting them - a trace that can interrupt a delivery is worse than no trace, since the failure would be invisible in precisely the tool built to make deliveries visible. Over COM as `GlobalObjects.MessageTrace` (beside `DeliveryQueue`, because the two answer the same question at different moments - where is my mail now, and what happened to it), and in the Control Panel as a search page next to the queue. Server-admin only: the search matches sender **or** recipient across the whole table, so a domain administrator who could run it would see who every other domain's users correspond with. **Off by default, and that is a privacy default rather than a performance one** - the table is a record of who corresponds with whom, retained. Six tests. |
| ✅ | Scheduled / automatic backups | CreateScheduledTasks_ registers greylist cleaning, expired-record removal, TLS-RPT reporting, log retention, message-store consistency, work-queue health, ACME renewal and — when ScheduledBackupTime or ScheduledBackupIntervalMinutes is set in hMailServer.ini — BackupScheduleTask, which queues the run on the maintenance work queue rather than performing it on the scheduler thread, backs off 5/10/20/40/60 minutes after a refused run, and pre-flights the destination and the database before claiming the single backup slot. Absent from a default installation, in which no backup task is created at all. |
| ✅ | SQL log device and NCSA log format | eLogDevice hLogDeviceSQL and eLogOutputFormat hLogFormatCSA are declared in the IDL, editable through COM and offered in the Control Panel's Logging page… |
| ✅ | W3C traceparent ingestion and propagation — shipped 21 Aug 2026 | Trace ids were always minted locally, so a message crossing this server started a brand-new trace and the link to whatever sent it was lost. Inbound context is now read from HTTP headers and — the interesting half — from the **message headers** on SMTP, where W3C context travels as a header field rather than a transport one. Reception **prepends** its own `traceparent` the way `Received:` is prepended, so the first occurrence is authoritative; the original line is deliberately **not deleted**, because a sender's DKIM signature may cover it and prepending is signature-safe where deletion is not. Delivery reads that header back, so every retry and every downstream hop join one trace. **Validation is a security boundary, not a formality**: the value is attacker-supplied on any internet-facing path, so the version and format are enforced exactly, an all-zero trace or span id is rejected, and a malformed value is **never propagated onward** — but it never refuses anything either, because observability must not be able to reject mail. The inbound `sampled` bit feeds **no decision in either direction**: it cannot force expensive sampling on, and it cannot be used as an attacker's opt-out of the audit trail. It is recorded as an attribute; every emitted flag is this server's own decision. |
| ✅ | Windows Event Log integration — shipped 21 Aug 2026, on by default | The "event log" was a text file plus a COM call for scripts, so Event Viewer, a monitoring agent, a SIEM collector and a task triggered on an event id all saw **nothing, ever**. (This row read "None" — not quite: `Logger::ReportWriteFailure_` has always written one event, once per process, for the one failure that cannot be logged because logging is what failed. It is untouched and id 0 is reserved for it.) **`Logger` turned out to be the wrong hook**: `LOG_APPLICATION` is mask-gated at the call site, so a default install with logging off would emit nothing, which defeats the whole case for a default-on feature. `ErrorManager` is the only chokepoint every error passes unconditionally. Curated ids cover what an operator alerts on — database unavailable, listener would not start, crash, backup failed, brute-force protection impaired, disk floor reached — and an unrecognised code lands in its severity's catch-all, so staleness costs specificity and never silence. **What is deliberately absent has reasons, not omissions**: service start/stop (the SCM already records every transition, and the interesting gap — service up, servers dead — is covered by the listener and database ids), database *restored* (no unconditional signal exists and inventing one was out of scope), certificate expiry (nothing detects it as an event yet), and all protocol chatter. On by default at Critical+High because **a healthy server writes zero events**, so the quiet server pays nothing while the administrator who never finds the setting is the one whose only monitoring is Event Viewer; a five-per-id-per-ten-minutes throttle answers the flood case and says how many it swallowed. The message-file question was settled **by measurement** — `FormatMessage` was called against .NET's `EventLogMessages.dll` for ids 0, 2001, 2010 and 65535 to confirm all map to `%1` — and the unregistered-source appearance is written down rather than discovered later. |

### Extensibility and scripting

39 shipped · 0 underway · 1 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Client object exposes TLS session detail | IPAddress, Port, Username, HELO, SessionID, Authenticated, EncryptedConnection plus CipherVersion, CipherName and CipherBits - so a script can gate on TLS version/cipher strength |
| ✅ | COM control of the script engine | hMailServer.Scripting exposes Enabled, Language, Directory, CurrentScriptFile, Reload() and CheckSyntax() - so an external tool can flip scripting on/off, swap language and hot-reload without restarting the service. `Language` is validated: only VBScript and JScript are accepted, case-insensitively, and the canonical spelling is stored - because `ScriptServer` compares the language with a case-sensitive `==`, so an unrecognised *or* mis-cased name used to be accepted and silently disable every handler |
| ✅ | Compile/syntax check before activation | LoadScripts compiles the file first and reports error HM5016 without registering anything if compilation fails; also exposed on demand via the COM API and Control Panel |
| ✅ | Control Panel event-script editor | Dedicated Scripts page: loads the file named by Scripting.CurrentScriptFile, edits it, writes a .bak beside it on save, then calls Reload() and reports the CheckSyntax() result inline. Plain TextBox - no syntax highlighting… |
| ✅ | Custom script function as a rule action | Rule action type ScriptFunction (5) invokes any named function in the script file with HMAILSERVER_MESSAGE; the message is reloaded afterwards so script edits to the file are picked up. Available to both global and per-account rules |
| ✅ | Event invocation by source-text concatenation | The call is built as text and appended to the script before parsing; string arguments are escaped per language (VBScript doubles quotes, JScript escapes apostrophes) - except OnDeliveryFailed… |
| ✅ | EventLog object always present | EventLog is added to every container by the constructor, so EventLog.Write is callable from any handler and writes to the server event log |
| ✅ | Fail-closed on a hung script file | If the file's top-level code exceeds the timeout during load, loading is deliberately abandoned and no event handlers are registered at all (including OnClientLogon / OnClientValidatePassword) until the admin fixes it and reloads… |
| ✅ | Fresh engine and full re-parse on every event | Each event creates a new CScriptSiteBasic, re-parses the whole script text with the call appended, runs it and terminates; no state persists between events and parse cost is paid per message/connection |
| ✅ | Full COM API reachable from scripts | Scripts can CreateObject("hMailServer.Application") and authenticate to reach the whole administration object model (domains, accounts, rules, messages)… |
| ✅ | Handler auto-registration by function probing | On load the server probes 15 named handler functions and only fires the ones that exist; each probe spins up a fresh engine and re-executes the file's top-level code… |
| ✅ | Legacy rules engine (global and per-account) | Eight predefined criteria fields (From/To/CC/Subject/Body/MessageSize/RecipientList/DeliveryAttempts) or any arbitrary header by name, with eight match types including regex and wildcard, and ten action types (Delete, Forward, Reply, MoveToIMAPFolder, ScriptFunction, StopRuleProcessing, SetHeaderValue, SendUsingRoute, CreateCopy, BindToAddress). This row said "nine predefined fields" and then listed eight: `RuleCriteria::PredefinedField` runs FTFrom=1 to FTDeliveryAttempts=8 and also carries FTUnknown=0, which is not selectable and should never have been counted. Available to both global and per-account rules. |
| ✅ | Objects injected into the script namespace | Container maps names to COM wrappers for six types: Result, Message (hMailServer.Message), Client, EventLog, FetchAccount and Account; all are added as global members so handlers reference them by name. Result and Client are also registered coclasses with their own ProgIDs, so a script can create one directly instead of using the one the engine supplies; an object created that way now refuses every accessor rather than dereferencing the empty pointer it was holding |
| ✅ | OnAcceptMessage(oClient, oMessage) | Fires after end-of-data, on the async accept task that holds the thread sending the 250, immediately before the message is saved and queued; script may rewrite the spool file. Same 554/453 reject mapping |
| ✅ | OnBackupCompleted() / OnBackupFailed(sReason) | Fired by the backup manager at the end of a backup run; notification only |
| ✅ | OnClientConnect(oClient) | Fires on every accepted TCP connection for all listeners (SMTP/IMAP/POP3) before the greeting; Result.Value=1 drops the socket immediately |
| ✅ | OnClientLogon(oClient) | Fires after a successful authentication on all three protocols (SMTP AUTH, POP3 and IMAP LOGIN); notification only, no Result object, so it cannot veto the session |
| ✅ | OnClientValidatePassword(oAccount, sPassword) | Can override password validation: Result 0 accepts the logon outright, 1 rejects it, any other value (default 2) falls through to normal validation… |
| ✅ | OnDeliverMessage(oMessage) | Fires per delivery attempt after global rules; Result.Value=1 deletes the message. Because it runs on every retry, a script must be idempotent |
| ✅ | OnDeliveryFailed(oMessage, sRecipient, sErrorMessage) | Fires once per failed recipient (so several times for one message); notification only - no Result object is added to the container |
| ✅ | OnDeliveryStart(oMessage) | Fires once per message when delivery begins; Result.Value=1 deletes the message and logs "Action triggered by script subscribing to OnDeliveryStart" |
| ✅ | OnError recursion guard | When the killed script is the OnError handler itself, the interruption is logged rather than reported, because reporting an error re-fires OnError and would recurse until the stack is exhausted |
| ✅ | OnError(iSeverity, iCode, sSource, sDescription) | Fires for every `ErrorManager::ReportError` in the server, guarded so it is skipped before settings are loaded; this is the hook that makes SIEM/alert forwarding possible. **It did not fire for every error until 13 August 2026, and the ones it skipped were the ones worth forwarding.** The event is invoked by building script source text, and `ReportError` flattened newlines for the log line it writes while pasting the RAW description into the generated call - so any error whose text contained a newline produced `OnError(…, "…<line break>…")`, which neither VBScript nor JScript permits inside a string literal. The call did not parse, the handler never ran, and the only trace was a "Script Error" line about a call that appears in nobody's script. That is not an obscure class: script compile reports are literally `"File: <path>[CRLF]Script Error: …"`, and resolver text and anything built from `FormatMessage` carry newlines too. Both copies now come from one normalisation, a lone CR or LF is flattened as well as a CRLF pair (so a description built from a peer's banner can no longer split one ERROR record into two), and the JScript branch escapes the backslash - it did not before, so a handler was handed `C:\temp\…` with a real tab in it and a description ending in a backslash escaped the closing quote. Reporting an error from inside the handler can no longer recurse until the stack is exhausted either: `ErrorManager` holds a per-thread guard, which is the fix `ScriptServer` had already applied by hand at the two sites somebody thought of - with a comment naming this recursion at each - and which now also holds at the two that were left (HM5018 when a script site cannot be created, HM5022 when the watchdog timer cannot be cancelled). `ReportError` no longer throws either, except `boost::thread_interrupted`, which is passed through because it is how the server shuts down; four call sites had already wrapped it in a try/catch of their own. Pinned by `Infrastructure/ErrorManagerDiagnosticsTests.cs`, which installs a working OnError handler, then reloads a script that will not compile, and requires the multi-line HM5710 to reach the handler - against the previous build nothing is written to the event log at all. |
| ✅ | OnExternalAccountDownload(oFetchAccount, oMessage, sRemoteUID) | Fires per message fetched by the POP3 external fetcher (with oMessage null/Nothing when the message was skipped); the Result overrides the remote delete policy - 1 deletes immediately, 2 keeps for Result.Parameter days |
| ✅ | OnHELO(oClient) | Fires on both HELO and EHLO after the domain argument parses; Result 1 -> "554 Rejected", 2 -> "554 <message>", 3 -> "453 <message>". Client object carries IP, port, session, HELO name and TLS cipher details |
| ✅ | OnRecipientUnknown(oClient, oMessage) | Fires when the server is about to answer 550 unknown user; notification only - the Result object is not passed and no return value is acted on. Suppressed once the invalid-command limit trips |
| ✅ | OnSMTPData(oClient, oMessage) | Fires on the DATA command, before any body bytes are read, so a message can be refused without transferring it; same 554/554+msg/453+msg result mapping |
| ✅ | OnTooManyInvalidCommands(oClient, oMessage) | Fires when DisconnectInvalidClients trips the MaxNumberOfInvalidCommands limit and the connection is being dropped; notification only, no Result object |
| ✅ | Result object (Value / Message / Parameter) | Three writable properties; semantics are per-hook (reject codes for SMTP hooks, delete for delivery hooks, auth verdict for password validation, retention days for external download) and are not documented in one place in the code |
| ✅ | Script error reporting with source position | Runtime and compile errors are captured through IActiveScriptSite::OnScriptError and logged as "Script Error: Source ... Description ... Line ... Column ... Code ..."; the last message is retrievable for the syntax-check API |
| ✅ | Script execution watchdog | Each script invocation and each compile runs under a TimerQueue watchdog bounded by [Settings] ScriptTimeout; a script that overruns is interrupted, logged with the file name… |
| ✅ | Script execution watchdog (bounded scripts) | Every script run is armed with a timer-queue watchdog that calls IActiveScript::InterruptScriptThread; ScriptTimeout in hMailServer.ini, default 60s, 0 disables the limit |
| ✅ | Server-side event scripting (VBScript/JScript) | In-process Active Scripting host firing OnClientConnect/OnHELO/OnAcceptMessage/OnDeliverMessage/OnDeliveryStart/OnDeliveryFailed/OnClientLogon/OnClientValidatePassword/OnExternalAccountDownload/OnError… |
| ✅ | Single global event-script file | Loads exactly one file, {EventFolder}\EventHandlers.vbs\|.js, set by the INI key Directories/EventFolder; no per-domain or per-account scripts, no include/import mechanism, no multi-file support |
| ✅ | Starter templates for external integrations | Three insertable VBScript OnAcceptMessage templates: external AV/DLP command-line scanner, fire-and-forget webhook POST (SIEM/Slack/Teams), and an external HTTP verdict API… |
| ✅ | VBScript and JScript event scripting | Windows Active Scripting (IActiveScript) engine created by CoCreateInstance on the language name; only the literal settings "VBScript" and "JScript" are recognised… |
| ✅ | Watchdog limitation: blocked COM calls | An interrupt aborts script execution but cannot release a handler blocked inside a COM call (e.g. a synchronous ServerXMLHTTP to a dead host) - the limit is documented in the error text the admin receives |
| ✅ | External filter hook (rspamd / MTA-hook style) | Shipped 20 August 2026 and inert until `FilterHookUrl` names an engine: the accepted message is POSTed to it with the envelope, connecting address and HELO as request headers — the shape rspamd's own check endpoint expects — and the verdict comes back as a score, so it lands alongside SPF, DKIM and DMARC and the administrator's existing thresholds decide what happens. Milter itself stays declined; the row above says why the HTTP shape was chosen over it. |
| ⬜ | Script sandboxing / capability restriction | None: scripts run in-process under the service account with unrestricted CreateObject (the shipped Control Panel templates use WScript.Shell and MSXML2.ServerXMLHTTP); the only bound is the wall-clock watchdog. No allow-list… |
| ✅ | XCLIENT / PROXY protocol — shipped 21 Aug 2026, both off by default | Put a load balancer, a TLS terminator or a Postfix relay in front of the SMTP listener and every connection appeared to come from **it**. That is not one missing feature, it is **DNSBL, SPF, greylisting, auto-ban and the IP-range rules all quietly evaluating the wrong address while still reporting success** — and the `Received:` header recording the lie. Both mechanisms now exist: PROXY protocol **v1 and v2** (consumed before the TLS handshake and before the greeting) and Postfix **XCLIENT** (`ADDR NAME PORT PROTO HELO LOGIN`, answered with a fresh 220 so the upstream re-EHLOs). **The security rule is the whole feature**: a peer that can rewrite its own source address has defeated every IP-based control here, so both are off by default, the trusted list is **empty by default**, and every trust decision is made against the **real TCP peer** — never an address a header just supplied, so chained proxies cannot bootstrap trust. A malformed entry in the list matches **nothing** rather than everything. XCLIENT is not even *advertised* to an untrusted peer, because advertising it invites the attempt and leaks the deployment shape; a PROXY header from an untrusted peer **drops the connection** rather than being ignored, since ignoring it would leave the real client's commands being read as the proxy's. The rewrite lands before everything that consumes the address, and the security range is **re-read for the asserted client**, so an auto-ban range still bites through the proxy. Two operational consequences are stated rather than hidden: trust is per-source, not per-port, so a listed proxy **must** send the header on every connection; and the upstream has to be configured to send one at all (HAProxy `send-proxy`), which this server cannot check. |

### Build, testing and supply chain

8 shipped · 1 underway · 0 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Fault injection, so error handling can actually be run | Three kinds, all INI-only and all off out of the box: `SimulateSpoolWriteFailure` (a disk that fails, or fills up mid-message), `CrashSimulationMode`, and — added 13 August 2026 — **`SimulateDatabaseFailureFor`**, which names a substring of a SQL statement and fails exactly the statements containing it. A substring rather than a switch because the server has delivery threads, scheduled tasks and protocol sessions issuing statements concurrently, so "fail the next one" would fail whatever happened to be next; naming the write is what makes it usable against a running server. It exists because three sweeps that week fixed 51 discarded database results, **and every defect among them lived in error handling that had never once executed** — so the fixes added 51 more branches of exactly the kind that had just been found to be full of bugs. Empty by default (one inline bool per statement), unreachable over COM or the network, and it reports **HM6119** at High severity on every start and reinitialise for as long as it is set, because a facility like this does its damage by being left on rather than by being turned on. `Infrastructure/DatabaseFailureHandling.cs` covers the four worst of the fifty-one. Three of those four tests failed on their first run for instructive reasons — `TestSetup` disables auto-ban so one proved nothing; two patterns matched a `SELECT`'s column list as well as the write, refusing the command for a *different* reason; and `SQLStatement` emits `name=@name_1` with no spaces — all now recorded in the tests themselves rather than in anyone's memory. |
| ✅ | The server builds in Debug | It did not, and had not for long enough that the `x64\Debug` output directory on the development machine contained no `hMailServer.exe` at all - only the minidump tool, which is a separate project. Two files failed, `BackupExecuter.cpp` and `RuleGuard.cpp`, with a `C2059`/`C2091`/`C2802` trio pointing into `boost/filesystem/directory.hpp` and `boost/regex`, at lines where boost declares its own `operator new`. Neither file had anything wrong with it, which is why reading them explained nothing: the cause was `Common\Persistence\PersistenceMode.h`, a nineteen-line header declaring one enum, which also carried the MFC leak-tracking boilerplate that 441 **.cpp** files in this tree carry. In a .cpp that block is harmless because it sits after every `#include`. In a header there is no such place, so `new` became a macro for the rest of every translation unit that reached it - including every `#include` that followed - and any third-party header declaring `operator new` was rewritten into nonsense. Both casualties include a `Persistent*` header before their boost include, an ordering nothing required them to get right. Deleted, with the reasoning left in the file so it is not pasted back. This is why the Debug configuration matters: a build with assertions and full symbols is what you attach to a customer's problem, and the one that was available was Release. Found 13 August 2026 while checking something unrelated. |
| ✅ | CI, supply-chain and installer verification | Nine GitHub workflows: server build, CodeQL, SBOM (SPDX + CycloneDX via Syft), dependency review, installer smoke test on a clean machine, general CI, release signing, OpenSSF Scorecard and a monthly upstream-comparison job with a checked-in baseline. CodeQL analyses **both** languages as of 13 Aug 2026 — the C++ server, every protocol parser and the whole surface that faces the internet, had never been analysed once; it now runs weekly on the self-hosted runner because a CodeQL-traced build needs all 516 translation units from cold and there is no hosted image that can build it. C# runs on every push under the `security-and-quality` suite, which is the suite the Code Quality page reads and the reason that page was blank until now; C++ runs `security-extended`, because CodeQL ships no quality-tagged queries for C++ at all. |
| ✅ | Installer and in-place upgrade path | A single Inno Setup x64 installer that preserves configuration and mail on upgrade, bundles and silently installs the **.NET 10** Desktop Runtime, ships SQL CE for zero-config installs and stages the MariaDB connector plus plugins. Hardened 13 Aug 2026 after an audit of the scripts themselves, which had never been checked by anything: the installed-runtime probe had been broken by a stray `0x08` byte since the .NET 10 migration and matched nothing, so the runtime was installed twice on every install and a machine already on a newer 10.x got a bogus failure dialog quoting the .NET 8 download page; the wait for the service to stop was unbounded and could not block the install, so a service that would not stop either froze the wizard or let the **old binary survive an upgraded schema**; the exit codes of `/Register`, `/RegisterTypeLib`, `net start` and msiexec were all discarded, so an install with no service and no database still finished on "Completed"; a silent install wrote the MD5 of the *empty string* as the administrator password; the uninstaller deleted the service before stopping it and raised three error dialogs for hMailServer 3/4 files that have not shipped in a decade; the InstallLocation registry value was never removed on uninstall; and the service was created with no failure-recovery actions. Guarded from now on by `RegressionTests\Installation\InstallerPackaging.cs`, which also asserts that the shipped `DBScripts` can carry every engine to `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION`. |
| ✅ | Regression coverage for hooks and scanners | 24 event tests covering every hook in both VBScript and JScript, 5 ClamAV tests including live EICAR detection plus unreachable-scanner fail-open, and 14 SpamAssassin tests including a deliberate spamd outage |
| ✅ | Regression coverage for the authentication surface | Dedicated NUnit suites exist for SCRAM-PLUS on IMAP/POP3/SMTP, TLS version and TLS option negotiation, OAuth2 bearer, hash policy, password pepper, secret protection, auto-ban, password masking and protocol fuzzing. |
| ✅ | Architecture guide and docs index | ARCHITECTURE.md is published at the repository root, and hmailserver/docs/ has an index (README.md) over five guides — DiagnosingStalledMail, HighAvailabilityRunbook, Upgrading, RegulatoryScope and Fuzzing — plus the Grafana dashboard and the third-party licence texts. Every factual claim across all of them was verified against the code on 13 August 2026 and each page now carries a "Verified against the code" footer naming the functions it was checked against. The audit reported one remaining gap here — "there is no CONTRIBUTING.md" — and that was itself wrong, which is worth recording because it is the same failure the audit exists to catch, in the same week, in the other direction: **`.github/CONTRIBUTING.md` has been there since 7 August**, and the root README links to it correctly. Only the repository root was checked. The real defect was one relative path: the docs index pointed at `../../CONTRIBUTING.md` rather than `../../.github/CONTRIBUTING.md`, so the single link a first-time contributor would follow from the documentation was the one broken link in it. Fixed. |
| 🔄 | The release gate does not depend on somebody else's DNS | Ten tests in the gate resolved live third-party names, and when the resolver was slow they failed and read as a regression in whatever change was being gated. Found across four consecutive gate runs on 19 August 2026, each one exposing more of them, while the change under test was in `IMAPFetch.cpp` and could not have touched any. **Nine are fixed.** `Shared/FakeDnsServer.cs` answers A, MX and TXT on 127.0.0.1 (UDP and TCP) and NODATA-with-SOA for everything else; `Shared/ServerIniFile.cs` points `DNSServer` at it and `TestFixtureBase.RestartServerAndReacquireCom()` makes that take effect, since the ini is cached at process start. Six fixtures now serve their own zone - `AntiSpam.DKIM.Verification`, `AntiSpam.Basics`, `AntiSpam.WhiteListing`, `AntiSpam.DmarcRptReporting`, `Infrastructure.TCPIP` and `POP3.Fetching.Basics` - needing five names between them, because everything those fixtures assert is a NEGATIVE answer and a lookup that never returned is indistinguishable from one that found nothing. The DKIM fixture went from **24-35 seconds per test to 7.2 seconds for the whole fixture**, and stopped depending on Microsoft not rotating a key, which nothing had written down. Its 437-character key does not fit one DNS character-string, so serving it as two also proves the RFC 6376 3.6.2 concatenation this suite had never tested. Two fixtures got **stronger** assertions rather than merely faster ones: `Infrastructure.TCPIP` and two `POP3.Fetching.Basics` tests used to compare the server's answer against a live lookup the TEST process made of the same name - two lookups that merely had to agree with each other - and now check an address the fixture chose, so an answer of the right shape for the wrong host is caught. `Rules.ActionBindToAddress` needed no fake at all: it delivered to gmail.com purely to reach a bind that fails first, and now uses a route, which resolves nothing. `Sieve.SieveSpamtestDelivery` was the tenth and got the same treatment: it grounds its verdict in the real anti-spam pipeline on purpose - so a sender cannot steer a recipient's filter with a forged header - which had the side effect of routing that verdict over the internet, and a slow lookup made it report "the flag is not reaching the evaluator through the delivery path". **All ten are now served locally** - and the next gate found four more, which is the finding that matters most here: `AntiSpam.Combinations.TestDeleteThresholdLowerThanMarkThreshold`, `AntiSpam.GreyListing.ItShouldBePossibleToBypassGreylistingOnMessagesArrivingFromMXorA`, `API.Events.TestOnDeliveryFailedJScript`, and `API.Events.TestOnDeliveryStart_SetHtmlBodyEmpty` (a knock-on from the previous one). Five consecutive full-suite runs each exposed a fresh set, so **this cannot be finished by pinning fixtures one at a time** - the population is larger than any single run reveals, and each round costs 35 minutes to discover four more. **The resolver was the answer, and the measurement found it in minutes where fixtures had taken a night.** Making 9.9.9.9 the machine's PRIMARY resolver (not its secondary - Windows falls through only when the primary does not answer at all, never merely because it is slow) took the suite straight to **1671/1671, nothing failed, nothing skipped**. Two cautions against reading that as solved. It is an ENVIRONMENT fix, so the four tests it rescued are still live-DNS-dependent and will fail again on a slow network - the ten pinned fixtures are the part that is actually durable, and they are kept rather than reverted because they are also faster and, in three cases, assert more than they used to. And one name fails on **every** resolver tried, including Quad9: `selector1._domainkey.outlook.com` answers **"Bad DNS packet"**, because its 437-character multi-string TXT record is mangled over UDP - the exact `DNS_ERROR_BAD_PACKET` case `DNSResolverWinApi` documents and retries over TCP for. Serving that key locally turned out to be right for a second reason nobody anticipated. The remaining direction, if this recurs: point the whole SUITE at one fake zone from TestSetup rather than fixture by fixture, since NODATA is the correct answer for almost everything it asks and only two names in the entire suite need a positive one. There is a third defect underneath, exposed by the fourth failure above: a test that leaves a message in the delivery queue poisons the NEXT test, because that test's setup deletes the recipient account and delivery then reports HM5165 during it. Clearing the ERROR log at teardown cannot fix that - the error genuinely belongs to the later test - so the queue needs draining too. One unrelated flake is recorded rather than left to be rediscovered: `AntiVirus.ScannerFailurePolicy.WithFailClosedTheMessageIsHeldAndNotLost`, the test that proves a held message is preserved rather than lost, failed twice in five full-suite runs that day and passes every time in isolation, taking 36 seconds before giving up on the forced retry. That is not DNS - it never was - so the resolver change does not explain it, and it did NOT recur on the 1671/1671 run. One clean run is not evidence that an intermittent is gone, so it stays recorded: the working hypothesis is the forced retry, where the test calls `DeliveryQueue.ResetDeliveryTime` and then `StartDelivery`, and a queue whose in-memory view of the next-try time is staler than the row just updated would skip the message - which matches both the ~36 second timeout and the intermittency. Untested. One constraint rules out the easy fix of pointing the machine at a public resolver: `multi.surbl.org` answers SERVFAIL to 1.1.1.1 by design (8.8.8.8 and 9.9.9.9 do answer). |
| ✅ | A test that leaves mail queued fails the next test | Distinct from the ERROR-log cascade above and not fixed by it. `TestFixtureBase.SetUp` recreates the test domain, which deletes its accounts - so a message still sitting in the delivery queue from the PREVIOUS test then has no recipient, `LocalDelivery` correctly reports **HM5165** ("the recipient account appears to have been deleted after the message was received"), and `AssertNoReportedError` fails the test that is running. Clearing the error log at the previous test's teardown cannot fix this one, because the error has not happened yet at that point and genuinely belongs to the later test: seen 19 August 2026 as `API.Events.TestOnDeliveryStart_SetHtmlBodyEmpty` failing on an HM5165 caused by `TestOnDeliveryFailedJScript` leaving one message queued. The fix is to drain the queue as well as the log when a test fails - `AntiVirus.ScannerFailurePolicy` already does exactly that in its own teardown (`DeliveryQueue.Clear()`), for exactly this reason, which is the argument for doing it in the base rather than once per fixture that happens to notice. Fixed in `TestFixtureBase.TearDown`: when a test has failed, the queue is drained before the error log is settled, and that ORDER is the point - a queued message is a delivery that has not happened yet, so clearing the log first and the queue second would leave the message to raise its error afterwards. Failed tests only, like the log, because a test that PASSED and still left mail queued is a different defect and hiding it here would remove the only signal that it exists. |

### Cross-cutting and platform

8 shipped · 3 underway · 1 not started · 0 deferred

| | Capability | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | Active/passive HA topology | A documented, tested warm-standby runbook: exactly one node running, shared SQL plus shared message store, floating VIP driven by the /readyz probe, no clustering code and explicit split-brain avoidance… |
| ✅ | Built-in diagnostics suite | A 'run diagnostics' set exposed through COM and the Control Panel: outbound port connectivity, connect-to-self, MX record checks, IP range sanity, data and backup directory checks, error-log inspection and an explicit IPv6 test… |
| ✅ | Certificate lifecycle outside ACME | ACME issues, renews at <30 days, auto-assigns and hot-reloads without a restart. For manually provisioned certificates there is no expiry check, no warning… |
| ✅ | Graceful shutdown drain | [Settings] ShutdownDrainSeconds makes the service wait up to N seconds for active sessions to finish before stopping (0, the default, stops immediately), and /readyz returns 503 for the duration so a load balancer sheds the node first. |
| ✅ | IPv6 support end to end | Real but uneven, and never stated as a whole: IPAddress models IPV6, security ranges and DNSBL reverse-nibble lookups handle it, AAAA is used in HELO/PTR checks and there is a dedicated IPv6 diagnostic — but the ManageSieve listener ca… |
| ✅ | Localisation of server, Control Panel and installer | Server UI strings ship in exactly two languages (english.ini, swedish.ini) selected by UserInterfaceLanguage; the Inno Setup installer declares only English; the WPF Control Panel has no resx localisation at all… |
| ✅ | Scheduler and background task set | A minute-polled scheduler running `ScheduledTask` objects: TLS-RPT reporter, ACME renewal (at startup and hourly), scheduled backup, log retention (at startup and six-hourly), message-store consistency, greylist and expired-IP-range cleanup, and the work-queue health check. **Corrected 13 Aug 2026: external fetch was listed here and is not scheduler-driven** — `ExternalFetchManager` runs its own one-minute loop as a permanent task on the main server queue. Two limits are recorded rather than left implied: each due task runs inline on the scheduler's own thread while it holds the lock `ScheduleTask` needs, so one long task delays every other and blocks registration; and the next run time is computed after the task returns, so the real period is the interval plus the run time and a task that overruns its interval drifts further behind on every pass. |
| 🔄 | Scripts and scanners cannot starve the accept path | **Overstated; corrected 13 Aug 2026.** Two of the three claims hold. Script and scanner work does run on the shared async queue — `SMTPConnection` posts its finalization task there and `HandleSMTPFinalizationTaskCompleted_` is where the script and scanner stages run — and the per-stage timings are real: a start/done line per stage, a queue-wait line above 5 s, and a 451 when the finalization deadline is passed rather than a session left unanswered. The third did not hold, and now does. `AsyncQueueReservedThreads` is read only by `WorkQueue::GetBlockingLimit_`, which caps tasks enqueued as `WorkQueue::TaskMayBlock` — and the only call site in the entire tree that passed `TaskMayBlock` was `BackupManager`, on the *maintenance* queue. Every async-queue task, message finalization included, was `TaskNormal`, so nothing was held back for short work and a burst of slow script or scanner work could take the whole pool. The throttle was implemented and correct and simply not wired to the queue this row is about. **Wired 13 Aug 2026:** the finalization task is posted `TaskMayBlock` from both sites that post it (DATA and BDAT), which is what it has always been in fact — it runs the script and scanner stages, so it blocks on things outside this process. With the defaults (15 async threads, 2 reserved) that caps concurrent script/scanner work at 13 and keeps 2 threads free for short work. The two caller constraints `TaskMayBlock` documents were checked first, because getting them wrong deadlocks mail rather than slowing it: the handler neither posts to nor waits on another task on that queue, and nothing anywhere waits on the task's `GetIsStartedEvent()`. |
| 🔄 | Work queue saturation reporting and session caps | **Narrowed 13 Aug 2026, claim by claim.** True: `WorkQueueHealthTask` samples once a minute from the scheduler's thread and names the task, thread id and elapsed seconds for every held thread, capped at twenty with an "and N more" tail; `AsyncQueueStallThreshold` bounds it (120 s default, 0 disables). Narrower than it read: the health task samples the **asynchronous queue only** — maintenance, name lookup, SMTP delivery, external fetch, IOCP and the main server queue are never checked; `GetQueueDepth`, `GetWaitingBlockingTaskCount` and `GetRunningTasks` are correct but have **no caller anywhere in the tree**, so none of it reaches an operator except through the stall report itself; and `AsyncQueueReservedThreads` bounded nothing here, for the reason in the row above — now fixed, see that row. **Coverage extended 13 Aug 2026, and deliberately not to everything.** The health task asked for the asynchronous queue by name, so a queue added later was never going to be covered; it now asks every queue that has opted in via `WorkQueue::SetMonitorForStalls`, which adds the **name-lookup** queue — whose whole purpose is blocking reverse lookups on behalf of live sessions, where an unreachable reverse zone is exactly what wedges it, and which was invisible until now. The maintenance, main server and IOCP queues are deliberately NOT opted in, and that is the interesting half: the main server queue runs the delivery manager and the external-fetch manager as permanent looping tasks and the IOCP threads sit in `io_context::run` for the life of the process, so "every thread busy for two minutes" is their healthy state and reporting it would raise a High error every minute on a stock installation — which would break the next fixture in the suite and teach every operator to ignore the one report that matters. A backup or a consistency pass legitimately outruns the threshold too. The opt-in sits at the point each queue is created, so whoever adds the next one has to decide which kind theirs is. Fixed the same day: the detector compared the running-task count against the number of threads `Start()` created, a count never decremented when a worker left — so a queue that had lost workers, the state most worth reporting, was the one state that could not be reported. It now counts the threads still inside `io_context::run()`. |
| 🔄 | Client-facing surfaces | **Reopened, August 2026.** Three of the five surfaces previously declined here are now in the next-generation programme above: a **webmail of our own**, **JMAP**, and **CalDAV/CardDAV**. Two remain declined and the reasons still hold: LMTP only matters behind another MTA, which is not a realistic Windows deployment; and EAS/EWS are proprietary, patent-encumbered, and being retired by their own vendor. |
| ⬜ | End-user self-service portal | There is no web surface for users at all — no password self-service, no vacation toggle, no quota view, no quarantine release. Every user-initiated change goes through an administrator… |
| ✅ | LDAP / Active Directory as a directory backend | **Shipped 15 August 2026 and verified end to end against a live Windows Server 2025 domain controller, not a stub.** `Common/LDAP/DirectorySync` reads the directory and works out which accounts should exist, then - only when asked - creates and updates them. The split that makes it trustworthy is structural rather than a matter of care: `Plan_` makes every decision and writes nothing, `Apply_` writes and decides nothing, so a preview cannot describe an action the apply would not take, because there is only one decider. `PreviewDirectorySync` and `ApplyDirectorySync` are on `Settings`, both server-admin gated, and there is a Control Panel page whose Apply button stays disabled until a preview of *the same options* has been run. **Nothing it does deletes an account or a message**; the most it does is clear `Active`. **A domain takes part only if its Active Directory domain name is set** - that field already exists, is already where an administrator records which directory domain a mail domain corresponds to, and requiring it makes provisioning opt-in per domain, so a search base pointed one level too high cannot provision into unrelated hosted domains. It also guarantees every created account carries a non-empty AD domain, which matters more than it looks: `SSPIValidation` passes the domain straight to `LogonUser`, and an EMPTY domain validates against this computer's own local Windows accounts - so an account provisioned with an empty domain and the user name "administrator" would start accepting the local Administrator password the day somebody set `Enabled=0`. **No password is stored and none is generated**: accounts are marked `IsAD` and authenticate through the directory from the moment they exist. **Four refusals are the substance of the feature, and each was tested rather than reasoned about.** Nothing is disabled when the enumeration was truncated, nor when it returned nothing at all, nor - per domain - when nothing in *that* domain was seen: all three are cases where an account that still exists is indistinguishable from one that was deleted, and the third was proved live by pointing one hMailServer domain at a directory the run never enumerated and confirming its account survived a run that correctly disabled a genuinely absent one. And an account that exists but is NOT directory-linked, or is linked to a different AD domain, is reported rather than taken over, because either would move who decides that mailbox's password. **The enumeration pages**, because Active Directory caps a search at `MaxPageSize` (1000) and answers a larger directory with the first page *and a success status* - a lab of eight users would never have shown it. **The service bind honours `BindMethod`**, so Negotiate works where no LDAPS certificate exists, which is the unjoined-DMZ case the whole feature is for. `Preferred()` reduces a multi-valued attribute deterministically rather than taking whichever value the directory happened to send first - LDAP guarantees no ordering, so `values.front()` let a re-run choose a different address for the same person, provisioning a second mailbox and making the first look deleted - and `proxyAddresses` works, its scheme stripped and its capitalised `SMTP:` primary preferred. An unattended schedule exists (`[LDAP] SyncScheduleMinutes`, 0 and off by default, clamped to 15 minutes .. 7 days) which runs on the maintenance work queue rather than the scheduler's single thread, and **creates and updates but never disables** - unattended is precisely where nobody is reading the report that would have shown a mailbox about to be taken away. Two adversarial review passes over code that had already run successfully against the live DC found seventeen defects between them, which is the honest measure of how little a clean `/W3 /WX` compile and a successful hand-run prove. |

The next generation: engine to service
--------------------------------------

Everything above this line is a mail *engine*, and a good one — the protocol and
transport-security work is ahead of the mainstream self-hosted field. What it has
never had is the surfaces people actually touch. You cannot read your mail with it
without installing something else, you cannot find a message without a linear scan,
and two people cannot share `info@` without sharing a password.

**The next generation is the same engine, with those surfaces built rather than
delegated.** That is the whole of it.

This section was designed in August 2026 by seven parallel designs against the real
tree — webmail, full-text search, the API, sharing, JMAP, groupware, and an
adversarial security review of all of it — then reconciled into one sequence. The
calls below are decisions, not options.

### The hard calls, and what they cost

**Webmail talks to the store, not to IMAP over a loopback socket.** An IMAP client
inside the server would reuse every existing access-control decision for free, which
is genuinely attractive. It is rejected because it would create a second instance of
the byte-fidelity defect already deferred in the 6.2.15 backlog, and because a
loopback protocol hop is a permanent tax on every request. **The mitigating clause
matters more than the decision:** each piece of logic extracted from `Server/IMAP/`
lands first as an *IMAP-only* refactor, proven by the existing IMAP tests, before any
web code is allowed to call it. That way the shared path is verified by the suite
that already exists rather than by the new code that needs it.

**REST first, JMAP-shaped, JMAP last — and droppable.** The JMAP-versus-REST framing
turned out to be wrong: roughly ninety of the API's endpoints are server
administration, which JMAP has nothing to say about, so a REST v2 is happening
regardless of what we decide about JMAP. For the contested mail-client half, REST
comes first but is deliberately built JMAP-shaped — the same entity names, opaque-id
semantics, and a per-id `/set` envelope — so that adding JMAP later is a translation
layer rather than a second server. JMAP itself goes last, and is the one phase that
can be dropped without the rest being a failure. Its own design conceded it must be
justified by what it does for our webmail; by the time we reach it the webmail
already has threading, keywords and push, so that justification has expired.

**A real HTTP server, on Boost.Asio.** Four of the seven designs proposed
hand-rolling HTTP/1.1. Boost 1.91 is already on the include path, so that is
rejected in favour of Beast: keep-alive, chunked encoding, `Range`, and multipart are
all things a mail client needs and none of them are things worth writing twice. It
also inherits TLS from `SslContextInitializer`, the security-range check at accept,
and the existing exception discipline — which is three of the prerequisites above
solved by construction rather than by remembering.

**No new compression dependency.** Precompressed sibling assets instead of linking
zlib. Cheaper, and it removes the BREACH question by absence rather than by
discipline — there is no dynamic compressor to leak a CSRF token through.

**Groupware before JMAP.** Contacts are what a webmail needs on day one for
compose auto-completion, and a calendar is what makes the product a plausible
Exchange alternative. Both serve users; JMAP serves an interoperability story that
nobody is currently asking for.

### Honest size

**Roughly 148 person-weeks of implementation — about three person-years.** For one
maintainer at realistic utilisation that is **four to five calendar years, and the
webmail alone is two of them.** That number is not padding and it is not
flinching; it is what the seven designs add up to when nobody is allowed to say
"and then the client part".

Two consequences, and they drive the ordering rather than being caveats to it:

1. **Every phase ends somewhere shippable.** No phase is a down payment that only
   pays off on completion, and each one can be abandoned at its boundary without
   what came before becoming dead code. A phase that fails that test is drawn
   wrong and gets redrawn.
2. **Something outranks all of it, and it already did.** The Microsoft 365
   Basic auth cutover was four months out when this section was written;
   outbound XOAUTH2 and OAuth2 POP3 collection shipped on 16 August 2026,
   before any phase here started. That is the rule working, not an exception
   to it — dated external deadlines beat architecture, every time, and
   the next one to bite is the Let's Encrypt lifetime drop in February 2027.
   See [Dated items](#dated-items--the-forcing-functions).

### The phases

| | Phase | What ships, and what a user can do afterwards that they cannot now |
|:-:|---|---|
| 🔄 | **0. Prerequisites** | The nine items in [Structural prerequisites](#structural-prerequisites), led by the crash oracle — because every gate after this assumes the suite can see a crash, and until 12 August 2026 it could not. Nothing user-visible. This is the phase that is tempting to skip and must not be. **Seven of the nine are closed:** the crash oracle is armed and checked before and after every test, `ManageSieveServer` has a barrier, every TLS listener in the server now takes its configuration from `SslContextInitializer` (so `SSL_CTX_new` appears nowhere), the SQL substitution fallback no longer corrupts queries — and closed a live SQL-injection path through a mail header while it was being fixed — and `IMAP SEARCH` is bounded. Fuzzing closed too, on the same day and last deliberately — it was the one item genuinely worth waiting for the oracle, because a fuzzer that cannot tell when it has found something is just a load generator — and it repaid the wait immediately by finding undefined behaviour in the MIME parser that every message with a charset and a transfer encoding was reaching. The ignored return values and NUL truncations closed on 13 August 2026, counted first rather than estimated: nineteen of them, the worst of which accepted a truncated message with a 250. **Remaining: the single authorisation choke point, and the web-shaped concurrency model — which is Phase 1's foundation.** |
| ⬜ | **1. HTTP foundation** | A real HTTP/1.1 server on Boost.Asio, with `RestApiServer` and `WebServicesServer` re-hosted on it and their raw accept loops deleted. User-visible outcome: the existing admin API and web services get keep-alive, correct caching, ACME certificate hot-reload and the same TLS hardening as the mail protocols. Modest on its own, and it is what makes everything after it possible. |
| ✅ | **2. Full-text search — shipped 21 Aug 2026, out of order** | Delivered on schema 6023 and off by default (`IndexerFullText`). `SEARCH BODY`/`TEXT` no longer loads and substring-scans every message in the folder; a term index first proves which messages *cannot* match. The narrowness is the design — **the index can never say yes, only prove no** — because IMAP BODY search is substring matching and a term index cannot confirm that "ell" occurs in a message. Every surviving candidate is still read and scanned exactly as before, so results are identical with the index on and off. Like the XOAUTH2 work noted above, this arrived before phase 1 rather than after it: it needed no HTTP foundation, and it improves `SEARCH` for every existing IMAP client with no webmail in sight — which was always the argument for scheduling it early. |
| ⬜ | **3. REST v2 and the self-service portal** | The full API — mailboxes, folders, messages, submission, attachments, search, settings, filters — plus browser sessions, and the smallest possible web surface on top: password change, vacation, quota. Afterwards: a user can change their own password without an administrator. That alone retires a permanent support burden. |
| ✅ | **4. Shared and delegated mailboxes — shipped 21 Aug 2026, out of order** | The IMAP other-users namespace and per-folder ACL evaluation through the single authorisation choke point, delivered ahead of the HTTP foundation it was scheduled behind — for the same reason full-text search was: it needed none of it, and it improves every existing IMAP client. Three people can now handle `info@` with their own credentials and their own second factor instead of a shared password. **Send-As is not part of it** and keeps its own row: submission authorises the authenticated account and nothing consults the ACL when choosing an envelope sender. |
| ⬜ | **5. Webmail** | The flagship, and two calendar years of the estimate. An SPA over the REST API, with the message body rendered in a unique opaque origin so that no HTML is ever generated in C++. Afterwards: read and send mail from a browser, with nothing else installed. |
| ⬜ | **6. Contacts, then calendaring** | CardDAV first because compose auto-completion needs it and users notice it immediately; then CalDAV with the WebDAV/ACL/sync-collection layer beneath it, and **iMIP**, which is the only standards-based bidirectional path to Exchange and Microsoft 365. |
| ⏸️ | **7. JMAP** | Core plus Mail. Deferred, deliberately last, and explicitly droppable — see the hard calls above. |

### What is still refused, under the higher bar

"A third party already does it" is no longer a reason. These survive that:

* **Exchange ActiveSync and EWS.** Both proprietary; EAS is patent-encumbered.
  **EWS has a published death date** — tenant-by-tenant disablement from 1
  October 2026, permanent shutdown 1 April 2027 — and building toward something
  its owner is switching off is not a judgement call. EAS has no retirement
  date, and saying it does would be the sort of error a reader checks and then
  distrusts the rest of this page over; what it has is a narrowing, with
  protocol versions below 16.1 blocked from Exchange Online since March 2026
  and direct certificate-based authentication gone by the end of 2026. Neither
  is a protocol to start implementing in 2026.
* **LMTP.** Only matters when another MTA fronts this one, which is not a
  realistic Windows deployment.
* **Active/active clustering.** Dovecot removed Director and its replicator
  outright and now documents its community edition as single-server; HA moved to
  the commercial product. Multi-node is no longer part of the open-source baseline,
  so its absence is not a gap. Warm standby, documented and tested, is the honest
  deliverable.
* **An embedded browser control in the Control Panel.** The WebView2 SDK licence
  prohibits distribution in a way that would subject it to copyleft, which is
  exactly what shipping it inside an AGPLv3 application does.

Structural prerequisites
-----------------------

**These are not features, and they are not optional.** Every client-facing item in
the next generation lands on top of the optional HTTP listeners and the SQL layer,
and neither is currently built to carry it. Shipping a web application on the
present foundation would be precisely the half-measure this programme exists to
avoid.

*Corrected 12 August 2026.* An earlier version of this section claimed all four
