The sweep only ever removes `.eml` files, so anything else in the archive is left where it is; it refuses to follow symlinks and junctions, because it deletes things and the archive is a directory somebody else controls; and it removes per-user folders that its own deletions emptied, which is what stops the archive turning into a tree of empty directories after a few years of staff turnover. **Still open**: per-domain scope, an index, immutability and legal hold. Searchable eDiscovery still depends on full-text search. |
| ✅ | **THREAD (RFC 5256)** | **This row was stale and is corrected on 20 August 2026: THREAD is implemented, and has been.** `IMAPThread.cpp` implements **both** algorithms the RFC defines — `REFERENCES` (the one Thunderbird uses) and `ORDEREDSUBJECT` — `CAPABILITY` advertises `THREAD=ORDEREDSUBJECT THREAD=REFERENCES`, `UID THREAD` is wired, and the untagged-EXPUNGE rule the RFC carries over from SORT is honoured. Thirteen regression tests cover it, including the parts that are easy to get wrong and impossible to notice: two replies to a **missing** parent threading together under a dummy node rather than as two strangers, a reply with no `References` falling back to base-subject matching, the search criteria bounding the tree so an excluded message does not appear even when another message references it, and a reply chain deeper than the depth cap being answered instead of taking the server down. The row was written from the assumption that SORT and THREAD would have arrived together and only SORT had; they did arrive together. |
| ✅ | **LDAP/Active Directory as a directory backend** | Done — see the row of the same name under *Cross-cutting and platform*. Preview and apply, a Control Panel page, and an optional unattended schedule that creates and updates but never disables. |
| ✅ | **Publish the architecture guide** | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) — the module map, the patterns that matter (the COM seam, layered persistence, four backends behind one abstraction, INI-vs-database settings, and why the optional listeners deliberately sit outside Boost.Asio *and therefore have no exception barrier and their own `SSL_CTX`*), a "where does this change go" table, and the constraints that cost real releases: every wait on a pooled thread needs a ceiling; an idle timeout is not a ceiling because it re-arms on every byte; distinguish "the answer is no" from "there was no answer"; `shared_from_this()` throws in a constructor; a diagnostic must not fire on the shipped default. |
| 🔄 | **Static-analysis backlog** | First `/analyze` pass done — three buffer overruns, a data race, NULL dereferences and a log-corrupting shadowed variable fixed. The remainder needs triage rather than blanket suppression. |
| ✅ | **Virus-scanner timeout policy — shipped 21 Aug 2026; the row overstated the work** | The configurable choice this row asked for already existed: `AVFailAction` decides what happens to a message the scanner could not examine — 0 delivers it anyway, 1 holds it on its own `AVFailRetryMinutes` schedule for at most `AVFailMaxHolds` attempts and then bounces, with the reasoning for both bounds written at `SMTPDeliverer::HandleUnscannableMessage_`. A killed scanner already routes into that policy as an error result. What was actually broken was one line of diagnosis: `ProcessLauncher::Launch` returns false for two opposite reasons — `CreateProcess` failed (a path the administrator must fix) or the process ran past `ExternalProcessTimeout` and was terminated (the scanner being slow) — and both custom-scanner call sites reported *either* as "Unable to launch executable", sending an operator to check a path that was perfectly correct. `Launch` now surfaces a `FailureReason`, and the CustomVirusScanner and ClamWin paths report the timeout as a timeout. The policy was never the gap; the error message was. |
| ⏸️ | **Backup scheduling in the GUI** | The scheduler itself now exists — BackupScheduleTask, with daily and interval modes, escalating back-off, destination pre-flight and retention — but it is configured only through hMailServer.ini (ScheduledBackupTime, ScheduledBackupIntervalMinutes, ScheduledBackupKeepCount, ScheduledBackupMaxAgeDays). What is still deferred is the Control Panel surface for it. **The reason given here — "needs the settings to move out of the ini and into the COM API first" — no longer holds:** `Settings.GetIniSetting`/`SetIniSetting` reach the `[Settings]` section over COM as of 14 August 2026, so all four scheduling values are now readable and writable from a Control Panel on another machine. What remains is only the page itself, and the one design question it has to answer: the four settings express two mutually exclusive modes (a daily time, or an interval in minutes) plus retention, and a card that shows both at once without saying which is in force would be a worse answer than the ini. |
| ⏸️ | **IMAP FETCH byte fidelity** | `FETCH` reconstructs parts rather than returning original octets. Semantically correct, no client known to be affected; a rework would be a large change to a heavily used path, so it waits for a real reported problem. |

Future-proofing: standards and protocols
----------------------------------------

| | Item | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | **Post-quantum key exchange — shipped 12 Aug 2026** | `SslContextInitializer` calls `SSL_CTX_set1_curves_list(ssl, "secp384r1:x25519:secp256r1")` unconditionally at context init. That **replaces** OpenSSL's default group list, which in 3.5+/4.x already contains `X25519MLKEM768`. So every SMTP, IMAP, POP3 and ManageSieve listener negotiated classical-only key exchange despite linking a PQC-capable library. Fixed: the list is now the setting `TlsKeyExchangeGroups`, defaulting to `X25519MLKEM768:SecP256r1MLKEM768:X25519:secp384r1:secp256r1`, with a fallback to the classical list if OpenSSL rejects the configured value — getting *that* wrong was the one way this change could have taken TLS down everywhere, so it is the part the regression tests cover hardest. **Scope note, updated 12 August 2026:** this now reaches every TLS listener in the server — SMTP, POP3, IMAP, ManageSieve, the REST API, the Web Services HTTPS listener and outbound delivery — because the two that built their own `SSL_CTX` were moved onto `SslContextInitializer`; see [Structural prerequisites](#structural-prerequisites). The metrics listener is not in the list because it serves plain HTTP and has no TLS at all. |
| ✅ | **DMARCbis: organizational domains by DNS tree walk — shipped 20 Aug 2026** | DMARCbis is now published — RFC 9989, 9990 and 9991, obsoleting 7489 — and its section 4.10 replaces the Public Suffix List with a walk up the DNS: query `_dmarc.<name>` at each level, stop at a `psd=` tag, and select the organizational domain from what was found (4.10.2: `psd=n` **is** the boundary; `psd=y` anywhere but the starting domain puts it one label below; otherwise the fewest-labels record). `DmarcTreeWalk` implements it, `DMARC::GetOrganizationalDomain` consults it first, and the PSL stays underneath as the `DmarcTreeWalkEnabled=0` escape hatch **and** as the answer whenever a lookup fails transiently — a resolver outage must not silently turn relaxed alignment into strict. Bounded at eight queries per domain (4.10 step 4) and cached for five minutes, because DMARC needs an org domain for the author domain *and* every identifier aligned against it, so uncached this would be two dozen lookups a message. Two records at one name discard **both**, per 4.10 steps 2 and 6 — guessing would let anyone weaken a policy by publishing a second one beside it. The 4.10.2 selection rules are pinned by vectors in `DmarcTreeWalkTester` because they are pure and they are where a wrong answer becomes a wrong *policy* decision; the end-to-end fixture `DmarcTreeWalkResolution` publishes a zone through `FakeDnsServer` and asserts each verdict twice — once with the walk on and once with it off, because a case both mechanisms get right proves nothing about which one ran. **Not** in scope here: the DMARCbis 2.0 aggregate-report namespace, split out to its own row below. |
| ✅ | **DMARCbis `np=`: the policy for subdomains that do not exist — shipped 20 Aug 2026** | RFC 9989 5.5.4. `p=` covers the domain and `sp=` covers its subdomains, and between them they left the cheapest forgery there is uncovered: a phisher does not need a subdomain to be delegated to them, only a From header that says `accounts.thebank.test` — and no domain owner can publish a record for a name they have never heard of. `np=` now applies to exactly that case, ahead of `sp=`, because it is the more specific statement. **The tag rests entirely on NXDOMAIN versus NODATA**, and getting that wrong rejects real mail: a subdomain holding only an MX, only a TXT, or merely something beneath it exists, and applying `np=reject` — usually the strictest policy the domain publishes — to those would hit real parts of a sender's own estate. So `DNSResolver::DomainExists` was added to tell the two apart, which nothing here could do before: every existing lookup treats "absent" and "no records of this type" as the same unusable answer, so the resolver now surfaces the RCODE. Fails **closed against the resolver rather than the sender**: a lookup that cannot be completed answers "it exists", so a DNS outage cannot turn `np=reject` loose on subdomains that do. `FakeDnsServer` gained `WithNxDomain`, because a fixture for this cannot be written against a server that only knows how to say NODATA — and the NODATA half of the test is the more important one. The parsed value is also carried on the evaluation for the aggregate reporter, which needs it for `policy_published` in the 2.0 schema below. |
| ✅ | **DMARCbis 2.0 aggregate-report namespace — shipped 21 Aug 2026, behind a setting that defaults old** | The other half of the tree-walk row. `DmarcRptSchemaVersion=2` emits the RFC 9990 form: the `urn:ietf:params:xml:ns:dmarc-2.0` namespace with — and this is the trap the whole item hides — **`<version>` still `1.0`**, which 3.1.1.2 states as a MUST and the Appendix B sample confirms; the 2.0 versions the schema, not the report. `policy_published` drops `pct`, gains `np` (which only this week's `np=` work could feed), `testing`, and `discovery_method` — reporting which mechanism *actually answered* for that day's first message, carried out of the evaluator rather than read from configuration, because the PSL fallback on a transient DNS failure makes those two differ. `policy_evaluated/disposition` gains `pass`, derived from the two aligned verdicts beside it rather than stored, so it can never disagree with them. The required `<selector>` is emitted empty when a signature's `s=` was unreadable — dropping the whole `<dkim>` would discard the `d=`, the more useful half. **The default stays 1 (RFC 7489 Appendix C), and must**: almost nothing parses 2.0 yet, and a report the receiving domain cannot read is indistinguishable from no report. The 7489 output is byte-for-byte what it was. Pure-XML vectors pin both forms in the C++ self-tests; `DmarcRpt2Schema.cs` pins the setting end to end, including that the default has not moved. |
| ✅ | **ACME renewal robustness and ARI — shipped 20 Aug 2026** | Two halves, both now done. **The renewal window is a fraction of the lifetime, not a fixed thirty days.** Thirty was right while certificates lasted ninety and stops being right the moment they do not, which is scheduled rather than speculative: Let's Encrypt defaults to 64 days from February 2027, the maximum falls to 100 days in March 2027 and to **47** in March 2029. A fixed 30-day window against a 47-day certificate begins renewing seventeen days after issuance and never stops — that is how an operator gets rate-limited by their own CA. Renewal now begins two thirds through whatever lifetime the certificate has, with a one-day floor so a failure always has room for another hourly attempt. **ARI (RFC 9773)** then lets the CA move that: the directory's `renewalInfo` endpoint is consulted every hour with the certificate's ARI identifier, and its suggested window wins when offered — which is the point, because a CA needs to be able to pull renewals forward during a mass revocation and a client that asked once at issuance would never hear about it. **Three things were designed rather than transcribed.** A point is chosen *inside* the suggested window rather than at its start, since renewing at `start` just moves the thundering herd; it is derived from the certificate's own identifier so the answer is stable across the hourly re-checks (a fresh roll each hour drifts renewals towards the start) and differs between servers. ARI is clamped to a day before expiry whatever it says, because it is advisory and the certificate is not. And a CA without ARI, a failed request or an unreadable answer all mean "decide for yourself", never "do not renew" — which is most CAs today, so that is the normal path. **The identifier is pinned against RFC 9773's own worked example**, and that caught a real bug before it shipped: the serial must be its DER content octets, which carry a leading zero when the top bit is set (`0x8765432101` — `00 87 65 43 21`), while the bytes OpenSSL stores do not. It would have been wrong for about half of all certificates, and the only symptom would have been a 404 and a silent fall back — which is to say nobody would ever have noticed. |
| ✅ | **Legacy algorithm audit (RFC 9905)** | **Swept 20 August 2026, and the sweep found two things worth fixing, both now shipped.** (1) **DKIM `rsa-sha1`**: RFC 8301 removed it from DKIM in 2018 — a signer MUST NOT use it, a verifier MUST treat a signature that does as invalid — and this server did both. It now does neither: a signature using it is refused, and a domain configured to sign with it is signed `rsa-sha256` instead and told so, because that configuration means "sign my mail" rather than "sign it weakly". (2) **DKIM keys below 1024 bits**: nothing checked, so a domain publishing a 512-bit key — several did for years, because a smaller key fits more comfortably in a TXT record — had its signatures verified as normal. A 512-bit RSA key is factorable in hours on rented hardware, after which anyone can sign as that domain and pass DMARC alignment with a signature this server called valid. The `rsa-sha1` half is behind `DkimAcceptSha1`; the key floor is not, and is not negotiable. **What the sweep cleared**, so it need not be re-audited: no RC4, DES, 3DES, IDEA or SEED anywhere; the TLS cipher policy already ships an `AEAD-ONLY` preset excluding every CBC and HMAC-SHA1 suite; certificate digests already substitute SHA-256 when a certificate is signed with MD5 or SHA-1; `PreferredHashAlgorithm` defaults to PBKDF2 with Argon2id available, and MD5-hashed passwords are re-hashed on the next successful logon rather than merely tolerated. **Deliberately left**: TOTP's HMAC-SHA1, which RFC 6238 and every authenticator app require — using SHA-256 there produces a QR code that scans and then never matches. **Both remaining questions were answered on 20 August 2026 and the row is closed.** (3) `PreferredHashAlgorithm` now has a floor. It names the scheme a *new* secret is stored under, and three of its six values are not password hashes at all — 0 is no hashing, 1 is reversible, 2 is MD5 — and two of the five places that read it hand the value straight to `Crypt` as the scheme to store an account or app password under. Anything below SHA256 is refused, PBKDF2 used instead, and the refusal **reported** rather than applied silently, because an administrator who set it deliberately is owed the reason it did not take. Clamped where the value is read rather than at each call site, so a call site added later inherits the floor instead of having to remember it. (4) The fetch and route credential stores were audited and are clean: route relayer passwords, fetch-account passwords, SSL private-key passwords and the new per-domain relay password all go through `Crypt::ProtectSecret` — machine-bound DPAPI, recoverable because the server presents them to another server, which is the right shape for a secret that cannot be hashed. No Blowfish and no plaintext remain in any credential store. |
| ✅ | **SPF void lookup limit — shipped 20 Aug 2026** | RFC 7208 4.6.4. A *void lookup* is a term of a sender's policy — an `a:`, `mx:` or `exists:` — whose DNS query comes back NXDOMAIN, or NOERROR with no answers. The evaluator already enforced the RFC's other limit, ten DNS-querying terms, and that bounds what a policy can *demand*; it does not bound what a policy can *waste*. Ten terms naming hosts that do not exist cost this server ten full resolutions, each a timeout away from the root, for a record that can never match — published against a domain an attacker can get mail addressed to, that is an amplifier pointed at the receiver. Now capped at two, per the RFC, with `SpfVoidLookupLimit` to raise it or set 0 to switch it off. Counted per **term** rather than per query, which is what 4.6.4 says: an `mx` whose hosts all fail to resolve is one void term, not one per host — over-counting would turn a working policy into a permerror and reject real mail, which is the error direction that matters. Both sides of the boundary are pinned in the server's self-tests, because a test that only checks three voids failing would pass on an implementation that rejects the *first* one, and that would refuse mail from every domain still listing a host it decommissioned. The vectors drive a **supplied** policy rather than a published one — the SPF library resolves through `DnsQuery` directly, not through this server's resolver, so the suite's `FakeDnsServer` cannot be put in front of it — and resolve their targets for real under `.invalid`, which RFC 2606 reserves so that it never resolves. One name across all the terms, after the first run measured the resolver's mood instead: three separate names produced two clean NXDOMAINs and one temporary failure. |
| ✅ | **Certificate expiry and queue-age metrics** | Both are things mail operators actually alert on, and the data is already there. Queue *depth* alone does not distinguish a burst from a stuck relay. |
| ⏸️ | **DKIM2** | Real momentum and the right backers. The charter said all documents to the IESG by 31 December 2025; eight months past that, the specification is still an Internet-Draft, nothing is in last call, and no revised schedule has been published. What has changed is that several independent implementations demonstrated interop in July 2026 — so the risk is no longer "this never happens" but "this happens on somebody else's timetable". Track; hedge only by keeping the signing path abstracted. |
| ⏸️ | **PQC signatures (ML-DSA) and PQC for DNSSEC/DANE** | 2028 at the earliest, and they arrive through OpenSSL and the ACME client rather than as code written here. No allocated DNSSEC algorithm and an unsolved packet-size problem. Track only. |

Future-proofing: platform and supply chain
------------------------------------------

| | Item | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | **Migrate to .NET 10 LTS** | Done 12 Aug 2026, ahead of the 10 Nov 2026 deadline. Nine projects to `net10.0-windows`; SDK 10.0.303 pinned in `global.json`; the bundled Desktop Runtime, the runtime-fetch script, the screenshot script and the docs all moved together. Two real findings on the way: .NET 9 added the **WFO1000** WinForms analyzer, which (correctly) rejected four properties on `ucText` whose designer-serialization intent was undeclared — the designer had been emitting meaningless `Number = 0` lines for years; and `System.DirectoryServices` is now in the Windows Desktop shared framework, so that `PackageReference` was redundant and is gone. **`MinVersion` stays at 10.0.14393** — .NET 10 still supports Windows 10 1607 and Server 2012, which was worth checking rather than assuming. **Correction, 13 Aug 2026:** the installer's shared-framework probe did *not* move with the rest of it, and this row said it had. The edit meant to produce `Microsoft.WindowsDesktop.App\10.*` wrote `Microsoft.WindowsDesktop.App` followed by a raw `0x08` byte — a `\10` read as an octal escape. A glob containing a control character matches nothing, so the probe answered "runtime missing" unconditionally and the bundled installer ran twice on every install. Invisible in every editor, diff and review; found by reading the bytes. Fixed, with the version now defined once (`DOTNET_MAJOR`) and both the version agreement and the absence of control characters asserted by `InstallerPackaging.cs`. |
| ✅ | **.NET target policy, written down** | Audited every project in the tree, not just the shipping ones. **All six shipped artefacts and the Control Panel publish as `net10.0`** — verified from their `runtimeconfig.json`, so nothing reaching a user is on .NET 8. The rest is deliberate, and now recorded so it is a decision rather than drift: the **regression suite and its three companions stay on .NET Framework 4.8.1**, which has *no end date* (Component Lifecycle Policy — supported as long as its host Windows) and drives the server over COM interop, so moving it to .NET 10 would be a large, risky change to the release gate for no benefit. **Six projects still on .NET Framework 4.5 — support ended 13 January 2016 — were bumped to 4.8.1**; they are dormant upstream dev tools referenced by no script, and since the 4.5 targeting pack is absent from a modern VS install they could not build at all before. **Seven projects remain older still**: five on .NET Framework 3.5 SP1 (supported to 10 January 2029), and two — `MemoryTests` and `TestInvalidConnections` — that declare no `TargetFramework` at all and carry Visual Studio 2005 markers (`ProductVersion 8.0.50727`, `ToolsVersion 3.5`, `OldToolsVersion 2.0`). All seven are dormant, referenced by no solution the build touches, and buildable by nobody today. Modernising code nothing exercises is a decision to take deliberately rather than blind; retiring them is the more honest option and is worth a separate look. |
| ⬜ | **Decide the OpenSSL branch** | 4.0.x is not LTS and dies 14 May 2027, and the successor is now dated: the release policy puts an LTS in April of every odd-numbered year, so the next lands April 2027, a month before 4.0.x expires. Two honest options — take that LTS on release and accept a one-month overlap, or step back to 3.5 LTS (EOL 8 Apr 2030) and revisit once. The default should be the former; the latter is the answer if anything in the 4.x line has proved unstable by then. |
| ⬜ | **Declare a supported Windows floor** | Server 2019 / Windows 10 21H2, effective with the first release after 12 Jan 2027. Zero code cost — Server 2019 is still `_WIN32_WINNT=0x0A00`, and that macro should *not* be raised. |
| 🔄 | **Artifact signing** | **Sigstore half done.** [`sign-release.yml`](.github/workflows/sign-release.yml) signs every release asset with cosign, keyless via OIDC -- deliberately keyless, because for a single-maintainer project a long-lived signing key stored in Actions is itself the thing most worth stealing. The signature is verified in the same job, so a malformed bundle cannot reach a user. **Authenticode is still open, and the two are complementary rather than alternatives:** SmartScreen and the UAC prompt care about Authenticode and not about Sigstore, so cosign gives verifiability to anyone who checks while Windows still shows an unknown-publisher warning. Azure Trusted Signing is the realistic route. |
| ✅ | **security.txt (RFC 9116)** | Served at `/.well-known/security.txt` by the web services listener, with a derived (never hardcoded) `Expires`, a `Policy` pointing at `SECURITY.md`, and a deliberate refusal to serve anything at all when no contact is configured — a placeholder address is worse than no file. Note it inherits the listener caveat above: the web services ports default to 0, and the server now says so at startup. |
| ✅ | **OpenSSF Scorecard** | [`scorecard.yml`](.github/workflows/scorecard.yml), weekly and on push, publishing SARIF to code scanning so supply-chain posture sits beside the CodeQL findings. Deliberately **not** published to the public OpenSSF badge API -- there is no reason to send repository telemetry to a third party to obtain a number we can read ourselves. Expect Branch-Protection and Code-Review to fail by design on a single-maintainer repository; the actionable checks are Pinned-Dependencies, Token-Permissions and Dangerous-Workflow. OSPS Baseline remains open. **Verified working on 13 August 2026, and the history is the point:** for its first six runs this workflow published nothing at all. It referenced `ossf/scorecard-action@v2`; that action ships only point tags and has no moving `v2`, so every run died in *Set up job* - which reads as infrastructure noise rather than a broken reference, and is how it sat green-adjacent in this table claiming to publish SARIF while never having done so. Now pinned to a full commit SHA (v2.4.4) and confirmed by result, not by a green tick: **71 findings in code scanning** across three SARIF submissions. Its first real output was immediately actionable - Pinned-Dependencies scored 0, so all 24 GitHub Action references in the nine workflows are now SHA-pinned with the version in a trailing comment, each at its existing major so that pinning smuggled in no version bumps. **47 alerts were then worked through one at a time on 13 August 2026.** Security-Policy is fixed (4/10 -> 10/10: the policy's only link was relative, and the check wants a literal `http(s)://`). Maintained is not actionable and self-clears on 10 September 2026. Code-Review and Branch-Protection remain structural, but less so than assumed - this is an *organization* repository with a second collaborator at read access, so Code-Review is a permission change away from being honest rather than impossible. Binary-Artifacts was the interesting one: not "the vendored build dependencies" as this row used to say, but 40 files of which **13 are dead weight** - ten byte-identical duplicates and three that nothing loads at all. See **Committed binary provenance** below. The lesson recorded here rather than learned twice: a tick box nobody checked against a run is worth less than no tick box, because it stops anyone looking. |
| ✅ | **Committed binary provenance** | Every binary file in the repository - 40 of them - is inventoried in [`third-party-binaries.json`](hmailserver/docs/third-party-binaries.json) with a SHA-256, an upstream, a version, a licence, what loads it and why it is in the tree, explained per component in [ThirdPartyBinaries.md](hmailserver/docs/ThirdPartyBinaries.md). [`verify-binary-provenance.yml`](.github/workflows/verify-binary-provenance.yml) fails the build if a committed binary changes hash, disappears, or if one appears that the inventory does not describe - turning an unreviewable `Binary files differ` into a reviewable text diff. Its detector matches Scorecard's, extension set plus content sniff, because an extension-only scan finds 38 here and Scorecard finds 40 (`libraries/msado28/*.tlb` are PE images wearing a `.tlb` extension). What the inventory turned up is the point: **13 of the 40 are not dependencies at all.** Ten are byte-identical duplicates of each other (`installation/Extras/*` against `libraries/mariadb-connector-c-3.4.9/*`); `System Files/dnsapi.dll` is a **Windows 2000** DLL that no `.iss` and no build step references; `isxdl.dll` is an unsigned HTTP downloader packed into the installer whose eight exports are all declared and none called; `SSCERuntime_x86-ENU.msi` is 8.7 MB the x64-only installer never stages. Also recorded honestly: 12 of 40 carry a valid Authenticode signature and 28 do not, and `ISC.dll` - unsigned, no version resource, no publisher, no upstream, and executed during installation - is the one binary here whose provenance cannot be recorded because there is nothing to record. Replacing it is open. |
| ⏸️ | **SQL Server Compact** | Long dead upstream and the one real dependency liability. Migration targets are SQLite or LocalDB. Deferred because it is a data-migration project for existing installs, not a swap, and no date forces it. **The case stopped being theoretical on 13 August 2026** - three defects found that day are specific to this backend, and it is the installer's DEFAULT, so they land on the installations least likely to have a DBA. `SQLCEConnection::BeginTransaction` returns true without starting a transaction, its own comment reading "SQL Server Compact does not support transactions, despite the fact that it's documented that they do" - so every transaction the server believes it has on the default backend is a no-op, which is half the reason the referential-integrity row cannot move. The DAL's `[IGNORE-ERRORS]` marker was matched anywhere in a statement and then discarded every error, on a backend whose DDL has no `IF NOT EXISTS` form and therefore relies on that marker. And `SqlLogDevice` sent `PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED` to a provider that does not accept it, which failed silently because of the marker above - so the SQL log device has been running without a primary key on `hm_log` for as long as that code has existed. None of these are hard to fix individually, and all three are the same shape: a backend nobody upstream maintains, whose divergences are discovered one at a time by the people running it. **The migration route for existing installations already exists and was traced on 13 August 2026, which changes what this deferral is waiting for.** Backup and restore are backend-agnostic by construction, not by intent: neither `BackupExecuter` nor `BackupRestorer` branches on database type, the archive is XML plus the message files, and - the part that makes it work - **the XML carries no identity values**. `Account::XMLStore` writes Name, Password and the rest and no accountid, `XMLLoad` sets none, so `SaveObject` sees `GetID() == 0`, inserts, and lets the new database assign its own identities; references are restored through the object graph (accounts under their domain, messages under their account) rather than by raw id. Nothing identity-shaped crosses the archive boundary, which is exactly why it can cross a backend boundary. So the customer path is: back up on SQL CE, point the server at the new database, restore - no tool to write. What was missing was not capability but **evidence**, and that gap was closed the same day. `docs\MigratingDatabaseBackend.md` is the runbook - the procedure, the verification list, rollback, and the `BackupMessagesDBOnly` mode that migrates the message *rows* while leaving a large mail store untouched on disk, which is the difference between minutes and most of a day. `RegressionTests\Infrastructure\BackendPortability.cs` is the test, and it is the honest version of one: the suite runs against a single backend, so it cannot perform a real cross-backend restore. What it does instead is exercise the half that carries the risk. It delivers a message, forces every identity in the database to be reassigned by deleting the whole domain tree and restoring it, and then proves the message is still found - same path on disk, byte for byte, readable end to end over POP3, with the alias and the distribution list still delivering to the reassigned account. That reassignment is deterministic rather than incidental: all four backends allocate from counters that do not go backwards when rows are deleted, so the restored rows cannot land on the numbers the deleted ones held. A different backend does nothing else differently here, which is what makes the substitution fair. What remains genuinely untested is the target-side schema create, which is DBSetup's job and is covered separately by `InstallerPackaging.cs`. |
| ⏸️ | **Boost and C++ standard upgrades** | No forcing function. Move when there is a reason. |

Future-proofing: deployment and operations
------------------------------------------

| | Item | Detail |
|:-:|---|---|
| ✅ | **Document the silent-install contract** | Done — see [README](README.md#unattended-install). Two limitations are now written down rather than discovered: the administrator password cannot be set on the command line at all, and a silent *upgrade* therefore cannot supply it either. Originally: The Inno script *already* branches on `WizardSilent()` and forwards credentials to `DBSetupQuick`, and already checks both `Exec` success and the child exit code so a failed schema upgrade cannot masquerade as success. None of that is documented, so an admin automating a hundred installs has to read the Pascal. Publish the switches and exit codes. Cheapest high-value win on this list. |
| ⬜ | **Migration *into* the server** | The biggest genuine opportunity here and largely unexploited: with Microsoft turning off Basic auth and EWS, people are moving. Partly wrong as previously written, and worth correcting: an **mbox route already ships**. `ImportTool` streams mbox files with `MboxParser`, stages each message into the server's own `{account}\XY\{GUID}.eml` layout and hands it to `Utilities.ImportMessageFromFileToIMAPFolder`, deleting the staged file if the server refuses it; it installs to `{app}\Addons\ImportTool` with a Start-menu entry, and text-file account import ships alongside it. What is genuinely absent is IMAP-to-IMAP (the `imapsync` / `doveadm import` equivalent), PST, Maildir, and any documentation at all — which is why this stays open. |
| ✅ | **Configuration as code** | [`build/hmconfig.ps1`](build/hmconfig.ps1) -- `export`, `diff` and `apply` the whole configuration as reviewable JSON, over the COM API so it can never disagree with the rules the server itself enforces. Two design decisions worth knowing: **`apply` is a dry run without `-Force`, and deletions additionally require `-AllowDelete`**, because deleting an account deletes its mail and a stale checkout, a partial export or a mistyped domain must not be able to do that -- additive and update operations are the default precisely because they cannot lose mail. And **passwords are never exported**: the server stores hashes, a hash is not a password, and a config file that silently reset every mailbox password on apply would be far worse than one that cannot set them at all. New accounts get a random password that must be reset. |
| ✅ | **Prometheus naming fixes** | All five are done. `hmailserver_state` is the OpenMetrics StateSet layout — one boolean series per state, so an alert reads `hmailserver_state{state="running"} == 0` instead of carrying its own copy of a 1/2/3/4 mapping (the declared type stays `gauge` on purpose: the 0.0.4 text parser rejects an unrecognised type name and would discard the whole scrape). `hmailserver_uptime_seconds` was replaced by `hmailserver_start_time_seconds` as a Unix timestamp, so a restart is a step change `changes()` detects rather than a reset indistinguishable from a scrape gap. `hmailserver_build_info` carries version, architecture and database schema version as labels with the value 1. Both latency families are histograms with cumulative buckets. `hmailserver_database_up` became `hmailserver_database_connected`, which no longer sits next to Prometheus's own synthetic `up` making a scrape failure look like a database failure — and, since August 2026, actually means the database answered a round trip rather than that the pool holds connection objects. |
| ✅ | **Per-domain metric labels — shipped 20 Aug 2026** | Every counter was server-wide, which is what stopped metrics becoming reporting: an operator could see how much mail the server handled, never how much any one domain did — and on a box hosting other people's domains that is the only question anyone asks. `hmailserver_domain_messages_received_total` and `..._sent_total` now carry a `domain=` label, behind `MetricsPerDomainEnabled`. **The reason this had not been done is cardinality**, and it is the part that needed designing rather than the counters. Every label value is a separate time series in whatever scrapes this, and the obvious implementation — label the sender domain of each message — lets anyone on the internet create series by sending mail. So the label set is bounded **by construction**: only domains this server hosts are ever labelled, which makes the series count equal to the number of domains the operator configured. That is why there is no cap to tune and no "other" bucket to explain, and the fixture's second test is the one that proves it — mail from an unhosted domain must create no series at all. A message to four people at one domain counts once for that domain, not four times, so the number agrees with everything else an operator can see. Off by default, because volume is a cost the operator should choose. **Not** in scope: per-ACCOUNT labels, which are unbounded by construction in a way domains are not — that one really would need a cap and a truncation story. |
| ✅ | **Ship Grafana dashboard JSON in the repo** | [`hmailserver/docs/grafana-dashboard.json`](hmailserver/docs/grafana-dashboard.json) — 15 panels under four rows, built against the metric names the exporter actually publishes rather than the ones it should. The panel descriptions state where the exporter is weak, so the dashboard does not quietly imply a tail-latency signal that is not there. |
| ✅ | **GDPR-shaped features — shipped 21 Aug 2026, the same day they were scoped** | Not because the project is a controller or processor — it is neither — but because operators are. Two COM methods, deliberately separate operations. **`Account.ExportMessages(directory)`** walks the folder tree and copies every message to `.eml` files mirroring the folder structure — folder names sanitised, since an IMAP name can contain every character Windows refuses — and **fails rather than reporting a partial export as success**, because a partial export handed to a person exercising an access right is exactly the wrong artefact. **`Utilities.EraseAddressTraces(address, includeArchive)`** removes what account deletion deliberately leaves: quarantine entries (via the store, so the file goes with the row), message-trace rows, greylisting triplets, distribution-list memberships, aliases naming the address as either half, and optionally the per-user archive tree. It works on an ADDRESS rather than an account, because the request usually arrives after the mailbox is already gone — and it is NOT part of deleting an account, because an archive can be under a legal hold that outranks a routine deletion, and "close this mailbox" and "remove this person" are different requests. Sweeps continue past a failed store and report what survived (HM6250); both methods are server-admin only; the alias and list caches are cleared so the erasure is effective immediately rather than at the next restart. Log retention already bounds the logs. **Deliberately out**: per-address redaction inside log lines (a log that can be selectively rewritten is worthless as a record), the archive's unattributed `Inbound` folder, and backups, which are outside the running system's reach by design. The fixture proves the second erasure pass finds nothing, which is the cheapest complete proof the first reached everything it counts. |
| 🔄 | **Warm-standby topology, documented and tested** | The document shipped 21 August 2026 — [`hmailserver/docs/WarmStandby.md`](hmailserver/docs/WarmStandby.md), verified claim by claim against the code the same day. It is organised around the three constraints that decide the shape: **exactly one service may run against the database** (both `UnlockAll`s at startup release everything a live twin holds mid-delivery, so a second server is actively destructive, not merely redundant); **DPAPI secrets do not travel** (`CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE` — so route, fetch and per-domain-relay passwords must be re-entered after every failover, in both directions, and the doc says to keep the list); and **the message store is replicated, not shared** — the SMB/CSV reasons this row asked for are argued from the accept path's local fsync, the per-recipient copy on the hot path, and a dead primary's file handles outliving it. The `hm_inisettings` mirror turns out to be the piece that makes a standby cheap: it inherits the primary's `[Settings]` on start, and deliberately not the per-machine sections. **The row stays underway because its second word is not yet true**: nothing here has rehearsed a failover on a real two-machine pair, and a standby that has never been started is a hope, not a topology — the doc's own words. The rehearsal needs a second Windows machine with a shared SQL backend; the Ubuntu lab VM cannot play either role. |
| 🔄 | **Backup verification** | The expectation has hardened from 3-2-1 to 3-2-1-1-0 — the trailing zero being "verified restores". Every backup now reads its own archive back through the exact code path a restore uses and checks the index describes what the run wrote, before retention is allowed to delete anything, so an archive that cannot be opened fails the backup and is discarded instead of displacing one that could be restored. That verifies the archive, not the mail inside it: a real verified restore — extract to a scratch directory and reconcile the message files against the rows — is still to do. |

Not planned
-----------

Saying no is part of a roadmap, and these are reasoned rather than reflexive.

| Item | Why not |
|---|---|
| **A rewrite**, in any language | This is upstream hMailServer with a current toolchain and a set of additions — 936 of 980 shared server source files are still byte-identical. That is the point of it. |
| **Removing the COM API** | It is how the Control Panel and every third-party script talk to the server. |
| **Matching upstream's dependency downgrades** | Deliberately ahead on OpenSSL, Boost and PostgreSQL. |
| **32-bit builds** | 64-bit only. |
| **Active/active clustering** | The ground moved here: Dovecot 2.4 **removed** Director and the replicator outright and now documents CE as single-server, with HA moved to the commercial product. Multi-node is no longer part of the open-source baseline, so its absence is not a gap. Warm standby is the right deliverable. |
| **Windows containers** | Base image sizes, the GUI tooling problem and thin adoption make this a poor fit, and no competitor does it on Windows. |
| **ActiveSync and EWS** | Both proprietary; EAS is patent-encumbered. EWS has had no feature work since 2018 and Microsoft has published its retirement — tenant-by-tenant disablement from 1 October 2026, permanent shutdown 1 April 2027. **EAS has no published retirement date**, which is worth stating precisely rather than borrowing EWS's: what it has is a narrowing, versions below 16.1 blocked from Exchange Online since March 2026 and direct certificate-based authentication retired by the end of 2026. |
| **IMAP NOTIFY (RFC 5465)** | Dovecot *does* implement it, so this is a real gap rather than an imagined one — but a low-value one, because what people want it for does not work anyway. iOS Mail does no push for generic IMAP at all ("If Push isn't available as a setting, your account will default to Fetch"), and `XAPPLEPUSHSERVICE` needs an APNs certificate gated on owning discontinued macOS Server. IDLE is the portable answer and NOTIFY would not change what any mobile client does. |
| **BIMI / VMC** | Never became an RFC after seven years. A sender-brand feature rendered by webmail UIs; the server's role is header pass-through, which already works. |
| **REQUIRETLS (RFC 8689)** | Near-zero deployment, and it converts delivery failures into hard bounces. The `TLS-Required: No` header half is the only useful part. |
| **LMTP (RFC 2033)** | Only matters when another MTA fronts this one, which is not a realistic Windows deployment. |
| **Multi-tenancy** | The per-domain model already covers the realistic cases. |
| **Embedded browser UI** | The WebView2 SDK is under a proprietary EULA that prohibits distribution "in ways that would subject it to copyleft licensing" — incompatible with AGPLv3. The runtime shipping in Windows does not help; the redistributed SDK assemblies are the problem. |
| **"Quantum-safe email" as a claim** | With no PQ signature story for DKIM and no PQ DNSSEC, only the transport hop can be made quantum-safe. Claiming more than "PQ hybrid key exchange on all TLS listeners" would be dishonest. |

Relationship with upstream
--------------------------

The original project is largely dormant but not dead, and real fixes do still
land there. A scheduled job compares this fork against upstream monthly and opens
a tracking issue when anything new appears; the baseline it compares from is in
[`.github/upstream-sync`](.github/upstream-sync), along with the reasoning for
the two upstream changes this fork deliberately does not take.

As of the last review, nothing upstream is missing here.

Influencing this list
---------------------

A concrete report with a log beats a feature request. Bugs and enhancements go to
[Issues](https://github.com/Progressiverobot/hmailserver/issues), open-ended
questions to
[Discussions](https://github.com/Progressiverobot/hmailserver/discussions), and
anything security-sensitive privately via [SECURITY.md](.github/SECURITY.md).

Something being on this list does not mean it is being worked on now, and
something not being on it does not mean it will be refused — it usually means
nobody has asked.
